237. ‘Ekaṃsena bhagavā nīvaraṇā’ means that the Blessed One decisively declared these phenomena to be hindrances, as they are hindrances to the arising of wholesome states in the mind. ‘Kāyakilamatho’ means bodily exhaustion. This is called ‘distress’ because it is a condition for the arising of trouble. Mental fatigue should be understood as arising from that cause. Therefore it is said: ‘For him, indeed,’ and so forth. The connection for ‘mental distress also’ is made by supplying ‘was calmed.’ ‘Maggeneva’ means by the noble path as it has been realized. ‘Assa’ means of Prince Abhaya. This refers to the twofold distress of body and mind.

Abhayasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Abhaya Sutta is concluded.

Sākacchavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sākaccha Chapter is concluded.

7. Ānāpānavaggo

7. The Chapter on Mindfulness of Breathing

1. Aṭṭhikamahapphalasuttādivaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Aṭṭhikamahapphalasutta and so forth.

238. Uppannasaññāti saññāsīsena upacārajjhānaṃ vadati. Tenāha “taṃ paneta”ntiādi. Chavicammampi upaṭṭhātīti idaṃ saviññāṇakaṃ aviññāṇakampi kāyasāmaññato gahetvā vuttaṃ. Sati vā upādiseseti ettha upādiyati attano ārammaṇaṃ gaṇhātīti upādi, upādānaṃ, etassa ekadese appahīne satīti attho.

238. ‘Uppannasaññā’ refers to access concentration under the heading of perception. Therefore it is said: ‘This, indeed,’ and so forth. The phrase ‘even skin and hide are present’ is stated by taking both the body with consciousness and that without consciousness as the body in general. As for ‘sati vā upādiseseti’ (or when there is a remainder of clinging), here ‘upādi’ means that which appropriates, that which takes its own object; that is, clinging. The meaning is: when a portion of this is not abandoned.

Ānāpānavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on Mindfulness of Breathing is concluded.

8. Nirodhavaggo

8. The Chapter on Cessation

1-10. Asubhasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-10. Commentary on the Asubhasutta and so forth.

248-257. Anabhiratinti anabhiramaṇaṃ anapekkhitaṃ. Accantanirodhabhūte nibbāne pavattasaññā nirodhasaññā. Tattha sā maggasahagatā lokuttarā[M 2.0449], yā pana nibbāne ninnabhāvena pavattā, upasamānussatisahagatā ca, sā lokiyāti āha – “nirodhasaññā missakā”ti. “Tesaṃ navasū”tiādi pamādapāṭho. “Ekādasasu appanā hoti, nava upacārajjhānikā”ti pāṭho gahetabbo. Vīsati kammaṭṭhānānīti idampi idhāgatanayo, na visuddhimaggādīsu āgatanayo[V 2.0155]. Ettha ca ārammaṇādīsu yathāyogaṃ appanaṃ upacāraṃ vā pāpuṇitvā arahattappattassa pubbabhāgabhūtā vipassanāmaggabojjhaṅgā kathitā.

248-257. ‘Anabhirati’ means non-delight, non-relishing. The perception operating in Nibbāna, which is the state of absolute cessation, is the perception of cessation. There, that which is associated with the path is supramundane; but that which operates inclining towards Nibbāna and is accompanied by mindfulness of peace, is mundane—thus it is said: ‘The perception of cessation is mixed.’ ‘Of these, nine…’ and so forth is a negligent reading. The reading to be adopted is: ‘In eleven, there is absorption; nine are of access jhāna.’ ‘Twenty meditation subjects’—this too is according to the method presented here, not according to the method presented in the Visuddhimagga and other texts. And here, having attained absorption or access as appropriate, depending on the object and so forth, the enlightenment factors of insight and the path, which are the preliminary part for the attainment of arahantship, are discussed.

Nirodhavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on Cessation is concluded.

Bojjhaṅgasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on the Factors of Enlightenment is concluded.

3. Satipaṭṭhānasaṃyuttaṃ

3. The Connected Discourses on the Foundations of Mindfulness

1. Ambapālivaggo

1. The Chapter on Ambapālī

1. Ambapālisuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Ambapālī Sutta

367. Ekāyanvāyanti [M 2.0450][V 2.0156] sandhivasena vuttaṃ o-kārassa va-kāraṃ a-kārassa dīghaṃ katvā. Ayaṃ kira saṃyuttābhilāpo, tattha ayana-saddo maggapariyāyo. Na kevalaṃ ayameva, atha kho aññepi maggapariyāyāti paduddhāraṃ karonto “maggassa hī”tiādiṃ vatvā yadi maggapariyāyo āyana-saddo, kasmā puna maggoti vuttanti codanaṃ sandhāyāha “tasmā”tiādi. Tattha ekamaggoti ekova maggo. Na hi nibbānagāmimaggo añño atthīti. Nanu satipaṭṭhānaṃ idha maggoti adhippetaṃ, tadaññepi bahū maggadhammā atthīti? Saccaṃ atthi, te pana satipaṭṭhānaggahaṇeneva gahitā tadavinābhāvato. Tathā hi ñāṇavīriyādayo niddese gahitā, uddese satiyā eva gahaṇaṃ veneyyajjhāsayavasenāti daṭṭhabbaṃ, satiyā maggabhāvadassanatthañca. Na dvedhāpathabhūtoti iminā imassa dvayabhāvābhāvaṃ viya anibbānagāmibhāvābhāvañca dasseti. Nibbānagamanaṭṭhenāti nibbānaṃ gacchati etenāti nibbānagamanaṃ, so eva aviparītabhāvanāya attho, tena nibbānagamanaṭṭhena, nibbānādhigamūpāyatāyāti attho. Magganīyaṭṭhenāti gavesitabbatāya.

367. The word ‘ekāyanvā’ is stated by way of sandhi, having changed the o-kāra to va-kāra and lengthened the a-kāra. This is indeed the expression of the Saṃyutta, where the word ‘ayana’ is a synonym for path. Not only this, but there are other synonyms for path—thus, when analyzing the word, saying ‘of the path,’ and so forth, if the word ‘ayana’ is a synonym for path, why then is ‘magga’ stated again? Addressing this objection, it is said: ‘Therefore,’ and so forth. Here, ‘ekamaggo’ means the one and only path. Indeed, there is no other path leading to Nibbāna. But is not the foundation of mindfulness intended here as the path? And are there not many other path factors as well? It is true they exist, but they are included by the mention of the foundation of mindfulness itself due to their inseparability. Thus, factors like wisdom and energy are included in the explanation, while in the summary, only mindfulness is mentioned for the sake of the inclinations of those to be taught—this should be understood—and also to show the nature of mindfulness as the path. ‘Na dvedhāpathabhūto’ (not being a twofold path) indicates its absence of duality, just as it indicates its not failing to lead to Nibbāna. ‘Nibbānagamanaṭṭhena’ means by the meaning of going to Nibbāna. ‘Nibbānaṃ gacchati etenā’ti ‘nibbānagamanaṃ’ means ‘one goes to Nibbāna by this.’ This is indeed the meaning of correct development; thus, by the meaning of going to Nibbāna, it means it is the means of attaining Nibbāna. ‘Magganīyaṭṭhena’ means because it is to be sought after.

Rāgādīhīti “rāgo malaṃ, doso malaṃ, moho mala”nti (vibha. 924) evaṃ vuttehi rāgādīhi malehi. Sā panāyaṃ saṃkiliṭṭhacittānaṃ visuddhi sijjhamānā yasmā sokādīnaṃ anuppādāya saṃvattati, tasmā vuttaṃ “sokaparidevānaṃ samatikkamāyā”tiādi. Tattha socanaṃ ñātibyasanādinimittaṃ cetaso santāpo antonijjhānaṃ soko. Ñātibyasanādinimittameva sokādhikatājanito “kahaṃ ekaputtakā”tiādinā paridevanavasena vācāvippalāpo paridevanaṃ paridevo. Tassa āyatiṃ anuppajjanaṃ idha samatikkamoti āha “pahānāyā”ti. Dukkhadomanassānanti ettha cetasikadukkhatāya domanassassapi dukkhasaddeneva gahaṇe [M 2.0451] siddhe saddena anivattanato [V 2.0157] sāmaññajotanāya visesavacanaṃ seṭṭhanti “domanassāna”nteva vuttaṃ. Cetasikadomanassassāti bhūtakathanaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Ñāyati etena yāthāvato paṭivijjhīyati catusaccanti ñāyo vuccati ariyamaggo. Nanu ayampi maggo, kiṃ maggo eva maggassa adhigamāya hotīti codanaṃ sandhāyāha – “ayaṃ hī”tiādi. Taṇhāva kammakilesavipākānaṃ vinanaṭṭhena saṃsibbanaṭṭhena vānaṃ. Tena taṇhāvānena virahitattā tassa abhāvāti attho. Attapaccakkhāyāti attapaccakkhatthāya.

‘Rāgādīhi’ means by these stains such as passion, as stated in ‘Passion is a stain, hatred is a stain, delusion is a stain’ (Vibh. 924). Since this purification, being accomplished for those with defiled minds, conduces to the non-arising of sorrow and so forth, it is therefore said: ‘For the overcoming of sorrow and lamentation,’ and so forth. Here, ‘soka’ (sorrow) is the inner burning, the mental distress, the inner brooding, caused by the misfortune of relatives and so forth. ‘Paridevana’ or ‘paridevo’ (lamentation) is the verbal outburst of wailing, such as ‘Where is my only child?’, generated by excessive grief due to the misfortune of relatives and the like. The non-arising of that in the future is here called ‘overcoming’; hence it is stated: ‘for the abandoning of.’ As for ‘of suffering and grief,’ although grief, being mental suffering, is already included by the word ‘suffering,’ because the word is not omitted and a specific term is better for illuminating the general meaning, it is stated as ‘of grief.’ ‘Of mental grief’ should be seen as a statement of fact. That by which the Four Noble Truths are known and rightly penetrated in their true nature is called ‘ñāya’ (the way), that is, the Noble Path. To address the objection, ‘But this too is a path; is the path for the attainment of the path itself?’, it is said: ‘For this is,’ and so forth. Craving is called ‘vāna’ (sewing) because it weaves and stitches together actions, defilements, and their results. The meaning is the absence of that, because of being devoid of that ‘sewing’ which is craving. ‘Attapaccakkhāya’ means for the purpose of direct realization.

Vaṇṇabhāsananti pasaṃsāvacanaṃ. Visuddhinti visujjhanaṃ kilesappahānaṃ. Uggahetabbanti ettha vācuggatakaraṇaṃ uggaho. Paricayakaraṇaṃ paripucchāmūlakattā taggahaṇeneva gahitanti daṭṭhabbaṃ.

‘Vaṇṇabhāsana’ means words of praise. ‘Visuddhi’ means cleansing, the abandoning of defilements. As for ‘uggahetabbaṃ’ (to be taken up), here ‘uggaho’ is the act of learning by heart. The act of familiarizing oneself should be understood as included by that very taking up, because it is rooted in questioning.

Na tato heṭṭhāti idha adhippetakāyādīnaṃ vedanādisabhāvattābhāvā kāyavedanācittavimuttassa tebhūmakadhammassa visuṃ vipallāsavatthantarabhāvena gahitattā ca heṭṭhā gahaṇesu vipallāsavatthūnaṃ aniṭṭhānaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Pañcamassa pana vipallāsavatthuno abhāvena “na uddha”nti āha. Ārammaṇavibhāgena hettha satipaṭṭhānavibhāgoti. Tayo satipaṭṭhānāti satipaṭṭhānasaddassa atthuddhāradassanaṃ, na idha pāḷiyaṃ vuttassa satipaṭṭhānasaddassa atthadassanaṃ. Ādīsu hīti ettha ādi-saddena “phassasamudayā vedanānaṃ samudayo, nāmarūpasamudayā cittassa samudayo, manasikārasamudayā dhammānaṃ samudayo”ti (saṃ. ni. 5.408) satipaṭṭhānāti vuttānaṃ satigocarānaṃ pakāsake suttapadese saṅgaṇhāti. Evaṃ paṭisambhidāmaggapāḷiyampi avasesapāḷipadesadassanattho ādi-saddo daṭṭhabbo. Satiyā paṭṭhānanti satiyā patiṭṭhātabbaṭṭhānaṃ.

‘Not below that’ is said with reference to the undesirability of the objects of distortion in the lower graspings, for two reasons: because the body and so forth intended here do not have the nature of feelings and so forth, and because the phenomena of the three planes, which are distinct from body, feeling, and mind, are taken separately as another object of distortion. But because of the absence of a fifth object of distortion, it is said, ‘not above’. Here, the division of the foundations of mindfulness is a division by way of the objects. ‘The three foundations of mindfulness’ is a demonstration of the extraction of the meaning of the word ‘foundations of mindfulness,’ not a demonstration of the meaning of the word ‘foundations of mindfulness’ as used here in the Pāli text. In the phrase ‘ādīsu hi,’ the word ‘ādi’ includes the sutta passages that reveal the objects of mindfulness, which are called ‘foundations of mindfulness,’ such as: ‘With the arising of contact, there is an arising of feelings; with the arising of name-and-form, there is an arising of mind; with the arising of attention, there is an arising of phenomena’ (Saṃyutta Nikāya 5.408). Similarly, in the Pāli text of the Paṭisambhidāmagga as well, the word ‘ādi’ should be understood as for the purpose of showing the remaining Pāli passages. ‘Satiyā paṭṭhāna’ means the place where mindfulness is to be established.

Ariyoti ārakattādinā ariyaṃ sammāsambuddhamāha. Etthāti etasmiṃ saḷāyatanavibhaṅgasutte (ma. ni. 3.311). Tattha hi –

‘Ariyo’ (Noble) refers to the Noble Perfectly Self-Enlightened One by way of being far from defilements and so forth. ‘Ettha’ (here) refers to this Saḷāyatanavibhaṅga Sutta (MN 3.311). For therein –

“Tayo satipaṭṭhānā yadariyo…pe… marahatīti iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ, kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ. Idha, bhikkhave, satthā sāvakānaṃ dhammaṃ deseti anukampako hitesī anukampaṃ upādāya – ‘idaṃ vo [M 2.0452] hitāya idaṃ vo sukhāyā’ti. Tassa sāvakā na sussūsanti, na sotaṃ odahanti, na aññā cittaṃ upaṭṭhapenti, vokkamma ca satthusāsanā vattanti. Tatra, bhikkhave, tathāgato na ceva anattamano hoti, na ca anattamanataṃ paṭisaṃvedeti, anavassuto ca viharati sato sampajāno. Idaṃ, bhikkhave, paṭhamaṃ satipaṭṭhānaṃ. Yadariyo sevati…pe... marahati. Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, satthā [V 2.0158] …pe… idaṃ vo sukhāyāti. Tassa ekacce sāvakā na sussūsanti…pe… ekacce sāvakā sussūsanti…pe… na ca vokkamma satthusāsanā vattanti. Tatra, bhikkhave, tathāgato na ceva anattamano hoti, na ca anattamanataṃ paṭisaṃvedeti, na ceva attamano hoti, na ca attamanataṃ paṭisaṃvedeti. Anattamanatañca attamanatañca tadubhayaṃ abhinivajjetvā upekkhako viharati sato sampajāno. Idaṃ, bhikkhave, dutiyaṃ satipaṭṭhānaṃ…pe… marahati. Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave,…pe… sukhāyāti, tassa sāvakā sussūsanti…pe… vattanti. Tatra, bhikkhave, tathāgato attamano ceva hoti, attamanatañca paṭisaṃvedeti, anavassuto ca viharati sato sampajāno. Idaṃ, bhikkhave, tatiyaṃ satipaṭṭhāna”nti –

“There are three foundations of mindfulness which the noble one cultivates… worthy of the noble one. And why was this said? Here, monks, the Teacher teaches the Dhamma to his disciples out of compassion, seeking their welfare, with compassion: ‘This is for your welfare, this is for your happiness.’ But his disciples do not listen, do not lend an ear, do not apply their minds to understanding, and they turn away from the Teacher’s instruction. In this case, monks, the Tathāgata is neither displeased nor does he experience displeasure; he dwells unattached, mindful and fully aware. This, monks, is the first foundation of mindfulness… worthy of the noble one. Again, monks, the Teacher teaches the Dhamma to his disciples out of compassion… ‘This is for your welfare, this is for your happiness.’ Some of his disciples do not listen… while some do listen… and do not turn away from the Teacher’s instruction. In this case, monks, the Tathāgata is neither displeased nor does he experience displeasure, nor is he pleased nor does he experience pleasure. Having abandoned both displeasure and pleasure, he dwells equanimous, mindful and fully aware. This, monks, is the second foundation of mindfulness… worthy of the noble one. Again, monks,… ‘This is for your happiness.’ His disciples listen… and do not turn away from the Teacher’s instruction. In this case, monks, the Tathāgata is pleased and experiences pleasure, yet he dwells unattached, mindful and fully aware. This, monks, is the third foundation of mindfulness.”

Evaṃ paṭighānunayehi anavassutatā niccaṃ upaṭṭhitassatitāya tadubhayavītivattatā “satipaṭṭhāna”nti vuttā. Buddhānaṃyeva hi niccaṃ upaṭṭhitassatitā hoti āveṇikadhammabhāvato, na paccekabuddhādīnaṃ. Pa-saddo ārambhaṃ joteti, ārambho ca pavattīti katvā āha “pavattayitabbatoti attho”ti. Satiyā karaṇabhūtāya paṭṭhānaṃ paṭṭhapetabbaṃ satipaṭṭhānaṃ. Ana-saddo hi bahulavacanena kammatthopi hotīti.

Thus, the transcending of both aversion and attraction, by means of being untainted by them and by the constant presence of mindfulness, is called 'satipaṭṭhāna'. Indeed, only the Buddhas possess the constant presence of mindfulness, as it is their unique quality, not the Paccekabuddhas and others. The prefix 'pa-' signifies a beginning, and taking 'beginning' as 'proceeding', it is said, 'the meaning is “that which is to be set in motion”'. Satipaṭṭhāna is the foundation to be established by means of mindfulness, which is the instrument. For the suffix '-ana', by its various applications, can also have the sense of the object.

Tathāssa kattuatthopi labbhatīti “patiṭṭhātīti paṭṭhāna”nti vuttaṃ. Upaṭṭhātīti ettha upa-saddo bhusatthavisiṭṭhaṃ pakkhandanaṃ dīpetīti “okkanditvā pakkhanditvā pavattatīti attho”ti vuttaṃ. Puna bhāvatthaṃ satisaddaṃ paṭṭhānasaddañca vaṇṇento “atha vā”tiādimāha. Tena purimavikappe sati-saddo paṭṭhāna-saddo ca kattuatthoti viññāyati. Saraṇaṭṭhenāti cirakatassa cirabhāsitassa ca anussaraṇaṭṭhena. Idanti yaṃ “satiyeva satipaṭṭhāna”nti vuttaṃ, idaṃ idha imasmiṃ suttapadese adhippetaṃ.

Thus, its meaning as agent is also obtained; hence, it is said, 'It establishes, thus it is a paṭṭhāna.' Here, in 'upaṭṭhāti,' the prefix 'upa-' signifies a vigorous and distinct leaping forward; hence, it is said, 'the meaning is: having leaped upon, having rushed forward, it proceeds.' Again, while explaining the words 'sati' and 'paṭṭhāna' in the sense of 'state' (bhāva), he said, 'or alternatively,' and so on. By this, it is understood that in the preceding alternative, the words 'sati' and 'paṭṭhāna' are in the sense of the agent. 'By the meaning of remembering' means by the meaning of recollecting what was done long ago and spoken long ago. 'This'—what was said, 'mindfulness itself is the foundation of mindfulness'—is what is intended here in this passage of the discourse.

Yadi [M 2.0453] evanti yadi sati eva satipaṭṭhānaṃ, sati nāma eko dhammo, evaṃ sante kasmā satipaṭṭhānāti bahuvacananti āha “satīnaṃ bahuttā”tiādi. Yadi bahukā tā satiyo, atha kasmā maggoti ekavacananti yojanā. Magganaṭṭhenāti niyyānaṭṭhena. Niyyāniko hi maggadhammo, teneva niyyānikabhāvena ekattupagato ekantato nibbānaṃ gacchati, atthikehi ca tadatthaṃ maggīyatīti āha “vuttañheta”ntiādi. Tattha catassopi cetāti kāyānupassanādivasena catubbidhāpi ca etā satiyo. Aparabhāgeti ariyamaggakkhaṇe. Kiccaṃ sādhayamānāti pubbabhāge kāyādīsu ārammaṇesu subhasaññādividhamanavasena visuṃ visuṃ pavattitvā maggakkhaṇe sakiṃyeva [V 2.0159] tattha catubbidhassapi vipallāsassa samucchedavasena pahānakiccaṃ sādhayamānā ārammaṇakaraṇavasena nibbānaṃ gacchanti, tamevassa catukiccasādhanataṃ upādāya bahuvacananiddeso, tathāpi atthato bhedābhāvato maggoti ekavacanena vuccati. Tenāha – “tasmā catassopi eko maggoti vuttā”ti.

If it is so, if mindfulness itself is the foundation of mindfulness, and mindfulness is a single phenomenon, then why is it expressed in the plural as 'satipaṭṭhānā'? It is said, 'because of the multiplicity of mindfulnesses,' and so on. If there are many instances of mindfulness, then why is it referred to in the singular as 'path'? This is the connection. It is because of its nature as a path—as a means of deliverance. For the path-phenomenon is indeed liberating, and by that very liberating nature, it converges into unity, certainly leading to Nibbāna. Moreover, seekers strive for that goal; hence it is said, 'as it has been stated,' and so on. Here, 'these four' means the four kinds of mindfulness, such as contemplation of the body. 'In the subsequent phase' means at the moment of the noble path. 'Accomplishing the task' means that having previously arisen separately in the earlier phase in regard to objects such as the body, by way of dispelling perceptions of beauty and so forth, they accomplish the task of abandoning the fourfold distortions by uprooting them all at once at the moment of the path. By making Nibbāna their object, they reach Nibbāna. It is with reference to this accomplishment of the fourfold task that the plural designation is given. Nevertheless, because there is no essential difference, it is referred to in the singular as 'path.' Therefore, it is said: 'Thus, the four are called a single path.'

Kathetukamyatāpucchā itarāsaṃ pucchānaṃ idha asambhavato niddesādivasena desetukāmatāya ca tathā vuttattā. “Ayañceva kāyo bahiddhā ca nāmarūpa”ntiādīsu (ma. ni. 1.271, 287, 297; pārā. 11) khandhapañcakaṃ, “sukhañca kāyena paṭisaṃvedetī”tiādīsu vedanādayo tayo arūpakkhandhā, “yā tasmiṃ samaye kāyassa passaddhi paṭippassaddhī”tiādīsu (dha. sa. 40) vedanādayo tayo cetasikā khandhā “kāyo”ti vuccanti, tato visesanatthaṃ “kāyeti rūpakāye”ti āha. Kāyānupassīti ettha tassīlatthaṃ dassento “kāyaṃ anupassanasīlo”ti āha. Aniccato anupassatīti catusamuṭṭhānikakāyaṃ “anicca”nti anupassati, evaṃ passanto eva cassa aniccākārampi anupassatīti vuccati, tathābhūtassa cassa niccagāhassa visesopi na hotīti vuttaṃ “no niccato”ti. Tathā hesa “niccasaññaṃ pajahatī”ti (paṭi. ma. 1.28) vutto. Ettha ca aniccato eva anupassatīti evakāro luttaniddiṭṭhoti tena nivattitamatthaṃ dassetuṃ “no niccato”ti vuttaṃ. Na cettha dukkhānupassanādinivattanamāsaṅkitabbaṃ paṭiyoginivattanaparattā eva-kārassa, upari desanāāruḷhattā ca tāsaṃ. Dukkhato anupassatītiādīsupi eseva nayo. Ayaṃ pana viseso – aniccassa dukkhattā [M 2.0454] tameva kāyaṃ dukkhato anupassati, dukkhassa anattattā anattato anupassatīti.

The question of desire to speak is so stated because other questions are impossible here, and due to the desire to teach by way of explanation and so forth. In 'This very body and, externally, name-and-form,' etc. (Majjhima Nikāya 1.271, 287, 297; Pārā. 11), the five aggregates are meant. In 'He experiences happiness with the body,' etc., the three formless aggregates—feeling and the others—are meant. In 'At that time, the tranquility and serenity of the body,' etc. (Dhammasaṅgaṇī 40), the three mental aggregates—feeling and the others—are called 'body.' To specify this, it is said, 'by "body" is meant the physical body.' Regarding 'one who contemplates the body,' explaining the meaning of 'one whose nature it is,' it says, 'one whose nature it is to contemplate the body.' 'He contemplates it as impermanent' means he contemplates the body arising from the four causes as 'impermanent.' One who sees thus is also said to contemplate the aspect of impermanence. For such a one, there is no particular grasping at permanence, as stated, 'not as permanent.' For so it is said of him, 'he abandons the perception of permanence' (Paṭisambhidāmagga 1.28). Here, in 'he contemplates it as impermanent,' the word 'eva' (only) is indicated as elided; to show what is negated by it, the words 'not as permanent' are stated. And here, the negation of the contemplation of suffering and so on should not be suspected, because the word 'eva' is concerned with negating its opposite, and because these contemplations are taken up in the teaching later on. This same method applies also to 'he contemplates it as suffering,' etc. This, however, is the distinction: because what is impermanent is suffering, he contemplates that very body as suffering; because what is suffering is not-self, he contemplates it as not-self.

Yasmā pana yaṃ aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattā, na taṃ abhinanditabbaṃ, yañca na abhinanditabbaṃ, na tattha rajjitabbaṃ, tasmā vuttaṃ “aniccato anupassati, no niccato, dukkhato anupassati, no sukhato, anattato anupassati, no attato, nibbindati, no nandati, virajjati, no rajjatī”ti. So evaṃ arajjanto rāgaṃ nirodheti, no samudeti, samudayaṃ na karotīti attho. Evaṃ paṭipanno ca paṭinissajjati, no ādiyati. Ayañhi aniccādianupassanā tadaṅgavasena saddhiṃ kāyatannissayakhandhābhisaṅkhārehi kilesānaṃ pariccajanato saṅkhatadosadassanena tabbiparīte nibbāne tanninnatāya pakkhandanato “pariccāgapaṭinissaggo ceva pakkhandanapaṭinissaggo cā”ti vuccati. Tasmā tāya samannāgato bhikkhu vuttanayena kilese ca pariccajati, nibbāne ca pakkhandati, tathābhūto ca pariccajanavasena kilese [V 2.0160] na ādiyati, nāpi adosadassitāvasena saṅkhatārammaṇaṃ. Tena vuttaṃ “paṭinissajjati, no ādiyatī”ti. Idāni nissitāhi anupassanāhi yesaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānaṃ hoti, taṃ dassetuṃ “aniccato anupassanto niccasaññaṃ pajahatī”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha niccasaññanti saṅkhārā niccāti evaṃ pavattaṃ viparītasaññaṃ. Diṭṭhicittavipallāsapahānamukheneva saññāvipallāsappahānanti saññāgahaṇaṃ, saññāsīsena vā tesampi gahaṇaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Nandinti sappītikataṇhaṃ. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva.

Since whatever is impermanent, suffering, and not-self should not be delighted in, and what should not be delighted in should not be lusted after, therefore it is said: 'One contemplates as impermanent, not as permanent; as suffering, not as pleasurable; as not-self, not as self. One becomes disenchanted, not delighted; one becomes dispassionate, not lustful.' Thus, by not being lustful, he brings lust to cessation; it does not arise; he does not cause its arising—this is the meaning. Practicing in this way, one relinquishes and does not appropriate. For this contemplation of impermanence and so on is called 'relinquishment by abandoning' and 'relinquishment by entering' because, by way of substitution, it is an abandoning of defilements together with the formations of the aggregates dependent on the body, and because, by seeing the fault in the conditioned, it is an entering into Nibbāna, which is the opposite, through inclination towards it. Therefore, a bhikkhu endowed with this practice abandons defilements in the manner described and enters into Nibbāna. Being thus, by way of abandoning, he does not appropriate defilements, nor does he appropriate a conditioned object by failing to see its drawbacks. Hence it is said: 'He relinquishes and does not appropriate.' Now, to show the abandonment of those states that are abandoned by means of these contemplations, it is said: 'Contemplating as impermanent, one abandons the perception of permanence,' and so on. Herein, 'perception of permanence' refers to the distorted perception that arises as 'formations are permanent.' The abandoning of the distortion of perception is shown by way of abandoning the distortions of view and mind; or it should be understood that these too are included under the heading of perception. 'Delight' refers to craving accompanied by joy. The rest is as already explained.

Viharatīti iminā kāyānupassanāsamaṅgino iriyāpathavihāro vuttoti āha – “iriyatī”ti, iriyāpathaṃ pavattetīti attho. Ārammaṇakaraṇavasena abhibyāpanato “tīsu bhavesū”ti vuttaṃ, uppajjanavasena pana kilesā parittabhūmakā evāti. Yadipi kilesānaṃ pahānaṃ ātāpananti taṃ sammādiṭṭhiādīnampi attheva, ātappa-saddoviya pana ātāpa-saddopi vīriye eva niruḷhoti vuttaṃ “vīriyassetaṃ nāma”nti. Atha vā paṭipakkhappahāne sampayuttadhammānaṃ abbhussahanavasena pavattamānassa vīriyassa sātisayaṃ tadātāpananti vīriyameva tathā vuccati, na aññe dhammā.

'He dwells': by this, the dwelling in the postures of one endowed with the contemplation of the body is spoken of. It is said, 'he comports himself,' meaning he carries on the postures. 'In the three realms' is said because of pervading them by way of making them an object; but by way of arising, the defilements are only of the limited sphere. Although the abandoning of defilements is ardor, that ardor exists for right view and so on as well. However, the word 'ātāpa' (ardor) is established in the meaning of energy, thus it is said, 'this is a name for energy.' Alternatively, because the energy that proceeds by way of the striving of its associated states in abandoning their opposites is pre-eminently that ardor, energy itself is so called, not other states.

Ātāpīti cāyamīkāro pasaṃsāya, atisayassa vā dīpakoti ātāpīgahaṇena sammappadhānasamaṅgitaṃ dasseti. Sammā samantato sāmañca pajānanto sampajāno, asammissato vavatthāne aññadhammānupassitābhāvena [M 2.0455] sammā aviparītaṃ, sabbākārapajānanena samantato, uparūpari visesāvahabhāvena pavattiyā sāmaṃ pajānantoti attho. Yadi paññāya anupassati, kathaṃ satipaṭṭhānatāti āha “na hī”tiādi. Tasmā satiyā laddhupakārāya eva paññāya ettha yathāvutte kāye kammaṭṭhāniko bhikkhu anupassako, tasmā “kāyānupassī”ti vuccati. Antosaṅkhepo antolīnatā, kosajjanti attho. Upāyapariggahoti ettha sīlavisodhanādi gaṇanādi uggahakosallādi ca upāyo, tabbipariyāyato anupāyo veditabbo. Yasmā ca upaṭṭhitassatī yathāvuttaṃ upāyaṃ na pariccajati, anupāyañca na upādiyati, tasmā vuttaṃ “muṭṭhassati…pe… asamattho hotī”ti. Tenāti upāyānupāyānaṃ pariggahaparivajjanesu apariccāgāpariggahesu ca asamatthabhāvena. Assa yogino.

The 'ī' in 'ātāpī' is for praise, or it indicates excellence; thus, by the word 'ātāpī,' endowment with right effort is shown. 'Sampajāno' (clearly comprehending) means knowing rightly, all-round, and by oneself. The meaning is: knowing 'rightly' as without distortion, by distinguishing without mixing, through not contemplating other things; knowing 'all-round' by knowing in all aspects; knowing 'by oneself' by proceeding in a way that brings about higher and higher distinctions. If one contemplates with wisdom, how is it a foundation of mindfulness? It is said, 'It is not...' and so on. Therefore, it is with wisdom that has received the assistance of mindfulness that a meditating monk contemplates the body as described here. Therefore, he is called 'one who contemplates the body.' 'Internal contraction' means internal sluggishness, that is, laziness. Regarding 'grasping the means,' here the means are purification of virtue, counting, skill in learning, and so on; the non-means should be understood as the opposite of these. And because one with established mindfulness does not abandon the means as described, and does not adopt the non-means, therefore it is said, 'one with forgetful mindfulness... is incapable.' The word 'tena' (thereby) refers to the inability to grasp the means and avoid the non-means, and the inability not to abandon the means and not to grasp the non-means. 'Assa yogino' means 'of that yogi.'

Yasmā satiyevettha satipaṭṭhānaṃ vuttā, tasmāssa sampayuttadhammā vīriyādayo aṅganti āha – “sampayogaṅgañcassa dassetvā”ti. Aṅga-saddo cettha kāraṇapariyāyo daṭṭhabbo. Satiggahaṇenevettha sammāsamādhissapi gahaṇaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ tassā samādhikkhandhe saṅgahitattā. Yasmā vā satisīsenāyaṃ [V 2.0161] desanā. Na hi kevalāya satiyā kilesappahānaṃ sambhavati, nibbānādhigamo vā, nāpi kevalā sati pavattati, tasmāssa jhānadesanāyaṃ savitakkādivacanassa viya sampayogaṅgadassanatāti aṅga-saddassa avayavapariyāyatā daṭṭhabbā. Pahānaṅganti “vivicceva kāmehī”tiādīsu viya pahātabbaṅgaṃ dassetuṃ. Yasmā ettha pubbabhāgamaggo adhippeto, na lokuttaramaggo, tasmā pubbabhāgiyameva vinayaṃ dassento “tadaṅgavinayena vā vikkhambhanavinayena vā”ti āha. Assāti yogino. Tesaṃ dhammānanti vedanādidhammānaṃ. Tesañhi tattha anadhippetattā “atthuddhāranayenetaṃ vutta”nti āha. Yaṃ panāti vibhaṅge, vibhaṅgapakaraṇeti adhippāyo. Etthāti “loke”ti etasmiṃ pade, tā ca lokiyā eva anupassanā nāma sammasananti katvā.

Because mindfulness itself is here called the foundation of mindfulness, its associated states—such as energy—are its limbs. This is stated as: 'having shown its associated limb.' Here, the word 'limb' should be understood as a synonym for cause. By the mention of mindfulness, the inclusion of right concentration should also be understood, since mindfulness is included in the concentration aggregate. Or, this teaching is headed by mindfulness. For neither the abandoning of defilements nor the attainment of Nibbāna is possible through mindfulness alone, nor does mindfulness proceed alone. Therefore, just as in the teaching on jhāna there are words like 'accompanied by initial application of thought,' so here the associated limbs are shown; thus, the word 'limb' should be understood as a synonym for component. 'The limb of abandoning' is to show the limb to be abandoned, as in 'secluded from sensual pleasures,' etc. Since the preliminary path is intended here, not the supramundane path, it shows only the preliminary restraint by stating, 'either by restraint by substitution, or by restraint by suppression.' 'Assa' means 'of the yogi.' 'Tesaṃ dhammānaṃ' means 'of those states,' such as feeling, etc. Since they are not intended there, it is said, 'this is stated by way of extracting the meaning.' 'Yaṃ pana' means 'but as for,' that is, in the Vibhaṅga, the treatise Vibhaṅga is intended. 'Ettha' means 'here,' that is, in the word 'loke' (in the world); and because those contemplations are indeed worldly, they are called 'comprehension.'

Dukkhatoti vipariṇāmasaṅkhāradukkhatāhi dukkhasabhāvato, dukkhāti anupassitabbāti attho. Sesapadadvayepi eseva nayo. Yo sukhaṃ [M 2.0456] dukkhato addāti yo bhikkhu sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vipariṇāmadukkhatāya dukkhanti paññācakkhunā addakkhi. Dukkhamaddakkhi sallatoti dukkhavedanaṃ pīḷājananato antotudanato dunnīharaṇato ca sallanti addakkhi passi. Adukkhamasukhanti upekkhāvedanaṃ. Santanti sukhadukkhānaṃ viya anoḷārikatāya paccayavasena vūpasantasabhāvattā ca santaṃ. Aniccatoti hutvā abhāvato udayabbayavantato tāvakālikato niccapaṭikkhepato ca aniccanti yo addakkhi. Sa ve sammaddaso bhikkhūti so bhikkhu ekaṃsena, paribyattaṃ vā vedanāya sammā passanakoti attho.

'As suffering' (dukkhato) means that, due to the suffering of change and the suffering of formations, it is suffering by nature; thus, it should be contemplated as suffering. This same method applies to the remaining two terms. ‘He who sees pleasure as suffering’ means the monk who, with the eye of wisdom, saw pleasant feeling as suffering on account of the suffering of change. ‘He saw suffering as a dart’ means he saw, he perceived painful feeling as a dart because it generates affliction, pierces within, and is difficult to remove. ‘Neither-painful-nor-pleasant’ refers to equanimous feeling. ‘Peaceful’ (santaṃ) means peaceful because, unlike pleasure and pain, it is not gross, and because its nature is calmed by way of conditions. ‘As impermanent’ (aniccatoti) means he saw it as impermanent because it ceases to be after having been, is subject to arising and passing away, is temporary, and is the opposite of permanence. ‘Truly, that monk sees rightly’ means that monk is one who sees feeling correctly, either definitively or clearly.

Dukkhātipīti saṅkhāradukkhatāya dukkhā itipi. Sabbaṃ taṃ vedayitaṃ dukkhasmiṃ antogadhaṃ pariyāpannanti vadāmi saṅkhāradukkhanti vattabbato. Sukhadukkhatopi cāti sukhādīnaṃ ṭhitivipariṇāmañāṇasukhatāya ca vipariṇāmaṭṭhitiaññāṇadukkhatāya ca vuttattā tissopi sukhato tissopi ca dukkhato anupassitabbāti attho. Satta anupassanā heṭṭhā pakāsitā eva.

‘It is suffering’ (dukkhātipi) means it is suffering also on account of the suffering of formations. ‘I say that all that is felt is comprised and included within suffering,’ because it should be referred to as the suffering of formations. ‘And also from pleasure and pain’ (sukhadukkhatopi ca) means: because it is stated that there is pleasure in knowing the presence and change of pleasure, etc., and suffering in not knowing their presence and change, therefore, all three feelings should be contemplated as pleasure and all three as suffering. The seven contemplations have already been explained below.

Ārammaṇā…pe… bhedānanti rūpādiārammaṇanānattassa nīlāditabbhedassa, chandādiadhipatinānattassa hīnāditabbhedassa, ñāṇajhānādisahajātanānattassa sasaṅkhārikāsaṅkhārika-savitakka-savicārāditabbhedassa, kāmāvacarādibhūminānattassa, ukkaṭṭhamajjhimāditabbhedassa, kusalādikammanānattassa, devagatisaṃvattaniyatāditabbhedassa, kaṇhasukkavipākanānattassa, diṭṭhadhammavedanīyatāditabbhedassa, parittabhūmakādikiriyānānattassa, tihetukāditabbhedassa vasena anupassitabbanti yojanā. Ādi-saddena savatthukāvatthukādinānattassa puggalattayasādhāraṇāditabbhedassa [V 2.0162] ca saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Sarāgādīnanti mahāsatipaṭṭhānasutte (dī. ni. 2.381; ma. ni. 1.114) āgatānaṃ sarāgavītarāgādibhedānaṃ. Salakkhaṇa-sāmaññalakkhaṇānanti phusanāditaṃtaṃsalakkhaṇānañceva aniccatādisāmaññalakkhaṇānañca vasenāti yojanā.

‘Of the object… and so on… of the distinctions’ means: this is the connection for how they are to be contemplated by way of the diversity of objects such as form, etc., and their distinctions such as blue, etc.; the diversity of predominances such as desire, etc., and their distinctions such as inferior, etc.; the diversity of what is co-arisen with knowledge, jhāna, etc., and their distinctions such as prompted, unprompted, with thought, with examination, etc.; the diversity of planes such as the sense-sphere, etc., and their distinctions such as superior, middling, etc.; the diversity of actions such as wholesome, etc., and their distinctions such as what leads to the deva-realm, etc.; the diversity of results such as dark and bright, and their distinctions such as what is to be experienced in this very life, etc.; and the diversity of functional consciousness of the limited plane, etc., and their distinctions such as triple-rooted, etc. By the word ‘etc.’ should be understood the inclusion of the diversity of things with and without a basis, etc., and the distinctions common to the three types of individuals, etc. ‘With lust, etc.’ refers to the distinctions of with lust, without lust, etc., as found in the Mahāsatipaṭṭhāna Sutta (Dī. Ni. 2.381; Ma. Ni. 1.114). ‘By specific characteristics and general characteristics’ means by way of the specific characteristics such as contact, etc., and the general characteristics such as impermanence, etc.—this is the connection.

Suññatadhammassāti anattatāsaṅkhātasuññatasabhāvassa. “Salakkhaṇa-sāmaññalakkhaṇāna”nti hi iminā yo ito bāhirakehi sāminivāsīkārakavedakaadhiṭṭhāyakabhāvena parikappito attā, tassa saṅkhāresu niccatā sukhatā viya katthacipi abhāvo vibhāvito. Natthi etesaṃ attāti anattā, yasmā pana saṅkhāresu ekadhammopi attā [M 2.0457] na hoti, tasmā te na attātipi anattāti ayaṃ tesaṃ suññatadhammo. Tassa suññatadhammassa, yaṃ vibhāvetuṃ abhidhamme (dha. sa. 121) “tasmiṃ kho pana samaye dhammā hontī”tiādinā suññatavāradesanā vuttā. Sesaṃ suviññeyyameva.

‘Of the nature of emptiness’ (suññatadhammassa) means of the nature of emptiness which is designated as not-self. For by this, ‘by specific characteristics and general characteristics,’ the absence in formations of the self—which is imagined by outsiders as an owner, dweller, maker, experiencer, or controller—is made clear, just as permanence and happiness are nowhere to be found. ‘There is no self of these, thus not-self.’ And since not even a single phenomenon among the formations is a self, therefore ‘they are not a self, thus not-self’—this is their nature of emptiness. To explain that nature of emptiness, the discourse on the section on emptiness is stated in the Abhidhamma (Dhammasaṅgaṇī 121), beginning with, “At that time, phenomena exist,” and so forth. The rest is easily understood.

Ambapālisuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Ambapālisutta is concluded.

2. Satisuttavaṇṇanā

2. The Commentary on the Satisutta

368. Saratīti sato. Ayaṃ pana na yāya kāyaci satiyā sato, atha kho edisāyāti dassento “kāyādianupassanāsatiyā”ti āha. Catusampajaññapaññāyāti catubbidhasampajaññapaññāya, abhikkamanaṃ abhikkantanti āha – “abhikkantaṃ vuccati gamana”nti. Tathā paṭikkamanaṃ paṭikkantanti vuttaṃ – “paṭikkantaṃ nivattana”nti. Nivattanañca nivattimattaṃ, nivattitvā pana gamanaṃ gamanameva. Kāyaṃ abhiharanto abhigamanavasena kāyaṃ nāmento. Ṭhānanisajjāsayanesu yo gamanādividhinā kāyassa purato abhihāro, so abhikkamo, pacchato apaharaṇaṃ paṭikkamoti dassento “ṭhānepī”tiādimāha. Āsanassāti pīṭhakādiāsanassa. Purimaaṅgābhimukhoti aṭanikādipurimāvayavābhimukho. Saṃsarantoti saṃsappanto. Paccāsaṃsarantoti paṭiāsappanto. Eseva nayoti iminā sarīrasseva abhimukhasaṃsappanapaṭiāsappanāni nidasseti.

368. ‘One who remembers is mindful’ (saratīti sato). However, this one is not mindful with just any mindfulness, but rather with such a kind; showing this, he said, ‘by the mindfulness of the contemplation of the body, etc.’ ‘By the wisdom of fourfold clear comprehension’ (catusampajaññapaññāya) means by the wisdom of the four kinds of clear comprehension. It is said that ‘going forward’ (abhikkamana) is ‘abhikkanta,’ as in ‘going is called abhikkanta.’ Similarly, ‘going backward’ (paṭikkamana) is called ‘paṭikkanta,’ as in ‘paṭikkanta is turning back.’ And ‘turning back’ (nivattana) is merely turning back; but after turning back, going is just going. ‘Bringing the body forward’ (kāyaṃ abhiharanto) means bending the body by way of approaching. To show that in standing, sitting, and lying down, the bringing forward of the body by way of going, etc., is ‘going forward’ (abhikkamo), and the taking back is ‘going backward’ (paṭikkamo), he said, ‘even in standing,’ etc. ‘Of a seat’ (āsanassa) means of a seat such as a stool, etc. ‘Facing the foremost limbs’ (purimaaṅgābhimukho) means facing the foremost parts such as the knees, etc. ‘Moving along’ (saṃsaranto) means creeping along. ‘Moving back’ (paccāsaṃsaranto) means creeping back. By ‘this same method’ (eseva nayo), he indicates the forward creeping and backward creeping of the body itself.

Sammā pajānanaṃ sampajānaṃ. Tena attanā kātabbakiccassa karaṇasīlo sampajānakārīti āha – “sampajaññena sabbakiccakārī”ti. Sampajānameva hi sampajaññaṃ. Sampajaññasseva vā kārīti [V 2.0163] sampajaññasseva karaṇasīlo. Sampajaññaṃ karotevāti abhikkantādīsu asammohaṃ uppādeti eva, sampajānasseva vā kāro etassa atthīti sampajānakārī.

‘Knowing rightly’ is clear comprehension (sampajānaṃ). Therefore, one who is in the habit of performing the duties that should be done by oneself is called ‘one who acts with clear comprehension’ (sampajānakārī), as in ‘one who performs all actions by means of clear comprehension.’ For clear comprehension (sampajāna) itself is indeed clear comprehension (sampajañña). Or, ‘one who acts with clear comprehension’ means one who is in the habit of performing clear comprehension. Or, ‘he simply performs clear comprehension’ means he simply produces non-delusion in going forward, etc. Or, ‘the action of one who clearly comprehends exists for him’; therefore, he is ‘one who acts with clear comprehension.’

Dhammato vaḍḍhitasaṅkhātena saha atthena vattatīti sātthakaṃ, abhikkantādi, sātthakassa sampajānanaṃ sātthakasampajaññaṃ. Sappāyassa attano [M 2.0458] upakārāvahassa hitassa sampajānanaṃ sappāyasampajaññaṃ. Abhikkamādīsu bhikkhācāragocare, aññatthāpi ca pavattesu avijahite kammaṭṭhānasaṅkhāte gocare sampajaññaṃ gocarasampajaññaṃ. Abhikkamādīsu asammuyhanameva sampajaññaṃ asammohasampajaññaṃ. Pariggaṇhitvāti tulayitvā tīretvā, paṭisaṅkhāyāti attho. Saṅghadassaneneva uposathapavāraṇādiatthaṃ gamanaṃ saṅgahitaṃ. Asubhadassanādīti ādi-saddena kasiṇaparikammādīnaṃ saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Saṅkhepato vuttamatthaṃ vivarituṃ “cetiyaṃ disvāpi hī”tiādi vuttaṃ. Arahattaṃ pāpuṇātīti ukkaṭṭhaniddeso eso. Samathavipassanuppādanampi hi bhikkhuno vuddhi eva. Dakkhiṇadvāreti cetiyaṅgaṇassa dakkhiṇadvāre, tathā pacchimadvāretiādīsu. Abhayavāpi pāḷiyanti abhayavāpiyā puratthimatīre.

‘Purposeful’ (sātthakaṃ) means it proceeds with a purpose that is designated as ‘growth in accordance with the Dhamma,’ as in ‘going forward,’ etc. The clear comprehension of what is purposeful is ‘purposeful clear comprehension’ (sātthakasampajañña). The clear comprehension of what is suitable (sappāya), helpful to oneself, and beneficial is ‘suitable clear comprehension’ (sappāyasampajañña). In going forward, etc., in the alms-round domain, and also in other activities that occur without abandoning the domain designated as the meditation subject, the clear comprehension is ‘domain clear comprehension’ (gocarasampajañña). Mere non-delusion in going forward, etc., is ‘non-delusion clear comprehension’ (asammohasampajañña). ‘Having considered’ (pariggaṇhitvā) means having weighed and judged; the meaning is ‘having reflected’ (paṭisaṅkhāya). By ‘seeing the Saṅgha’ is included going for the purpose of the Uposatha, Pavāraṇā, etc. ‘Seeing the foul,’ etc.—by the word ‘etc.,’ the inclusion of preliminary work for kasiṇas, etc., should be understood. To explain the meaning stated briefly, ‘for even having seen a shrine,’ etc., is said. ‘He attains arahantship’—this is a statement of the highest level. For the arising of serenity and insight is indeed a bhikkhu’s growth. ‘By the southern gate’ (dakkhiṇadvāre) means at the southern gate of the shrine courtyard; similarly for ‘by the western gate,’ etc. ‘Abhayavāpi in the Pāli text’ means on the eastern bank of the Abhaya tank.

Buddhavaṃsa-ariyavaṃsa-cetiyavaṃsa-dīpavaṃsādivaṃsakathanato mahāariyavaṃsabhāṇako thero. Paññāyanaṭṭhāneti cetiyassa paññāyanaṭṭhāne. Ekapaduddhāreti paduddhārapatiṭṭhānaparivattanaṃ akatvā ekasmiṃyeva avaṭṭhāne. Kecīti abhayagirivāsino.

Because of his narration of the chronicles such as the Buddhavaṃsa, Ariyavaṃsa, Cetiyavaṃsa, and Dīpavaṃsa, the elder was a great reciter of the Noble Lineage. ‘In the place of appearance’ (paññāyanaṭṭhāne) means in the place where the shrine becomes visible. ‘In one stretch’ (ekapaduddhāre) means without changing the lifting and placing of his feet, remaining in one and the same posture. ‘Some’ (kecī) means the residents of Abhayagiri.

Tasmiṃ panāti sātthakasampajaññavasena pariggahitaatthepi gamane. Attho nāma dhammato vaḍḍhīti yaṃ sātthakanti adhippetaṃ gamanaṃ, taṃ sappāyamevāti siyā kassaci āsaṅkāti tannivattanatthaṃ “cetiyadassanaṃ tāvā”tiādi āraddhaṃ. Cittakammarūpakāni viyāti cittakammakatā paṭimāyo viya, yantapayogena vā vicittakammā paṭimāya sadisā yantarūpakā viya. Asamapekkhanaṃ gehassitaaññāṇupekkhāvasena ārammaṇe ayoniso olokanādi. Yaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā uppajjati upekkhā bālassa mūḷhassa puthujjanassā”tiādi (ma. ni. 3.308). Hatthiādisammaddena jīvitantarāyo. Visabhāgarūpadassanādinā brahmacariyantarāyo.

'But in that' (tasmiṃ pana) refers to going even when the purpose is grasped by means of meaningful clear comprehension. Since the purpose is growth according to the Dhamma, and the intended going is meaningful, someone might have a doubt that it is suitable; to dispel that, 'first, seeing the shrine,' etc., is introduced. 'Like images of artwork' (cittakammarūpakāni viya) means like statues made by artwork, or like mechanical images resembling statues of varied artwork due to mechanical application. 'Not examining properly' (asamapekkhanaṃ) means unwise attention to the object, such as looking at it improperly due to indifference rooted in ignorance connected to household life. This refers to what was said: 'When a fool, a deluded ordinary person, sees a form with the eye, indifference arises,' etc. (Majjhima Nikāya 3.308). Danger to life from trampling by elephants, etc. Danger to the holy life from seeing dissimilar forms, etc.

Pabbajitadivasato paṭṭhāya bhikkhūnaṃ anuvattanakathā āciṇṇā, ananuvattanakathā pana tassā aparā dutiyā nāma hotīti āha – “dve kathā nāma na kathitapubbā”ti. Evanti iminā [V 2.0164] “sace panā”tiādikaṃ sabbampi vuttākāraṃ paccāmasati, na “purisassa mātugāmāsubha”ntiādikaṃ vuccamānaṃ.

From the day of ordination onward, talk of compliance by the monks was customary. But talk of non-compliance was a second kind, one that had not existed before—thus it is said: 'Two kinds of talk had not been spoken of before.' 'Thus' (evaṃ) refers back to all that was previously said, such as 'but if,' etc., but not what is now being said, such as 'the impurity of a woman to a man,' etc.

Yogakammassa [M 2.0459] pavattiṭṭhānatāya bhāvanāya ārammaṇaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ vuccatīti āha “kammaṭṭhānasaṅkhātaṃ gocara”nti. Uggahetvāti yathā uggahanimittaṃ uppajjati, evaṃ uggahakosallassa sampādanavasena uggahetvā.

The object of meditation is called a meditation subject because it is the foundational locus for the development of meditative practice. Thus it is said, 'the domain designated as a meditation subject.' 'Having grasped' (uggahetvā) means having grasped in such a way, through accomplishing skill in grasping, that the learning sign arises.

Haratīti kammaṭṭhānaṃ pavatteti, yāva piṇḍapātapaṭikkamā anuyuñjatīti attho. Na paccāharatīti āhārūpabhogato yāva divāṭṭhānupasaṅkamanā kammaṭṭhānaṃ na paṭineti. Samādāya vattati sammā ādiyitvā tesaṃ vattānaṃ paripūraṇavasena vattati. Sarīraparikammanti mukhadhovanādisarīrapaṭijagganaṃ. Dve tayo pallaṅketi dve tayo nisajjāvāre dve tīṇi uṇhāsanāni. Tenāha – “usumaṃ gāhāpento”ti. Kammaṭṭhānasīsenevāti kammaṭṭhānamukheneva kammaṭṭhānaṃ avijahanto eva. Tena “pattopi acetano”tiādinā pavattetabbakammaṭṭhānaṃ, yathāparihariyamānaṃ vā kammaṭṭhānaṃ avijahitvāti dasseti. Tathevāti tikkhattumeva. Paribhogacetiyato sarīracetiyaṃ garutaranti katvā “cetiyaṃ vanditvā”ti cetiyavandanāya paṭhamaṃ karaṇīyatā vuttā. Tathā hi aṭṭhakathāyaṃ – “cetiyaṃ bādhayamānā bodhisākhā haritabbā”ti vuttā. Buddhaguṇānussaraṇavaseneva bodhiñca paṇipātakaraṇanti āha – “buddhassa bhagavato sammukhā viya nipaccakāraṃ dassetvā”ti. Gāmasamīpeti gāmassa upacāraṭṭhāne.

'He carries on' (harati) means he maintains the meditation subject, that is, he applies himself to it until the return from the alms round. 'He does not bring back' (na paccāharati) means he does not turn back the meditation subject from the consumption of food until approaching the day's dwelling. 'He undertakes and maintains' (samādāya vattati) means he acts by properly undertaking and fulfilling those duties. 'Bodily care' (sarīraparikammaṃ) means attending to the body, such as washing the face. 'Two or three sittings' (dve tayo pallaṅke) means two or three sitting sessions, two or three warm seats. Hence it is said: 'causing warmth to be taken.' 'With the meditation subject as the main point' (kammaṭṭhānasīseneva) means with the meditation subject as the opening, without abandoning the meditation subject itself. Thus, by 'even the bowl is non-sentient,' etc., it shows that the meditation subject to be maintained, or the meditation subject being managed, should not be abandoned. 'Similarly' (tatheva) means three times. Considering the bodily shrine as more important than the shrine of use, it is said: 'having paid homage to the shrine'—thus, paying homage to the shrine is stated as the first thing to be done. For in the commentary, it is said: 'If a Bodhi tree branch is obstructing the shrine, it should be removed.' And paying homage to the Bodhi tree is by way of recollecting the virtues of the Buddha—thus it is said: 'as if showing reverence in the presence of the Blessed One himself.' 'Near the village' (gāmasamīpe) means in the vicinity of the village.

Janasaṅgahaṇatthanti “mayi akathente etesaṃ ko kathessatī”ti dhammānuggahena janasaṅgahaṇatthaṃ. Tasmāti yasmā “dhammakathā nāma kathetabbā evā”ti aṭṭhakathācariyā vadanti, yasmā ca dhammakathā kammaṭṭhānavinimuttā nāma natthi, tasmā. Kammaṭṭhānasīsenevāti attanā parihariyamānaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ avijahanto tadanuguṇaṃyeva dhammakathaṃ kathetvā. Anumodanaṃ katvāti etthāpi “kammaṭṭhānasīsenevā”ti ānetvā sambandhitabbaṃ. Sampattaparicchedenevāti paricito aparicitotiādivibhāgaṃ akatvā sampattakoṭiyā eva, samāgamamattenevāti attho. Bhayeti paracakkādibhaye.

'For the purpose of gathering people' (janasaṅgahaṇatthaṃ) means for the purpose of gathering people by favoring them with the Dhamma, thinking: 'If I do not speak, who will speak to them?' 'Therefore' (tasmā) means because the commentary teachers say, 'A Dhamma talk should indeed be given,' and because there is no Dhamma talk that is entirely free from a meditation subject, therefore. 'With the meditation subject as the main point' (kammaṭṭhānasīseneva) means without abandoning the meditation subject that one personally maintains, but giving a Dhamma talk in accordance with that very one. 'Having made an expression of approval' (anumodanaṃ katvā)—here too, 'with the meditation subject as the main point' should be brought in and connected. 'By the measure of who is present' (sampattaparicchedeneva) means without making distinctions such as 'familiar' or 'unfamiliar,' etc., but simply by the group of those who have arrived; by the mere fact of their assembly is the meaning. 'In fear' (bhaye) means in fear of a foreign army, etc.

Kammajatejoti gahaṇiṃ sandhāyāha. Kammaṭṭhānavīthiṃ nārohati khudāparissamena kilantakāyattā samādhānābhāvato. Avasesaṭṭhāneti yāguyā [M 2.0460] aggahitaṭṭhāne. Poṅkhānupoṅkhanti kammaṭṭhānupaṭṭhānassa [V 2.0165] aviccheda-dassanametaṃ, yathā poṅkhānupoṅkhaṃ pavattāya sarapaṭipāṭiyā anavicchedo, evametassapi kammaṭṭhānupaṭṭhānassāti vuttaṃ hoti.

'Energy born of kamma' (kammajatejo) refers to the digestive fire. He does not ascend the path of meditation because, due to hunger and the fatigue of the body, concentration is lacking. 'In the remaining place' (avasesaṭṭhāne) means in the place not reached by the rice gruel. 'Sequentially' (poṅkhānupoṅkhaṃ) shows the uninterrupted attending to the meditation subject. It is said that just as there is no interruption in a series of arrows proceeding sequentially, so too is the uninterrupted attending to this meditation subject.

Nikkhittadhuro bhāvanānuyoge. Vattapaṭipattiyā apūraṇena sabbavattāni bhinditvā. Kāme avītarāgo hoti. Kāye avītarāgo. Rūpe avītarāgo. Yāvadatthaṃ udarāvadehaṃ bhuñjitvā seyyasukhaṃ passasukhaṃ middhasukhaṃ anuyutto viharati. Aññataraṃ devanikāyaṃ paṇidhāya brahmacariyaṃ caratī”ti (dī. ni. 3.320; ma. ni. 1.186) evaṃ vuttaṃ pañcavidhacetovinibandhacitto. Caritvāti pavattitvā.

One who has laid down the burden in the application to development. By not fulfilling the duties of practice, having broken all observances. He is not free from lust for sensual pleasures. He is not free from lust for the body. He is not free from lust for forms. Having eaten just enough to fill his belly, he lives indulging in the pleasure of sleep, the pleasure of reclining, the pleasure of drowsiness. 'He lives practicing the holy life, aspiring to a certain group of deities' (Dīgha Nikāya 3.320; Majjhima Nikāya 1.186)—thus is described one whose mind is bound by the five mental bonds. 'Having lived' (caritvā) means having conducted oneself.

Gatapaccāgatikavattavasenāti bhāvanāsahitaṃyeva bhikkhāya gatapaccāgataṃ gamanapaccāgamanaṃ etassa atthīti gatapaccāgatikaṃ, tadeva vattaṃ, tassa vasena. Attano hitasukhaṃ kāmenti icchantīti attakāmā, dhammacchandavanto. “Dhammo”ti hi hitaṃ taṃnimittakañca sukhanti. Atha vā viññūnaṃ dhammānaṃ attaniyattā attabhāvaparicchannattā ca attā nāma dhammo. Tenāha bhagavā – “attadīpā, bhikkhave, viharatha attasaraṇā”tiādi (saṃ. ni. 3.43). Taṃ kāmenti icchantīti attakāmā. Usabhaṃ nāma vīsati yaṭṭhiyo. Tāya saññāyāti tāya pāsāṇasaññāya, “ettakaṃ ṭhānamāgatā”ti jānantāti adhippāyo. So eva nayo ayaṃ bhikkhūtiādiko yo ṭhāne vutto, so eva nisajjāyapi nayo. Pacchato āgacchantānaṃ chinnabhattabhāvabhayenapi yonisomanasikāraṃ paribrūheti.

By means of the practice of one who comes and goes: this means that the coming and going for alms is accompanied by meditation; that is the practice, by means of that. 'Desiring their own welfare' (attakāmā) means they are desiring their own welfare, endowed with a desire for the Dhamma. For 'Dhamma' refers to welfare and the happiness associated with it. Alternatively, because conscious qualities are possessed by oneself and are encompassed by one’s own being, the Dhamma is called the self. Thus the Blessed One said: 'Dwell as an island unto yourselves, monks, with yourselves as refuge,' and so on (Saṃyutta Nikāya 3.43). Desiring that, they are 'desiring their own welfare.' An usabha is twenty rods. 'By that perception' means by that perception of the stone, knowing, 'We have come this far'—this is the intended meaning. The same method that was stated for the standing posture concerning the monk, etc., also applies to sitting. For those coming afterward, one should also foster wise attention out of fear of missing the meal.

Maddantāti dhaññakaraṇaṭṭhāne sālisīsāni maddantā. Mahāpadhānaṃ pūjessāmīti amhākaṃ atthāya lokanāthena cha vassāni kataṃ dukkaracariyaṃ evāhaṃ yathāsatti pūjessāmīti. Paṭipattipūjā hi satthupūjā, na āmisapūjāti. Ṭhānacaṅkamamevāti adhiṭṭhātabbairiyāpathakālavasena vuttaṃ, na bhojanādikālesu avassaṃ kātabbanisajjāya paṭikkhepavasena.

'They are threshing' means they are crushing rice heads at the threshing ground. 'I will honor the great exertion' means, 'For our sake, the Lord of the World practiced the difficult asceticism for six years—thus, I will honor it to the best of my ability.' For honoring through practice is indeed the true honoring of the Teacher, not honoring with material offerings. 'Only standing and walking' refers to what should be determined according to the time for the prescribed posture, not as a rejection of sitting which is necessary at mealtime and on other occasions.

Vīthiṃ otaritvā ito cito anoloketvā paṭhamameva vīthiyo sallakkhetabbāti āha “vīthiyo sallakkhetvā”ti. Yaṃ sandhāya [M 2.0461] vuccati – “pāsādikena abhikkantenā”tiādi. Taṃ dassetuṃ “tattha cā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Āhāre paṭikūlasaññaṃ upaṭṭhapetvātiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ heṭṭhā vuttameva. Aṭṭhaṅgasamannāgatanti “yāvadeva imassa kāyassa ṭhitiyā”tiādinā (ma. ni. 1.23; 2.24; 3.75; saṃ. ni. 4.120; a. ni. 6.58; 8.9) vuttehi aṭṭhahi aṅgehi samannāgataṃ katvā. Neva davāyātiādi pana paṭikkhepadassanaṃ.

Having descended onto the path, without looking here and there, one should first observe the paths—thus he said, 'having observed the paths.' With reference to this, it is said: 'with a pleasing demeanor while going forward,' etc. To show that, 'and therein,' etc., was stated. Regarding establishing the perception of repulsiveness in food, etc., what should be said has already been said below. 'Endowed with eight factors' means having made it endowed with the eight factors stated in passages such as 'just for the maintenance of this body,' etc. (Majjhima Nikāya 1.23; 2.24; 3.75; Saṃyutta Nikāya 4.120; Aṅguttara Nikāya 6.58; 8.9). 'But not for pleasure,' etc., however, shows the rejection.

Paccekabodhiṃ [V 2.0166] sacchikaroti, yadi upanissayasampanno hotīti sambandho. Idañca yathā heṭṭhā tīsu ṭhānesu, evaṃ ito paresu ṭhānesu upanetvā sambandhitabbaṃ. Tattha paccekabodhiyā upanissayasampadā kappānaṃ dve asaṅkhyeyyāni satasahassañca tajjaṃ puññañāṇasambhārasambharaṇaṃ, sāvakabodhiyaṃ aggasāvakānaṃ ekaṃ asaṅkhyeyyaṃ kappasatasahassañca, mahāsāvakānaṃ kappasatasahassameva tajjaṃ sambhārasambharaṇaṃ, itaresaṃ atītāsu jātīsu vivaṭṭasannissayavasena nibbattitaṃ nibbedhabhāgiyaṃ kusalaṃ. Bāhiyo dārucīriyoti bāhiyavisaye jātasaṃvaddhatāya bāhiyo, dārucīrapariharaṇena dārucīriyoti laddhasamañño. So hi āyasmā “tasmātiha te, bāhiya, evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ – diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṃ bhavissatī”tiādivasappavattena (udā. 10) saṃkhitteneva ovādena khippataraṃ visesaṃ adhigacchi. Tena vuttaṃ “khippābhiñño vā hoti seyyathāpi thero bāhiyo dārucīriyo”ti. Evaṃ mahāpañño vātiādīsu yathārahaṃ vattabbanti.

He realizes Paccekabodhi if he is endowed with the supporting conditions—this is the connection. And this should be connected to the other cases by applying it as explained above in the three instances. Herein, the fulfillment of supporting conditions for Paccekabodhi involves accumulating the requisites of merit and knowledge for two incalculable aeons and a hundred thousand aeons. For Sāvakabodhi, the chief disciples require one incalculable aeon and a hundred thousand aeons, while the great disciples require only a hundred thousand aeons for the same accumulation of requisites. For others, it is the wholesome qualities partaking of penetration, developed in past lives by way of a support for turning away from saṃsāra. Bāhiyo Dārucīriyo—he was called Bāhiyo because he was born and raised in the Bāhiya country, and Dārucīriyo because he wore a robe of bark-cloth. Indeed, that venerable one quickly attained a special attainment through the brief instruction: "Therefore, Bāhiya, you should train thus: 'In the seen, there will be merely the seen...'" (Ud. 1.10). Hence it is said, "Or he may be one of quick direct knowledge, like the elder Bāhiyo Dārucīriyo." Similarly, in the cases of "one of great wisdom," and so forth, it should be explained appropriately.

Tanti asammuyhanaṃ. Evanti idāni vuccamānākāradassanaṃ. Attā abhikkamatīti iminā andhaputhujjanassa diṭṭhiggāhavasena abhikkame sammuyhanaṃ dasseti, ahaṃ abhikkamāmīti pana iminā mānaggāhavasena, tadubhayaṃ pana taṇhāya vinā na hotīti taṇhāggāhavasenapi sammuyhanaṃ dassitameva hoti, “tathā asammuyhanto”ti vatvā taṃ asammuyhanaṃ yena ghanavinibbhogena hoti, taṃ dassento “abhikkamāmīti citte uppajjamāne”tiādimāha. Tattha yasmā vāyodhātuyā anugatā tejodhātu uddharaṇassa paccayo. Uddharaṇagatikā hi tejodhātūti uddharaṇe vāyodhātuyā tassā anugatabhāvo, tasmā imāsaṃ dvinnamettha dhātūnaṃ sāmatthiyato adhimattatā, itarāsañca omattatāti dassento “ekekapāduddharaṇe…pe… balavatiyo”ti āha. Yasmā pana tejodhātuyā anugatā [M 2.0462] vāyodhātu atiharaṇavītiharaṇānaṃ paccayo. Tiriyagatikāya hi vāyodhātuyā atiharaṇavītiharaṇesu sātisayo byāpāroti tejodhātuyā tassānugatabhāvo, tasmā imāsaṃ dvinnamettha sāmatthiyato adhimattatā, itarāsañca omattatāti dassento “tathā atiharaṇavītiharaṇesū”ti āha. Satipi anugamanānugantabbatāvisese tejodhātuvāyodhātubhāvamattaṃ sandhāya tathā-saddaggahaṇaṃ.

The thread of non-delusion. 'Thus' shows the mode now being spoken of. 'A self moves forward'—this shows the delusion of the blind ordinary person regarding movement, through the grasping of views. 'I move forward'—this shows delusion through the grasping of conceit. But since neither of these occurs without craving, delusion through the grasping of craving is also shown. Having stated, 'thus, not being deluded,' he then shows that non-delusion which occurs through the breaking up of compactness, saying, 'when the thought "I move forward" arises,' and so on. Herein, because the fire element, accompanied by the wind element, is the condition for lifting. For the fire element has an upward-moving nature, and in lifting, it is accompanied by the wind element. Therefore, to show that due to their capacity these two elements are predominant here, and the others are inferior, he says, 'in the lifting of each foot... are strong.' And because the wind element, accompanied by the fire element, is the condition for bringing forward and moving aside. For the wind element has a transverse motion, and its function is pre-eminent in bringing forward and moving aside, wherein it is accompanied by the fire element. Therefore, to show that due to their capacity these two elements are predominant here, and the others are inferior, he says, 'so too in bringing forward and moving aside.' Although there is a distinction between that which accompanies and that which is accompanied, the word 'thus' is employed in reference to merely the state of being the fire element and the wind element.

Tattha akkantaṭṭhānato pādassa ukkhipanaṃ uddharaṇaṃ, ṭhitaṭṭhānaṃ atikkamitvā purato haraṇaṃ atiharaṇaṃ. Rukkhakhāṇuādipariharaṇatthaṃ, patiṭṭhitapādaghaṭṭanapariharaṇatthaṃ vā passena haraṇaṃ vītiharaṇaṃ. Yāva patiṭṭhitapādo, tāva āharaṇaṃ atiharaṇaṃ, tato paraṃ haraṇaṃ vītiharaṇanti ayaṃ vā etesaṃ viseso. Yasmā pathavīdhātuyā anugatā āpodhātu vossajjanassa paccayo. Garutarasabhāvā hi āpodhātūti vossajjane pathavīdhātuyā tassānugatabhāvo, tasmā tāsaṃ dvinnamettha [V 2.0167] sāmatthiyato adhimattatā, itarāsañca omattatāti dassento āha “vossajjane…pe… balavatiyo”ti. Yasmā pana āpodhātuyā anugatā pathavīdhātu sannikkhepanassa paccayo. Patiṭṭhābhāve viya patiṭṭhāpanepi tassā sātisayakiccattā āpodhātuyā tassā anugatabhāvo, tathā ghaṭṭanakiriyāya pathavīdhātuyā vasena sannirumbhanassa sijjhanato tatthāpi pathavīdhātuyā āpodhātuanugatabhāvo, tasmā vuttaṃ – “tathā sannikkhepanasannirumbhanesū”ti.

Therein, lifting the foot from the place where it was set down is 'uddharaṇa' (raising). Moving it forward beyond the standing place is 'atiharaṇa' (bringing forward). Moving it sideways to avoid a tree, stump, or the like, or to avoid striking the already placed foot, is 'vītiharaṇa' (moving aside). Or, this is the distinction among them: 'āharaṇa' (bringing) and 'atiharaṇa' (bringing forward) occur until the foot is placed; after that, 'haraṇa' (moving) and 'vītiharaṇa' (moving aside). Because the water element, accompanied by the earth element, is the condition for letting go. For the water element is heavier in nature, and in letting go, it is accompanied by the earth element. Therefore, to show that due to their capacity these two elements are predominant here, and the others are inferior, he says, 'in letting go... are strong.' And because the earth element, accompanied by the water element, is the condition for depositing. Because its (the earth element's) function is pre-eminent in establishing as well as in being established, it is accompanied by the water element; similarly, because the act of restraining is accomplished by means of the earth element in the action of contact, there too the earth element is accompanied by the water element. Therefore, it is said: 'Likewise, in depositing and restraining.'

Tatthāti tasmiṃ abhikkamane, tesu vā vuttesu uddharaṇādīsu chasu koṭṭhāsesu. Uddharaṇeti uddharaṇakkhaṇe. Rūpārūpadhammāti uddharaṇākārena pavattā rūpadhammā taṃsamuṭṭhāpakā arūpadhammā ca. Atiharaṇaṃ na pāpuṇanti khaṇamattāvaṭṭhānato. Tattha tatthevāti yattha yattha uppannā, tattha tattheva. Na hi dhammānaṃ desantarasaṅkamanaṃ atthi. Pabbaṃ pabbantiādi uddharaṇādikoṭṭhāse sandhāya sabhāgasantativasena vuttanti veditabbaṃ. Atiittaro hi rūpadhammānampi pavattikkhaṇo, gamanassādānaṃ devaputtānaṃ heṭṭhupariyāyena [M 2.0463] paṭimukhaṃ dhāvantānaṃ sirasi pāde ca baddhakhuradhārāsamāgamatopi sīghataro. Yathā tilānaṃ bhajjiyamānānaṃ taṭataṭāyanena bhedo lakkhīyati, evaṃ saṅkhatadhammānaṃ uppādenāti dassanatthaṃ “taṭataṭāyantā”ti vuttaṃ. Uppannā hi ekantato bhijjantīti.

Therein: in that advancing, or in those six sections mentioned, such as lifting. At the moment of lifting: this refers to the moment of lifting. Material and immaterial phenomena: material phenomena occurring in the manner of lifting, and the immaterial phenomena that produce them. They do not reach the stage of being brought forward, as they last for only a moment. There and there only: wherever they arise, there and there only they cease, for phenomena do not move from one place to another. 'Section by section,' and so on, referring to the sections like lifting, should be understood as spoken in terms of a homogeneous continuity. Indeed, the duration of a moment of material phenomena is exceedingly brief, swifter than the meeting of razor-blades bound to the heads and feet of celestial youths who delight in movement, as they race towards each other, one above and one below. Just as the bursting of sesame seeds being roasted is perceived by their crackling sound, so the word 'crackling' (`taṭataṭāyantā`) is used to show that conditioned phenomena break up upon arising. For whatever has arisen, certainly breaks.

Saddhiṃ rūpenāti idaṃ tassa tassa cittassa nirodhena saddhiṃ nirujjhanakarūpadhammavasena vuttaṃ, yaṃ tato sattarasamacittassa uppādakkhaṇe uppannaṃ. Aññathā yadi rūpārūpadhammā samānakkhaṇā siyuṃ, “rūpaṃ garupariṇāmaṃ dandhanirodha”ntiādivacanehi virodho siyā. Tathā – “nāhaṃ, bhikkhave, aññaṃ ekadhammampi samanupassāmi, yaṃ evaṃ lahuparivattaṃ, yathayidaṃ citta”nti (a. ni. 1.48) evamādipāḷiyā ca. Cittacetasikā hi sārammaṇasabhāvā yathābalaṃ attano ārammaṇapaccayabhūtamatthaṃ vibhāventāyeva uppajjantīti tesaṃ taṃsabhāvanipphattianantaraṃ nirodho, rūpadhammā pana anārammaṇā pakāsetabbā. Evaṃ tesaṃ pakāsetabbabhāvanipphatti soḷasahi cittehi hotīti taṅkhaṇāyukatā tesaṃ icchitā, lahukaviññāṇassa visayasaṅgatimattapaccayatāya tiṇṇaṃ khandhānaṃ, visayasaṅgatimattatāya ca viññāṇassa lahuparivattitā, dandhamahābhūtapaccayatāya rūpadhammānaṃ dandhaparivattitā. Nānādhātuyā yathābhūtañāṇaṃ kho pana tathāgatasseva, tena ca purejātapaccayo rūpadhammova vutto, pacchājātapaccayo ca tassevāti rūpārūpadhammānaṃ samānakkhaṇatā na yujjateva, tasmā vuttanayenevettha attho veditabbo.

The phrase 'with form' (`saddhiṃ rūpena`) is spoken in reference to a material phenomenon that ceases along with the cessation of its respective consciousness, which material phenomenon arose at the arising-moment of the seventeenth consciousness prior. Otherwise, if material and immaterial phenomena were of the same moment, there would be a contradiction with such statements as, 'form undergoes heavy transformation and has a slow cessation.' And likewise with such Pāli texts as: 'Monks, I do not perceive even one other thing that changes as swiftly as this mind' (A.N. 1.48). For consciousness and mental factors, which by nature have an object, arise only as they discern their respective objective condition according to their capacity; thus their cessation occurs immediately after the accomplishment of that nature. Material phenomena, however, are without an object and are to be made manifest. Thus, the accomplishment of their state of being manifest occurs by means of sixteen consciousnesses, and their duration for that period is accepted. The swift changeableness of the three aggregates is due to their being a condition for merely connecting with the object of a light consciousness, and the swift changeableness of consciousness is due to its merely connecting with an object. The slow changeableness of material phenomena is due to their being conditioned by the slow great elements. However, the knowledge of the diverse elements as they really are belongs only to the Tathāgata. And by him, the pre-nascent condition is said to be only a material phenomenon, and the post-nascent condition also pertains to this relationship. Therefore, the simultaneity of material and immaterial phenomena is indeed not tenable. Thus, the meaning here should be understood in the way that has been stated.

Aññaṃ [V 2.0168] uppajjate cittaṃ, aññaṃ cittaṃ nirujjhatīti yaṃ purimuppannaṃ cittaṃ, taṃ aññaṃ, taṃ pana nirujjhantaṃ aparassa anantarādipaccayo hutvā eva nirujjhatīti tathāladdhapaccayaṃ aññaṃ uppajjate cittaṃ. Yadi evaṃ tesaṃ antaro labbheyyāti, noti āha “avīcimanupabandho”ti. Yathā vīci antaro na labbhati, “tadeveta”nti avisesavidū maññanti, evaṃ anu anu pabandho cittasantāno rūpasantāno ca nadīsotova nadiyaṃ udakappavāho viya vattati.

One consciousness arises, another consciousness ceases. The consciousness that arose first is different; and that consciousness, as it ceases, ceases only after becoming an immediately proximate condition and so forth for the next, and so another consciousness arises having obtained such a condition. If it were so, could an interval be found between them? To this, it is said, 'No, it is an uninterrupted continuity.' Just as no interval is found between waves, and those who do not know the distinction think, 'It is just that same thing,' so too the continuous succession of consciousness and the continuous succession of matter proceeds, like the current of a river, like a flow of water in a river.

Abhimukhaṃ lokitaṃ ālokitanti āha “puratopekkhana”nti. Yasmā yaṃdisābhimukho gacchati tiṭṭhati nisīdati vā, tadabhimukhaṃ pekkhanaṃ ālokitaṃ[M 2.0464], tasmā tadanugataṃ vidisālokanaṃ vilokitanti āha “vilokitaṃ nāma anudisāpekkhana”nti. Sammajjanaparibhaṇḍādikaraṇe olokitassa, ullokāharaṇādīsu ullokitassa, pacchato āgacchantassa parissayassa parivajjanādivasena apalokitassa ca siyā sambhavoti āha – “iminā vā mukhena sabbānipi tāni gahitānevā”ti.

He says that 'looking directly' (ābhimukhaṃ lokitaṃ) is 'looking ahead' (ālokitaṃ), meaning 'looking forward' (puratopekkhanaṃ). Since looking in the direction one faces when going, standing, or sitting is 'looking ahead' (ālokitaṃ), therefore, following that, he says that looking in the intermediate directions (vidisālokanaṃ) is 'looking around' (vilokitaṃ), meaning 'looking towards the intermediate directions' (anudisāpekkhanaṃ). Since there might be the occurrence of looking down when sweeping, arranging articles, and so forth; of looking up when fetching things from above, and so forth; and of looking back for the purpose of avoiding dangers coming from behind, and so forth, he says, 'Or by this method, all of those are indeed included.'

Kāyasakkhinti kāyena sacchikatavantaṃ, paccakkhakārinanti attho. Sohāyasmā vipassanākāle eva “yamevāhaṃ indriyesu aguttadvārataṃ nissāya sāsane anabhiratiādivippakāraṃ patto, tameva suṭṭhu niggahessāmī”ti ussāhajāto balavahirottappo, tattha ca katādhikārattā indriyasaṃvare ukkaṃsapāramippatto, teneva naṃ satthā – “etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāvakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ indriyesu guttadvārānaṃ yadidaṃ nando”ti (a. ni. 1.230) etadagge ṭhapesi.

'Bodily witness' (kāyasakkhī) means one who has realized with the body, that is, one who has directly experienced. This venerable one, during the time of insight, resolved thus: 'That very thing by which I, on account of the unguardedness of the sense doors, have attained such states as dissatisfaction with the Teaching—that very thing I will thoroughly restrain.' Thus, he became energetic, with strong moral shame and fear of wrongdoing, and because of the foundation he had laid therein, he reached the highest perfection in the restraint of the faculties. For that reason, the Teacher placed him in the foremost position, declaring: 'Monks, among my monk disciples who are foremost in guarding the sense doors, Nanda is the foremost' (A. Ni. 1.230).

Sātthakatā ca sappāyatā ca veditabbā ālokitavilokitassāti ānetvā sambandho. Tasmāti kammaṭṭhānāvijahanasseva gocarasampajaññabhāvatoti vuttamevatthaṃ hetubhāvena paccāmasati. Attano kammaṭṭhānavaseneva ālokanavilokanaṃ kātabbaṃ, khandhādikammaṭṭhānikehi añño upāyo na gavesitabboti adhippāyo. Yasmā ālokitādisamaññāpi dhammamattasseva pavattiviseso, tasmā tassa yāthāvato pajānanaṃ asammohasampajaññanti dassetuṃ “abbhantare”tiādi vuttaṃ. Cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphāravasenāti kiriyamayacittasamuṭṭhānavāyodhātuyā calanākārapavattivasena. Adho sīdatīti thokaṃ otarati. Uddhaṃ laṅghetīti laṅghantaṃ viya upari gacchati.

The connection is made by bringing in the phrase 'the purposefulness (sātthakatā) and the suitability (sappāyatā) of looking ahead and looking around should be understood.' 'Therefore' re-examines the meaning already stated as a cause, namely, that clear comprehension of the domain (gocarasampajañña) belongs only to one who does not abandon the meditation subject. The intention is that one should look ahead and look around only in accordance with one's own meditation subject; those whose meditation subject is the aggregates, etc., should not seek another method. Since even the designation 'looking ahead' and so forth is merely a particular mode of occurrence of phenomena alone, therefore, to show that understanding it as it really is is clear comprehension of non-delusion (asammohasampajañña), the phrase 'internally' and so forth is stated. 'Through the manifestation of the wind element of mental activity' means through the mode of occurrence as the movement of the wind element produced by a functional mind. 'It sinks downward' means it descends slightly. 'It leaps upward' means it goes upward as if leaping.

Aṅgakiccaṃ sādhayamānanti padhānabhūtaṃ aṅgakiccaṃ nipphādentaṃ, upapattibhavassa sarīraṃ hutvāti attho. Paṭhamajavanepi…pe… sattamajavanepi na hotīti idaṃ pañcadvāraviññāṇavīthiyaṃ “itthī puriso”ti [V 2.0169] rajjanādīnaṃ abhāvaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Tattha hi āvajjanavoṭṭhabbanānaṃ ayoniso āvajjanavoṭṭhabbanavasena iṭṭhe itthirūpādimhi lobhamattaṃ, aniṭṭhe paṭighamattaṃ uppajjati. Manodvāre pana “itthī puriso”ti rajjanādi hoti, tassa pañcadvārajavanaṃ mūlaṃ, yathāvuttaṃ [M 2.0465] vā sabbaṃ bhavaṅgādi. Evaṃ manodvārajavanassa mūlabhūtadhammaparijānanavaseneva mūlapariññā vuttā, āgantukatāvakālikatā pana pañcadvārajavanasseva apubbabhāvavasena ceva ittarabhāvavasena ca vuttā. Heṭṭhupariyavasena bhijjitvā patitesūti heṭṭhimassa uparimassa ca aparāparaṃ bhaṅgappattimāha.

'Accomplishing the function of a factor' means producing the principal function of a factor; the meaning is being the body for the rebirth-process existence. The statement 'it does not occur in the first impulsion... nor in the seventh impulsion' is said with reference to the absence of lust and so forth regarding 'a woman,' 'a man,' in the five-door cognitive process. For there, through unwise adverting and determining by the adverting and determining consciousnesses, mere greed arises towards a desirable object such as a female form, and mere aversion towards an undesirable one. But in the mind-door, lust and so forth regarding 'a woman,' 'a man,' occurs; for that, the five-door impulsion is the root, or all that was said, such as the life-continuum, is the root. Thus, full understanding of the root is spoken of through understanding the phenomena that are the root of the mind-door impulsion. But the adventitious and temporary nature is spoken of for the five-door impulsion itself, on account of its unprecedented nature and its transient nature. 'Having broken up and fallen away below and above' describes the successive attainment of dissolution of the lower and the upper.

Tanti javanaṃ. Tassa javanassa na yuttanti sambandho. Āgantuko abbhāgato. Udayabbayaparicchinno tāvatako kālo etesanti tāvakālikāni.

'Thread' (tanti) means impulsion (javana). The connection is: 'it is not suitable for that impulsion.' An 'adventitious one' (āgantuko) is a visitor (abbhāgato). 'Temporary' (tāvakālikāni) are so called because their time is limited by arising and passing away.

Etaṃ asammohasampajaññaṃ. Samavāyeti sāmaggiyaṃ. Tatthāti pañcakkhandhavasena ālokanavilokane paññāyamāne tabbinimutto – ko eko āloketi, ko viloketi. Upanissayapaccayoti idaṃ suttantanayena pariyāyato vuttaṃ. Sahajātapaccayoti nidassanamattametaṃ aññamaññasampayuttaatthiavigatādipaccayānampi labbhanato.

This is clear comprehension of non-delusion (asammohasampajañña). 'Concord' (samavāya) means harmony (sāmaggiyaṃ). 'Therein' (tatthāti) means that when looking ahead and looking around are being understood by way of the five aggregates, one is freed from the notion: 'Who is the one that looks ahead? Who is the one that looks around?' The 'dependence condition' (upanissayapaccayo) is stated indirectly, in the manner of the Suttas. The 'co-nascence condition' (sahajātapaccayo) is merely an indication, since other conditions such as mutuality (aññamañña), association (sampayutta), presence (atthi), and non-disappearance (avigata), and so forth, are also found.

Kāleti samiñjituṃ yuttakāle samiñjantassa, tathā pasāretuṃ yuttakāle pasārentassa. “Maṇisappo nāma ekā sappajātī”ti vadanti. Laḷananti kampanaṃ, līḷākaraṇaṃ vā.

'At the time' means of one who contracts at the proper time to contract, and likewise of one who stretches out at the proper time to stretch out. They say that 'Maṇisappo' is a species of snake. 'Dallying' (laḷanaṃ) means trembling (kampanaṃ) or playing (līḷākaraṇaṃ).

Uṇhapakatiko pariḷāhabahulakāyo. Sīlassa vidūsanena ahitāvahattā micchājīvavasena uppannaṃ asappāyaṃ. “Cīvarampi acetana”ntiādinā cīvarassa viya kāyopi acetanoti kāyassa attasuññatāvibhāvanena “abbhantare”tiādinā vuttamevatthaṃ paridīpento itarītarasantosassa kāraṇaṃ dasseti. Tenāha “tasmā”tiādi.

The body is by nature hot (uṇhapakatiko), abounding in feverish heat (pariḷāhabahulakāyo). What is unsuitable (asappāyaṃ) arises by way of wrong livelihood, which is unbeneficial because it corrupts virtue. By explaining the meaning already stated in the passage beginning 'internally' (abbhantare), revealing the emptiness of self in the body—that is, just as the robe is inanimate, so too is the body inanimate, as in the passage beginning 'the robe is also inanimate'—he shows the cause for contentment with whatever one gets. Therefore, he says, 'Therefore' (tasmā) and so forth.

Catupañcagaṇṭhikāhatoti āhatacatupañcagaṇṭhiko, catupañcagaṇṭhikāhi vā hatasobho.

'Struck by four or five knots' (catupañcagaṇṭhikāhato) means one who is struck by four or five knots (āhatacatupañcagaṇṭhiko), or one whose splendor is destroyed by four or five knots (catupañcagaṇṭhikāhi vā hatasobho).

Aṭṭhavidhopi atthoti aṭṭhavidhopi payojanaviseso. Mahāsivattheravādavasena “imassa kāyassa ṭhitiyā”tiādinā nayena vutto daṭṭhabbo. Imasmiṃ pakkhe “neva davāyātiādinā nayenā”ti pana idaṃ paṭikkhepaṅgadassanamukhena pāḷi āgatāti katvā vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ.

The 'eightfold benefit' (aṭṭhavidhopi attho) means the eightfold special purpose (payojanaviseso).
