It should be understood as stated in the way beginning with 'for the maintenance of this body' (imassa kāyassa ṭhitiyā), according to the teaching of the Elder Mahāsiva. But in this case, it should be understood that the passage beginning with 'not for amusement' (neva davāya) and so forth is stated because the Pāli text has come by way of showing the limb of refutation.

Pathavīsandhārakajalassa [M 2.0466][V 2.0170] taṃsandhārakavāyunā viya paribhuttassa āhārassa vāyodhātuyāva āsaye avaṭṭhānanti āha – “vāyodhātuvaseneva tiṭṭhatī”ti. Atiharatīti yāva mukhā abhiharati. Vītiharatīti tato kucchiyaṃ vimissaṃ karonto harati. Atiharatīti vā mukhadvāraṃ atikkāmento harati. Vītiharatīti kucchigataṃ passato harati. Parivattetīti aparāparaṃ cāreti. Ettha ca āhārassa dhāraṇaparivattanasañcuṇṇanavisosanāni pathavīdhātusahitā eva vāyodhātu karoti, na kevalāti tāni pathavīdhātuyā kiccabhāvena vuttāni, sā eva dhāraṇādīni kiccāni karontassa sādhāraṇāti vuttāni. Allattañca anupāletīti yathā vāyodhātuādīhi aññehi visosanaṃ na hoti, tathā anupāleti allabhāvaṃ. Tejodhātūti gahaṇīsaṅkhātā tejodhātu. Sā hi antopaviṭṭhaṃ āhāraṃ paripāceti. Añjaso hotīti āhārassa pavisanādīnaṃ maggo hoti. Ābhujatīti pariyesanavasena, ajjhoharaṇajiṇṇājiṇṇatādipaṭisaṃvedanavasena ca āvajjeti, vijānātīti attho. Taṃtaṃvijānanassa paccayabhūtoyeva hi payogo “sammāpayogo”ti vutto. Yena hi payogena pariyesanādi nipphajjati, so tabbisayavijānanampi nipphādeti nāma tadavinābhāvato. Atha vā sammāpayogaṃ sammāpaṭipattiṃ anvāya āgamma ābhujati samannāharati. Ābhogapubbako hi sabbopi viññāṇabyāpāroti tathā vuttaṃ.

Just as the earth element supports water, and the wind element supports that water, so too the food that has been consumed remains in the stomach by means of the wind element—thus it is said, "It remains by means of the wind element." Atiharati means it brings food up to the mouth. Vītiharati means it then mixes and carries it into the stomach. Alternatively, atiharati means it carries the food past the mouth opening. Vītiharati means it carries the food that has gone into the stomach towards digestion. Parivatteti means it circulates it back and forth. Here, the functions of holding, turning, grinding, and drying the food are performed by the wind element together with the earth element, not by the wind element alone. Therefore, these functions—holding and so on—are spoken of as the duties of the earth element, since it is a common factor in performing the functions of holding and so on. And 'it maintains moisture' means it preserves the moist state, so that it is not dried out by other elements such as the wind element. The fire element refers to the fire element known as the digestive fire (gahaṇī). For it digests the food that has entered inside. Añjaso hoti means it becomes a passage for the food's entry and so on. Ābhujati means it attends to the food by way of searching, and by way of experiencing whether the food is swallowed, digested, undigested, and so on; that is, it discerns. Indeed, the exertion which is the condition for discerning this and that is called "right exertion." For by the exertion through which searching and so on is accomplished, that also produces discernment of that subject, since they are inseparable. Alternatively, it attends and gathers the mind by following and relying on right exertion, that is, right practice. For all activities of consciousness are preceded by reflection—thus it is said.

Gamanatoti bhikkhācāravasena gocaragāmaṃ uddissa gamanato. Pariyesanatoti gocaragāme bhikkhatthaṃ āhiṇḍanato. Paribhogatoti āhārassa paribhuñjanato. Āsayatoti pittādiāsayato. Āsayati ettha ekajjhaṃ pavattamānopi kammabalavavatthito hutvā mariyādavasena aññamaññaṃ asaṅkarato sayati tiṭṭhati pavattatīti āsayo, āmāsayassa upari tiṭṭhanako pittādiko. Mariyādattho hi ayamākāro. Nidheti yathābhutto āhāro nicito hutvā tiṭṭhati etthāti nidhānaṃ, āmāsayo, tato nidhānato. Aparipakkatoti gahaṇīsaṅkhātena kammajatejena avipakkato. Paripakkatoti yathābhuttassa āhārassa vipakkabhāvato. Phalatoti nipphattito. Nissandatoti ito cito ca vissandanato[M 2.0467]. Sammakkhanatoti sabbaso makkhanato. Ayamettha saṅkhepo, vitthāro pana visuddhimaggasaṃvaṇṇanāya (visuddhi. mahāṭī. 1.294) gahetabbo.

Going means going to an alms-village for an alms-round. Seeking means wandering in the alms-village for alms. Consuming means partaking of the food. Abode means the abode of bile and so on. Here, āsayati means that even though things occur simultaneously, due to the strength of kamma, having been distinctly ordered, they do not mix with each other, thus it abides (sayati), stands, proceeds; it is an abode (āsayo), the bile and so on, standing above the stomach. For this prefix 'ā' has the meaning of a boundary. Storage means the food remains having accumulated there; it is the storage place (nidhānaṃ), the stomach; thus, from the storage. Undigested refers to the food not yet processed by the digestive heat known as gahaṇī, generated by kamma. Digested means the food has undergone ripening. Resulting refers to completion. Oozing means oozing from here and there. Assimilating means complete assimilation. This is the summary here; the detailed explanation, however, should be taken from the commentary on the Path of Purification (Visuddhimagga Mahāṭīkā 1.294).

Sarīrato sedā muccantīti vegasandhāraṇena uppannapariḷāhato sarīrato sedā muccanti. Aññe ca rogā kaṇṇasūlabhagandarādayo. Aṭṭhāneti manussāmanussapariggahe ayuttaṭṭhāne khettadevāyatanādike. Kuddhā hi manussā amanussāpi vā jīvitakkhayaṃ pāpenti. Vissaṭṭhattā neva tassa [V 2.0171] bhikkhuno attano, kassaci anissajjitattā jigucchanīyattā ca na parassa, udakatumbatoti veḷunāḷiādiudakabhājanato. Tanti chaḍḍitaudakaṃ.

'From the body, sweat is released' means that due to the feverish heat arising from the suppression of urges, sweat is released from the body. Other ailments include earaches, fistulas, and so on. 'In an improper place' means in an unsuitable place within the domain of humans or non-humans, such as fields, shrines, and so on. For when angered, humans or even non-humans may bring about the loss of life. Because it has been discharged, it does not belong to that monk himself; and because it has not been given to anyone and is repulsive, it does not belong to another. Udakatumbato means from a water vessel like a bamboo tube and so on. That is, discarded water.

Ettha ca eko iriyāpatho dvīsu ṭhānesu āgato, so pubbe abhikkamapaṭikkamagahaṇena. “Gamanepi purato pacchato ca kāyassa abhiharaṇaṃ vuttanti idha gamanameva gahita”nti apare. Yasmā idha sampajaññakathāyaṃ asammohasampajaññameva dhuraṃ, tasmā antarantare iriyāpathe pavattānaṃ rūpārūpadhammānaṃ tattha tattheva nirodhadassanavasena sampajānakāritā gahitāti. Majjhimabhāṇakā pana evaṃ vadanti – eko hi bhikkhu gacchanto aññaṃ cintento, aññaṃ vitakkento gacchati, eko kammaṭṭhānaṃ avissajjetvāva gacchati, tathā eko tiṭṭhanto, nisīdanto, sayanto aññaṃ cintento, aññaṃ vitakkento sayati, eko kammaṭṭhānaṃ avissajjetvāva sayati. Ettakena pana na pākaṭaṃ hotīti caṅkamanena dīpenti. Yo bhikkhu caṅkamanaṃ otaritvā caṅkamanakoṭiyaṃ ṭhito pariggaṇhāti – “pācīnacaṅkamanakoṭiyaṃ pavattā rūpārūpadhammā pacchimacaṅkamanakoṭiṃ appatvā ettheva niruddhā, pacchimacaṅkamanakoṭiyaṃ pavattāpi pācīnacaṅkamanakoṭiṃ appatvā ettheva niruddhā, caṅkamanamajjhe pavattā ubho koṭiyo appatvā ettheva niruddhā, caṅkamane pavattā rūpārūpadhammā ṭhānaṃ appatvā ettheva niruddhā, ṭhāne pavattā nisajjaṃ appatvā ettheva niruddhā, nisajjāya pavattā sayanaṃ appatvā ettheva niruddhā”ti, evaṃ pariggaṇhanto pariggaṇhanto eva cittaṃ bhavaṅgaṃ otāreti, uṭṭhahanto pana kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva uṭṭhahati. Ayaṃ bhikkhu gatādīsu sampajānakārī nāma hotīti.

Here, one posture is included in two places; that was previously by taking 'going forward and going back.' Others say: "Even in going, the movement of the body forward and backward has been mentioned, but here only going is taken." Since here, in this discussion of clear comprehension, clear comprehension of non-delusion alone is the main point, therefore, being one who acts with clear comprehension is understood as seeing the cessation of material and immaterial phenomena arising intermittently in the postures, right there and then. The Majjhima reciters, however, say this: One monk walks while thinking of something else, pondering something else. Another walks without abandoning his meditation subject. Similarly, one may stand, sit, or lie down while thinking of something else, pondering something else. Another lies down without abandoning his meditation subject. But since this alone is not clear, they illustrate it with walking meditation. The monk who has begun walking meditation, standing at the end of the walking path, contemplates thus: "The material and immaterial phenomena occurring at the eastern end of the walking path cease right there without reaching the western end. Those occurring at the western end also cease right there without reaching the eastern end. Those occurring in the middle of the path cease right there without reaching either end. The material and immaterial phenomena occurring during walking cease right there without reaching standing. Those occurring during standing cease right there without reaching sitting. Those occurring during sitting cease right there without reaching lying down." Reflecting thus again and again, his mind sinks into the life-continuum. But when he rises, he rises only after taking up his meditation subject. Such a monk is called one who acts with clear comprehension in going and so forth.

Evampi [M 2.0468] sutte kammaṭṭhānaṃ avibhūtaṃ hoti, kammaṭṭhānaṃ avibhūtaṃ na kātabbaṃ, tasmā so bhikkhu yāva sakkoti, tāva caṅkamitvā ṭhatvā nisīditvā sayamāno evaṃ pariggahetvā sayati – “kāyo acetano mañco acetano, kāyo na jānāti ‘ahaṃ mañce sayito’ti, mañcopi na jānāti ‘mayi kāyo sayito’ti, acetano kāyo acetane mañce sayito”ti, evaṃ pariggaṇhanto eva cittaṃ bhavaṅgaṃ otāreti, pabujjhanto kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva pabujjhatīti ayaṃ sutte sampajānakārī nāma hoti. Kāyikādikiriyānibbattanena tammayattā āvajjanakiriyānibbattakattā āvajjanakiriyāsamuṭṭhitattā ca javanaṃ, sabbampi vā chadvārappavattaṃ kiriyāmayapavattaṃ nāma, tasmiṃ sati jāgaritaṃ nāma hotīti pariggaṇhanto jāgarite sampajānakārī nāma. Apica rattindivaṃ cha koṭṭhāse katvā pañca koṭṭhāse jaggantopi jāgarite sampajānakārī nāma hotīti.

Even so, while sleeping the meditation subject becomes indistinct. An indistinct meditation subject should not be worked on. Therefore, that monk, as long as he is able, having walked, stood, and sat, lies down reflecting thus: "The body is non-conscious, the bed is non-conscious. The body does not know, 'I am lying on the bed,' nor does the bed know, 'The body is lying on me.' The non-conscious body lies on the non-conscious bed." Reflecting in this way, he lets his mind sink into the life-continuum. Upon waking, he awakens only after taking up the meditation subject. This one is called one who acts with clear comprehension while sleeping. Impulsion—due to producing bodily and other actions, being composed of them, producing the action of adverting, and arising from the action of adverting—or indeed, all that occurs through the six doors, is called an 'occurrence consisting of action.' When that is present, it is called 'wakefulness.' Comprehending thus, one is called 'one who acts with clear comprehension in wakefulness.' Furthermore, even if one divides day and night into six portions and remains awake for five portions, one is still called one who acts with clear comprehension in wakefulness.

Vimuttāyatanasīse [V 2.0172] ṭhatvā dhammaṃ desentopi bāttiṃsatiracchānakathaṃ pahāya dasakathāvatthunissitasappāyakathaṃ kathentopi bhāsite sampajānakārī nāma hoti. Aṭṭhatiṃsāya ārammaṇesu cittaruciyaṃ ārammaṇaṃ manasikāraṃ pavattentopi dutiyajjhānaṃ samāpannopi tuṇhībhāve sampajānakārī nāma. Dutiyañhi jhānaṃ vacīsaṅkhārappahānato visesato tuṇhībhāvo nāmāti. Oṭṭhe cātiādīsu ca-saddena kaṇṭhasīsanābhiādīnaṃ saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Tadanurūpaṃ payoganti tassa uppattiyā anucchavikaṃ cittassa pavattiākārasaññitaṃ payogaṃ, yato sabbe vicārādayo nipphajjanti. Upādārūpapavattiyāti viññattivikārasahitasaddāyatanuppattiyā. Evanti vuttappakārena. Sattasupi ṭhānesu asammuyhanavasena “missaka”nti vuttaṃ. Maggasammāsatiyāpi kāyānupassanādianurūpattā sampajaññānurūpapubbabhāgaṃ sattaṭṭhāniyassa ekantalokiyattā.

Standing at the head of the sphere of liberation, even while teaching the Dhamma, abandoning the thirty-two kinds of animal talk and speaking beneficial talk based on the ten topics of discussion—one who does so is called 'one who acts with clear comprehension in speech.' Even when directing attention of mind to a pleasing object among the thirty-eight objects, or when attaining the second jhāna, one who remains silent is called 'one who acts with clear comprehension in silence.' For the second jhāna is specifically named 'silence' because of the abandoning of verbal formations. In phrases like 'with the lips, etc.,' by the word 'ca' (and), the inclusion of the throat, head, navel, and so on should be understood. 'The appropriate effort' means the application, characterized by the mind's mode of activity, which is suitable for its arising, from which all initial and sustained thoughts, etc., are produced. 'Regarding the manifestation of derived materiality' means by the arising of the sound base together with intimation and modification. 'Thus' means in the manner stated. In all seven instances, it is called 'mixed' on account of non-confusion. And in the case of the right mindfulness of the path, due to its conformity with the contemplation of the body and so on, the preliminary stage corresponding to clear comprehension of the sevenfold practice is entirely mundane.

Satisuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Sati Sutta is completed.

3. Bhikkhusuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Bhikkhu Sutta

369. Yasmā so bhikkhu “desetu me, bhante, bhagavā saṃkhittena dhamma”nti saṃkhittena dhammadesanaṃ yāci, tassa saṃkhittarucibhāvato, tasmā saṃkhitteneva [M 2.0469] dhammaṃ desetukāmo bhagavā “tasmātihā”tiādimāhāti vuttaṃ – “yasmā saṃkhittena desanaṃ yācasi, tasmā”ti. Kammassakatādiṭṭhikasseva lokiyalokuttaraguṇavisesā ijjhanti, na diṭṭhivipannassa, tasmā vuttaṃ “diṭṭhīti kammassakatādiṭṭhī”ti.

369. Because that monk requested, "Venerable sir, may the Blessed One teach me the Dhamma in brief," thus asking for a brief Dhamma teaching, and because of his liking for brevity, the Blessed One, wishing to teach the Dhamma concisely, said, "Therefore, here..." and so on. Thus it was said: "Because you ask for a brief teaching, therefore..." The distinctions of worldly and supramundane virtues are successfully realized only for one who has the view of ownership of kamma, not for one whose view is perverted. Therefore, it is said: "'View' means the view of ownership of kamma."

4. Sālasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Sāla Sutta

370. Yathānusiṭṭhaṃ paṭipajjamāne apāyadukkhe apātanavasena dhāraṇaṭṭhena dhammo, sāsanabrahmacariyaṃ, tadeva tadaṅgādivasena kilesānaṃ vinayanaṭṭhena vinayoti āha – “dhammoti vā…pe… nāma”nti. Paṭipakkhadhammehi anabhibhūtatāya eko udetīti ekodīti laddhanāmo samādhi bhūto jāto etesanti ekodibhūtā. Ettha ca ekodibhūtāti etena upacārajjhānāvaho pubbabhāgiko samādhi vutto. Samāhitāti etena upacārappanāsamādhi. Ekaggacittāti etena subhāvito vasippatto appanāsamādhi vuttoti veditabbo. Navakabhikkhūhi bhāvitasatipaṭṭhānā pubbabhāgā. Te hi yathābhūtañāṇāya bhāvitā. Yathābhūtañāṇanti hi sotāpattimaggañāṇaṃ idhādhippetaṃ. Khīṇāsavehi bhāvitasatipaṭṭhānāpi pubbabhāgā. Tesañhi katakaraṇīyānaṃ [V 2.0173] diṭṭhadhammasukhavihārāya satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā, sekkhānaṃ pana satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā pariññatthāya pavattā lokiyā, parijānanavasena pavattā lokuttarāti “missakā”ti vuttaṃ.

370. When one practices according to the instruction, the Dhamma—in the sense of upholding by preventing one from falling into the suffering of the lower realms—is the holy life of the Dispensation. That same holy life is called the Vinaya in the sense of removing defilements by way of its components and so on. Thus it is said: "Dhamma or... name." Because it arises alone, unvanquished by opposing states, concentration obtains the name 'ekodī.' 'Ekodibhūtā' (become one-pointed) means that for these, it has become, it has arisen. Here, by 'ekodibhūtā' is meant the preliminary concentration that leads to access jhāna. By 'samāhita' (composed) is meant access and attainment concentration. By 'ekaggacitta' (with a unified mind) should be understood the well-developed attainment concentration that has reached mastery. For new monks, the developed foundations of mindfulness are the preliminary stage. They are developed for the sake of knowledge of things as they are. 'Knowledge of things as they are' here is intended to mean the knowledge of the path of stream-entry. Even for those whose taints are destroyed, the developed foundations of mindfulness are the preliminary stage. For them, whose task is done, the development of the foundations of mindfulness is for dwelling happily in the present life. For trainees, however, the development of the foundations of mindfulness that proceeds for the sake of full understanding is mundane, while that which proceeds by way of complete understanding is supramundane; hence it is called 'mixed.'

5. Akusalarāsisuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Akusalarāsi Sutta

371. Pañcame kevaloti kusaladhammehi asammisso, tato eva sakalo sukkapakkho anavajjaṭṭho. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva.

371. In the fifth (sutta), 'kevala' means unmixed with unwholesome states; therefore, the entire bright side is blameless. The rest is as previously explained.

6. Sakuṇagghisuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Sakuṇagghi Sutta

372. Pākatikasakuṇakulesu balavabhāvato tesaṃ hananato sakuṇagghi. Senoti vuttaṃ “senassetaṃ adhivacana”nti, senaviseso pana so veditabbo. Āmisatthāya pattattā “lobhasāhasena pattā”ti vuttaṃ, lobhanimittena sāhasākārena pattāti attho. Naṅgalena kaṭṭhaṃ kasitaṃ naṅgalakaṭṭhaṃ, taṃ karīyati etthāti naṅgalakaṭṭhakaraṇanti [M 2.0470] āha – “adhunā kaṭṭhaṃ khettaṭṭhāna”nti. Leḍḍuyo tiṭṭhanti etthāti leḍḍuṭṭhānaṃ, leḍḍunimittaṃ kasitaṭṭhānaṃ. Vajjatīti vadaṃ, vacanaṃ. Kucchitaṃ vadaṃ avadaṃ. Garahane hi ayaṃ a-kāro yathā – “aputto abhariyā”ti. Avadaṃ mānetīti avadamānā, attano balamadena lāpaṃ atimaññitvā vadantīti attho. Tenevāha “gaccha kho, tvaṃ lāpa, tatrapi me gantvā na mokkhasī”ti. Yaṃ pana aṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ “attano balassa suṭṭhu vaṇṇaṃ vadamānā”ti, tattha attato āpannaṃ gahetvā vuttaṃ. “Vadamāno”ti vā pāṭho, sārambhavasena avhāyantoti attho. Tenāha “ehi kho dāni sakuṇagghī”ti. Suṭṭhu ṭhapetvāti jiyāmuttasarassa viya sīghapātayogyatākaraṇena ubho pakkhe sammā ṭhapetvā. Ayanti sakuṇagghi. Ñatvāti yathābhūtadassanena attano ñāṇena jānitvā. Phālīyitthāti phālacchedatikhiṇasikhare sukkhaleḍḍusmiṃ bhijjittha.

372. Because of its strength among ordinary bird families and because it kills them, it is called a 'bird-hawk' (sakuṇagghi). It is called a 'hawk' (sena), as in the phrase 'this is a term for a hawk,' but it should be understood as a specific type of hawk. Because it attacks for the sake of prey, it is said, 'They strike with greed and violence,' meaning they strike with the mark of greed and in a violent manner. A field plowed by a plow is 'plow-field' (naṅgalakaṭṭhaṃ). The place where that is done is 'plow-field-making' (naṅgalakaṭṭhakaraṇaṃ); thus it is said: 'now the plowed field is the field-place.' The place where clods stand is the 'clod-place' (leḍḍuṭṭhānaṃ), a plowed place marked by clods. 'Vadaṃ' means speech. Despicable speech is 'avadaṃ.' In reproach, this 'a-' prefix is used, as in 'sonless' (aputto) or 'wifeless' (abhariyā). 'Avadamānā' means one who esteems despicable speech; that is, they speak despising the quail's call due to the intoxication of their own strength. Therefore, it said: 'Go now, quail, even if you go there you will not escape me.' But what is said in the commentary, 'speaking well of one’s own strength,' is stated by taking what pertains to oneself. Alternatively, the reading is 'vadamāno,' meaning calling out with aggression. Hence, it says: 'Come now, bird-hawk.' 'Having positioned well' means having properly positioned both wings, making them suitable for a swift descent, like an arrow released from a bowstring. 'This' (ayaṃ) means this bird-hawk. 'Having known' means having known with one's own knowledge through seeing things as they are. 'Was shattered' (phālīyittha) means it was shattered on a dry clod, on the sharp point of a plowshare's cut.

7. Makkaṭasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Makkaṭa Sutta

373. Sañcāroti sañcaraṇaṃ. Lepanti silesasadisaṃ ālepanaṃ. Kājasikkā viyāti kājadaṇḍake olaggetabbā sikkā viya. Apica catasso rajjuyo upari bandhanaṭṭhānañcāti pañcaṭṭhānaṃ. Oḍḍitoti olambito. Thunantoti nitthunanto.

373. 'Sañcāro' means wandering. 'Lepaṃ' means a smear, a smearing like an adhesive substance. 'Kājasikkā viya' means like a net-bag to be hung on a carrying pole. Moreover, the four ropes and the binding place above make five places. 'Oḍḍito' means hanging down. 'Thunanto' means lamenting.

8. Sūdasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Sūda Sutta

374. Āhārasampādanena [V 2.0174] taṃtaṃāhāravatthugate sūdeti paggharetīti sūdo, bhattakārako. Daharehi manāpataraṃ accetabbato atikkamitabbato accayā, bhojane rasavisesā, tasmā nānaccayehīti nānappakārarasavisesehīti attho. Tena vuttaṃ “nānaccayehī”tiādi. Aggīyati asaṅkarato vibhajīyatīti aggo, ambilameva aggo ambilaggoti āha – “ambilaggehīti ambilakoṭṭhāsehī”ti. Dātuṃ abhiharitabbatāya abhihārā, deyyadhammā. Tenāha – “abhihaṭānaṃ dāyāna”nti. Idaṃ me kammaṭṭhānaṃ anulomaṃvāti idaṃ mama kammaṭṭhānaṃ evaṃ pavattamānaṃ anulomāvasānameva hutvā tiṭṭhati. Evaṃ puna pavattamānaṃ [M 2.0471] ussakkitvā visesanibbattanatthameva hotīti evaṃ nimittaṃ gahetuṃ na sakkoti bālo abyatto, paṇḍito pana sakkoti. Attano cittassāti attano bhāvanācittassa. Pubbabhāgavipassanā satipaṭṭhānāva kathitā “sakassa cittassa nimittaṃ uggaṇhātī”ti vuttattā.

374. A cook is called a 'sūda' because by preparing food he extracts the essence from the various food items; he is a meal-maker. 'Accayā' (specialties) are special flavors in food, because they are to be surpassed, to be exceeded, being more pleasing than what is for the young. Therefore, 'nānaccayehī' means 'with various kinds of special flavors.' Thus it is said: 'with various specialties,' etc. It is called 'agga' (foremost/part) because it is distinguished, separated without mixing. Sourness itself is the foremost part, hence 'ambilagga' (the sour part); thus it is said: 'ambilaggehi' means 'from the sour parts.' 'Abhihārā' are offerings, things to be given, because they are to be brought forward to be given. Hence, it is said: 'of the gifts that have been brought forth.' 'This meditation subject is conducive for me' means: 'My meditation subject, proceeding in this way, remains conducive right to its conclusion. When it continues thus, striving further, it serves only to produce a higher distinction.' A foolish, unskilled person cannot grasp such a sign, but a wise one can. 'Of his own mind' means of his own mind of meditation. The preliminary insight practice is itself called the foundations of mindfulness, because it is said: 'he grasps the sign of his own mind.'

9. Gilānasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Gilāna Sutta

375. Pādagāmoti nagarassa padasadiso mahantagāmo. Tenevāha “vesāliyaṃ viharati veḷuvagāmake”ti. Ahitanisedhana-hitaniyojana-byasanapariccajana-lakkhaṇo mittabhāvo yesu atthi, te mittā. Ye pana diṭṭhamattasahāyā, te sandiṭṭhā. Ye savisesaṃ bhattimanto, te sambhattāti dassento “mittāti mittāvā”tiādimāha. Assāti bhagavato. Pañcamiyaṃ aṭṭhamiyaṃ cātuddasiyaṃ pañcadasiyanti ekekasmiṃ pakkhe cattāro vāre katvā māsassa aṭṭhavāre.

375. A 'foot-village' (pādagāmo) is a large village similar to the foot of a city. Hence it is said, 'He dwells in the village of Veḷuva in Vesālī.' Those in whom there is friendship, which is characterized by the warding off of harm, the undertaking of benefit, and the forsaking of misfortune—they are friends. Those who are companions merely by sight are acquaintances. Showing that those who have special devotion are close companions, it is said, 'Friends indeed are friends...' 'His' (assa) refers to the Blessed One. On the fifth, eighth, fourteenth, and fifteenth—thus four times in each fortnight, making eight times a month.

Vedanānaṃ balavabhāvena kharo pharuso kakkhaḷo. Ābādhoti pubbakammahetutāya kammasamuṭṭhāno ābādho saṅkhāradukkhatāsaṅkhāto sabbakālikattā sarīrassa sabhāgarogo nāma. Nāyamīdiso ābādho, ayaṃ pana bahalatarabyādhitāya “visabhāgarogo”ti vutto. Anta-saddo samīpapavattoti āha – “maraṇantaṃ maraṇasantika”nti. Vedanā…pe… akaronto ukkaṃsagatabhāvitakāyāditāya. Apīḷiyamānoti apīḷiyamāno viya. Ovādameva bhikkhusaṅghassa apalokananti āha – “ovādānusāsaniṃ adatvāti vuttaṃ hotī”ti. Pubbabhāgavīriyaṃ nāma phalasamāpattiyā parikammabhūtavipassanāvīriyaṃ. Jīvitampi jīvitasaṅkhāro patituṃ adatvā attabhāvassa abhisaṅkharaṇato.

Due to the overpowering strength of the feelings, it is rough, coarse, and hard. An affliction (ābādho) is a disease inherent in the body (sabhāgarogo) because it is always present (sabbakālikattā), originated from kamma (kammasamuṭṭhāno) due to past kamma as its cause (pubbakammahetutāya), and designated as the suffering of conditioned things (saṅkhāradukkhatāsaṅkhāto). This affliction is not of that kind; rather, due to a very intense sickness, it is called 'a non-inherent disease' (visabhāgarogo). The word 'anta' (end) means occurring nearby; thus he says, 'ending in death, near to death.' Feelings...pe... not doing so—this is due to the highly developed state of the body, etc. 'Not being oppressed' means as if not being oppressed. Taking leave of the community of monks is itself advice; this is said to mean 'without giving advice and instruction.' 'Preliminary effort' means the effort of insight meditation, which is preparatory for the attainment of fruition. Even life, the life-formation, not allowing it to fall, due to the fashioning of one's individual existence.

“Ettakaṃ [V 2.0175] kālaṃ atikkamitvā vuṭṭhahissāmī”ti khaṇaparicchedavatī samāpatti khaṇikasamāpatti. Niggumbaṃ nijjaṭaṃ katvāti rūpasattakārūpasattakavasena pavattiyamānaṃ vipassanābhāvanaṃ sabbaso khilavirahena niggumbaṃ, abyākulatāya nijjaṭaṃ katvā. Mahāvipassanāvasenāti paccekaṃ savisesaṃ vitthāritānaṃ aṭṭhārasādīnaṃ mahāvipassanānaṃ vasena vipassitvā samāpannā yā samāpatti, sā suṭṭhu vikkhambheti vedanaṃ mahābalavatāya pubbārammaṇassa, mahānubhāvatāya tathāpavattitavipassanāvīriyassa. Yathā [M 2.0472] nāmātiādinā tassa nidassanaṃ dasseti. Vedanāti dukkhavedanā. Cuddasahākārehīti tasseva sattakadvayassa vasena vadati. Sannetvāti antarantarā samāpannajjhānasamāpattisambhūtena vipassanāpītisinehena temetvā. Samāpattīti phalasamāpatti.

'After passing this much time, I will emerge'—this is an attainment with a defined time limit, a momentary attainment. 'Having made it free from obstruction and entanglement' means having made the insight meditation—which proceeds by way of the sevenfold of form and the sevenfold of the formless—entirely free from obstructions, due to the absence of mental barrenness, and free from entanglement, due to being unconfused. 'By means of great insight' means the attainment that is entered into after having contemplated by means of the eighteen great insights and so forth, each distinct and detailed. That attainment thoroughly suppresses feeling due to the great power of the preceding object and the great influence of the energy of insight that has thus occurred. By 'Just as...' and so forth, he shows an illustration of this. 'Feeling' means painful feeling. 'With fourteen aspects' refers to that same pair of sevenfolds. 'Having moistened' means having repeatedly saturated it with the moisture of insight-joy born of the jhāna-attainment entered into from time to time. 'Attainment' means fruition attainment.

Gilānā vuṭṭhitoti gilānabhāvato vuṭṭhito. Sarīrassa garuthaddhabhāvappatti madhurakatāti āha – “sañjātagarubhāvo sañjātathaddhabhāvo”ti. Nānākāratoti puratthimādibhedato. Satipaṭṭhānadhammāti pubbe attanā bhāviyamānā satipaṭṭhānadhammā. Pākaṭā na honti kāyacittānaṃ akammaññatāya. Tanti dhammāti pariyattidhammā na ñāyanti.

'Risen from illness' means risen from the state of being ill. The body's attainment of heaviness and stiffness is 'madhurakatā'; thus he says, 'a state of heaviness has arisen, a state of stiffness has arisen.' 'In various ways' means by divisions such as east, and so on. 'The principles of the foundations of mindfulness' are the principles of the foundations of mindfulness previously cultivated by oneself. They do not become manifest due to the unworkability of body and mind. 'Those texts' means the teachings of the scriptures are not clear.

Anantaraṃ abāhiranti dhammavasena puggalavasena ca antarabāhiraṃ akatvā. Ettakantiādinā vuttamevatthaṃ vivarati. Daharakāleti attano daharakāle. Na evaṃ hotīti “ahaṃ bhikkhusaṅghaṃ pariharissāmī”tiādiko mānataṇhāmūlako issāmacchariyānaṃ pavattiākāro tathāgatassa na hoti, nattheva pageva tesaṃ samucchinnattāti āha – “bodhipallaṅkeyevā”tiādi. Paṭisaṅkharaṇena veṭhena missakena. Maññeti yathāvuttaṃ paṭisaṅkharaṇasaññitena veṭhamissakena viya jarasakaṭaṃ. Arahattaphalaveṭhenāti arahattaphalasamāpattisaññitena atthabhāvaveṭhena.

'Without inner or outer' means not making a distinction between inner and outer with respect to phenomena or persons. By 'This much,' and so on, he explains the meaning already stated. 'In youth' means in one's own youth. 'It is not so' means that for the Tathāgata, there is no such mode of occurrence of jealousy and stinginess rooted in conceit and craving, as in thinking, 'I will lead the community of monks,' etc. Indeed, they do not exist at all, how much less arise, since they were eradicated at the very seat of enlightenment—thus he says, 'at the seat of enlightenment,' etc. With a mixed wrapping for repair. Just as an old cart is maintained by what is called a mixed wrapping for repair, as described. 'With the wrapping of the fruit of arahantship' means with the wrapping of one's individual existence, which is designated as the attainment of the fruition of arahantship.

Phalasamāpattiyā adhippetattā “ekaccānaṃ vedanānanti lokiyānaṃ vedanāna”nti vuttaṃ. Attadīpāti ettha atta-saddena dhammo eva vutto, svāyamattho heṭṭhā vibhāvito eva. Navavidho lokuttaradhammo veditabbo. So hi catūhi oghehi anajjhottharaṇīyato “dīpo”ti vutto. Tamaaggeti tamayogābhāvena sadevakassa lokassa agge. Sabbesanti sabbesaṃ sikkhākāmānaṃ. Te “dhammadīpā viharathā”ti vuttā catusatipaṭṭhānagocarāva bhikkhū agge bhavissanti.

Because fruition-attainment is intended, it is said, 'of some feelings,' meaning of worldly feelings. Here, in 'an island for oneself' (attadīpā), by the word 'self' (atta), the Dhamma itself is meant; this meaning has already been explained below. The ninefold supramundane Dhamma is to be understood. It is called an 'island' (dīpa) because it cannot be overwhelmed by the four floods. 'Foremost over darkness' (tamaagge) means at the forefront of the world with its gods, due to the absence of connection with darkness. 'Of all' means of all who desire the training. Those monks who are told, 'Dwell with the Dhamma as an island,' and whose pasture is the four foundations of mindfulness, will be foremost.

10. Bhikkhunupassayasuttavaṇṇanā

10. The Commentary on the Discourse at the Nuns' Quarters

376. Kammaṭṭhānakammikāti [V 2.0176] kammaṭṭhānamanuyuttā. Visesetīti viseso, atisayo, svāyaṃ pubbāparaviseso upādāyupādāya gahetabboti taṃ dassetuṃ “tatthā”tiādimāha.

376. 'A practitioner of a meditation subject' means one devoted to a meditation subject. 'He distinguishes' means a distinction, an excellence. To show that this distinction between preceding and succeeding states should be grasped by taking them up again and again, he said, 'Therein...' and so on.

Uppajjati [M 2.0473] kilesapariḷāhoti kāye asubhādivasena manasikāraṃ adahantassa manasikārassa vīthiyaṃ apaṭipannatā subhādivasena kāyārammaṇo kilesapariḷāho ca uppajjati. Vīriyārambhassa abhāvena tasmiṃ ārammaṇe cetaso vā līnattaṃ hoti, gocarajjhattato bahiddhā puthuttārammaṇe cittaṃ vikkhipati. Evaṃ kilesapariḷāhe cātiādinā tividhampi bhāvanānuyogassa kilesavatthubhāvaṃ upādāya samuccayavasena aṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ. Yasmā pana te pariḷāhalīnattavikkhepā ekajjhaṃ na pavattanti, tasmā pāḷiyaṃ “kāyārammaṇo vā”tiādinā aniyamattho vā-saddo gahito. Kilesānurañjitenāti kilesavivaṇṇitacittena hutvā na vattitabbaṃ. Kathaṃ pana vattitabbanti āha “kismiñcidevā”ti. Na ca vitakketi na ca vicāretīti kilesasahagate vitakkavicāre na pavatteti. Sukhitoti jhānasukhena sukhito.

'The heat of defilement arises' means that for one who does not apply attention to the body in terms of unattractiveness and so forth, because one has not entered the path of attention, the heat of defilement arises with the body as its object in terms of attractiveness and so forth. Due to a lack of initiated energy, either the mind becomes sluggish with regard to that object, or it scatters the mind towards various external objects outside its own domain. Thus, by 'the heat of defilement, etc.,' the commentary speaks collectively of the threefold basis for defilements in the practice of meditation. But since that heat, sluggishness, and distraction do not occur all at once, the word 'or' (vā) is used in the Pāli text in the sense of an alternative, as in 'with the body as its object, or...'. 'Colored by defilements' means one should not act with a mind discolored by defilements. But how should one act? He says, 'in some way or another.' 'He neither applies thought nor sustains thought' means he does not engage in applied and sustained thought associated with defilements. 'Happy' means happy with the bliss of jhāna.

Imassa bhikkhuno bhāvanā pavattāti sambandho. Yasmā hi imassa bhikkhuno taṃ mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ paripanthe sati ṭhapetvā buddhaguṇādianussaraṇena cittaṃ pasādetvā mūlakammaṭṭhānabhāvanā pavattā, tasmā paṇidhāya bhāvanāti vuttanti sambandho. Aṭṭhapetvāti cittaṃ appavattetvāti attho veditabbo. Kammaṭṭhānādīnaṃ tiṇṇampi vasena atthayojanā sambhavati. Tenāha “tatthā”tiādi. Sārento viyāti rathaṃ vāhayanto viya. Samappamāṇato aṭṭhakādivasena sutacchitaṃ pakkhipanto viya. Sukheneva kilesānaṃ okāsaṃ adento antarā asajjanto alagganto. Vipassanācārassa āraddhavuttitaṃ aparipanthatañca dassento opammadvayamāha. Byābhaṅgiyāti kājadaṇḍena. Kilesapariḷāhādīnanti kilesapariḷāhalīnattavikkhepānaṃ.

'This monk's meditation proceeds'—this is the connection. For when an obstacle to this monk's basic meditation subject arises, having set that aside and gladdened his mind by recollecting the qualities of the Buddha and so forth, the meditation on the basic meditation subject proceeds. Therefore, it is called 'meditation with resolve'—this is the connection. 'Not establishing' should be understood to mean not allowing the mind to proceed. An application of the meaning is possible with regard to all three: the meditation subject, and so on. Therefore, he said, 'Therein...' and so on. 'Like one guiding' is like one driving a chariot. 'Inserting with precise measurement' is like inserting a well-planed piece into a joint, and so on. Easily, without giving an opportunity to the defilements, not clinging or getting stuck in between. Showing that the practice of insight is continuous and unobstructed, he gives two similes. 'With a splint' means with a piece of a carrying pole. 'Of the heat of defilements, etc.' refers to the heat of defilements, sluggishness, and distraction.

Guḷakhaṇḍādīnīti guḷakhaṇḍasakkharakhaṇḍādīni ucchuvikārabhūtāni. “Kāye kāyānupassī viharāmī”tiādivacanato “pubbabhāgavipassanā kathitā”ti vuttaṃ.

'Lumps of jaggery, etc.' means lumps of jaggery, pieces of sugar, and so on, which are products of sugarcane. From the statement, 'I dwell contemplating the body in the body,' etc., it is said that 'the preliminary stage of insight is spoken of.'

Ambapālivaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on Ambapālī is concluded.

2. Nālandavaggo

2. The Chapter on Nālandā

2. Nālandasuttavaṇṇanā

2. The Commentary on the Discourse at Nālandā

378. Dutiyavagge [M 2.0474][V 2.0177] paṭhamasuttaṃ heṭṭhā vuttanayattā suviññeyyanti taṃ laṅghitvā “dutiye”ti vuttaṃ.

378. The first discourse in the second chapter is easy to understand, as it follows the method explained below; therefore, having skipped it, 'the second' is stated.

Bhiyyataro abhiññātoti sambodhiyā seṭṭhataroti abhilakkhito. Bhiyyatarābhiññoti sabbasattesu adhikatarapañño. Tenāha “uttaritarañāṇo”ti. Sammā anavasesato bujjhati etenāti sambodhīti āha – “sambodhiyanti sabbaññutaññāṇe”ti. Nippadesā gahitāti anavasesā buddhaguṇā gahitā aggamaggasiddhiyāva bhagavato sabbaguṇānaṃ siddhattā. Na kevalañca buddhānaṃ, atha kho aggasāvakapaccekabuddhānampi taṃtaṃguṇasamijjhanaṃ aggamaggādhigamenevāti dassento “dvepi aggasāvakā”tiādimāha.

“Bhiyyataro abhiññāto” means he is recognized as superior through enlightenment. “Bhiyyatarābhiñño” refers to his wisdom surpassing all beings. Hence it is said, “possessing superior knowledge.” ‘Through this one awakens rightly and without remainder,’ thus it is enlightenment. It is stated: “enlightenment means omniscient knowledge.” “The qualities are grasped without a remainder”—this is because all the Buddha’s qualities are perfected with the attainment of the supreme path. And not only for the Buddhas, but also for the chief disciples and Paccekabuddhas, the fulfillment of their respective qualities is by the attainment of the supreme path. To illustrate this, it is said: “the two chief disciples,” and so on.

Aparo nayo – pasannoti iminā pasādassa vattamānatā dīpitāti uppannasaddhoti imināpi saddhāya paccuppannatā pakāsitāti āha – “evaṃ saddahāmīti attho”ti. Abhijānāti abhimukhabhāvena sabbaññeyyaṃ jānātīti abhiñño, bhiyyo adhiko abhiññoti bhiyyobhiñño. So eva atisayavacanicchāvasena bhiyyobhiññataroti vuttoti āha – “bhiyyataro abhiññāto”ti. Dutiyavikappe pana abhijānātīti abhiññā, abhivisiṭṭhā paññā. Bhiyyo abhiññā etassāti bhiyyobhiñño, so eva atisayavacanicchāvasena bhiyyobhiññataro. Svāyaṃ assa atisayo abhiññāya bhiyyobhāvakatoti āha – “bhiyyatarābhiñño vā”ti. Sambujjhati etāyāti sambodhi, sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ aggamaggañāṇañca. Sabbaññutaññāṇapadaṭṭhānañhi aggamaggañāṇaṃ, aggamaggañāṇapadaṭṭhānañca sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ sambodhi nāma. Tattha padhānavasena tadatthadassane paṭhamavikappo, padaṭṭhānavasena dutiyavikappo. Kasmā panettha arahattamaggañāṇasseva gahaṇaṃ, nanu heṭṭhimānipi bhagavato maggañāṇāni savāsanameva yathāsakaṃ paṭipakkhavidhamanavasena pavattāni. Savāsanappahānañhi ñeyyāvaraṇappahānanti? Saccametaṃ, taṃ pana aparipuṇṇaṃ paṭipakkhavidhamanassa vippakatabhāvatoti āha “arahattamaggañāṇe vā”ti. Sabbanti sammāsambuddhena adhigantabbaṃ sabbaṃ.

Another method: By “pleased” (pasanno) the present state of faith (pasāda) is indicated; thus, by “one in whom faith has arisen” (uppannasaddho) the present state of faith is also revealed. Therefore, it is said, “the meaning is: thus I believe.” “One who knows all that is to be known directly knows thoroughly”; thus, one with direct knowledge is called abhiñño. One with more, superior direct knowledge is bhiyyobhiñño. That very one, out of a desire to express pre-eminence, is called bhiyyobhiññataro. Therefore, it is said, “bhiyyataro abhiññāto.” In the second interpretation, “knows thoroughly” refers to abhiññā, which is highly distinguished wisdom. One who has more abhiññā is bhiyyobhiñño. That very one, out of a desire to express pre-eminence, is bhiyyobhiññataro. This pre-eminence of his is due to the abundance of direct knowledge. Therefore, it is said, “or bhiyyatarābhiñño.” ‘By this one awakens fully,’ thus it is perfect enlightenment (sambodhi), which is omniscient knowledge and the knowledge of the supreme path. For the knowledge of the supreme path is the basis of omniscient knowledge, and omniscient knowledge has the knowledge of the supreme path as its basis; this is called perfect enlightenment. Here, the first interpretation shows that meaning by way of prominence, the second interpretation by way of basis. But why is only the knowledge of the Arahant path mentioned here? Did not the Blessed One’s earlier path-knowledges also operate by dispelling their respective opposing factors along with their latent tendencies? For the abandonment of latent tendencies is the abandonment of the obstruction to knowledge. This is true, but that was incomplete because the dispelling of opposing factors was unfinished; therefore, it is said: “or in the knowledge of the Arahant path.” “All” means everything to be fully realized by the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Khādanīyānaṃ [M 2.0475][V 2.0178] uḷāratā sātarasānubhāvenāti āha “madhure āgacchatī”ti. Pasaṃsāya uḷāratā visiṭṭhabhāvenāti āha – “seṭṭhe”ti. Obhāsassa uḷāratā mahantabhāvenāti vuttaṃ – “vipule”ti. Usabhassa ayanti āsabhī, idha pana āsabhī viyāti āsabhī. Tenāha – “usabhassa vācāsadisī”ti. Yena guṇena sā taṃsadisā, taṃ dassetuṃ “acalā asampavedhī”ti vuttaṃ. Yato kutoci anussavanaṃ anussavo. Vijjāṭṭhānādīsu kataparicayānaṃ ācariyānaṃ taṃ taṃ atthaṃ ñāpentī paveṇī ācariyaparamparā. Kevalaṃ attano matiyā “iti kira evaṃ kirā”ti parikappanā itikirā. Piṭakassa ganthassa sampadānato bhūtato tassa gahaṇaṃ piṭakasampadānaṃ. Yathāsutānaṃ attānaṃ ākārassa parivitakkanaṃ ākāraparivitakko. Tathevassa “evameta”nti diṭṭhiyā nijjhānakkhamanaṃ diṭṭhinijjhānakkhanti. Āgamādhigamehi vinā kevalaṃ anussutato takkamaggaṃ nissāya takkanaṃ takko. Anumānavidhiṃ nissāya gahaṇaṃ nayaggāho. Yasmā buddhavisaye ṭhatvā bhagavato ayaṃ therassa codanā therassa ca so avisayo, tasmā “paccakkhato ñāṇena paṭivijjhitvā viyā”ti vuttaṃ. Sīhanādo viyāti sīhanādo. Taṃsadisatā cassa seṭṭhabhāvena, so cettha evaṃ veditabboti dassento “sīhanādo”tiādimāha. Unnādayantenāti asanisadisaṃ karontena.

The excellence of edibles is due to their delightful taste, hence it is said, “comes sweetly.” The excellence in praise is due to its distinguished nature, hence it is said, “foremost.” The excellence of radiance is due to its greatness, hence it is stated, “vast.” The word “āsabhī” means “belonging to a bull”; here, however, it is used in the sense of “like a bull.” Therefore, it is said, “like the speech of a bull.” The quality by which it resembles that is shown by the words, “unshaken, unperturbed.” Hearing from any source whatsoever is “hearsay” (anussavana). The lineage of teachers, experienced in the fields of knowledge and so on, who make known the respective meanings, is “teacher-succession” (ācariyaparamparā). Mere speculation based on one’s own opinion, such as “so it is said, thus it is said,” is “report” (itikirā). Grasping the text of the Piṭaka truly according to its transmission is “transmission of the Piṭaka” (piṭakasampadāna). Reflecting on the aspects of things as they have been heard is “reflection on aspects” (ākāraparivitakka). Similarly, the acceptance of a view through patient consideration that “it is so” is “acceptance of a view after pondering” (diṭṭhinijjhānakkhanti). Reasoning based on the path of logic, merely from what has been heard, without reliance on scripture or realization, is “reasoning” (takka). Grasping through the method of inference is “grasping by method” (nayaggāha). Because this admonition of the elder to the Blessed One stands within the Buddha’s domain, and that is not the elder’s domain, therefore it is said, “as if having penetrated it with direct knowledge.” Like a lion’s roar is “sīhanādo.” Its similarity is in its excellence, and it should be understood thus here; to show this, it is said, “sīhanādo,” and so on. “By one who causes to resound” means by one making a sound like thunder.

Anuyogadāpanatthanti anuyogaṃ sodhāpetuṃ. Vimaddakkhamañhi sīhanādaṃ nadanto atthato anuyogaṃ sodheti nāma, anuyujjhanto ca naṃ sodhāpeti nāma. Dātunti sodhetuṃ. Keci “dānattha”nti atthaṃ vadanti, taṃ na yuttaṃ. Na hi yo sīhadānaṃ nadati, so eva tattha anuyogaṃ detīti yujjati. Nighaṃsananti vimaddanaṃ. Dhamamānanti tāpayamānaṃ. Tāpanañcettha gaggariyā dhammāpanasīsena vadati.

“For the purpose of having the challenge given” means to have the challenge examined. For one who roars a lion’s roar, being capable of crushing, indeed examines the challenge in substance; and one who cross-examines has it examined. “To give” means to examine. Some say the meaning is “for the purpose of giving,” but that is not fitting. For it is not fitting that the one who roars the lion’s roar is the very one who gives the challenge there. “Nighaṃsana” means crushing. “Dhamamāna” means heating. And here, “heating” is spoken of in the sense of heating a water pot.

Sabbe teti sabbe te atīte niruddhe sammāsambuddhe. Tenetaṃ dasseti – ye te ahesuṃ atītamaddhānaṃ tava abhinīhārato oraṃ sammāsambuddhā, tesaṃ tāva sāvakañāṇagocare dhamme paricchindanto mārādayo viya ca buddhānaṃ lokiyacittācāraṃ tvaṃ jāneyyāsi. Ye pana te abbhatītā, tato parato chinnavaṭumā chinnapapañcā pariyādinnavaṭṭā sabbadukkhavītivattā sammāsambuddhā, tesaṃ sabbesampi tava sāvakañāṇassa [M 2.0476] avisayabhūte dhamme kathaṃ jānissasīti. Tenāti sambodhisaṅkhātena sabbaññutaññāṇapadaṭṭhānena arahattamaggañāṇena. Evaṃsīlāti tādisasīlā. Samādhipakkhāti samādhi ca samādhipakkhā ca samādhipakkhā ekadesasarūpekasesanayena. Tattha samādhipakkhāti vīriyasatiyo tadanuguṇā ca dhammā veditabbā[V 2.0179]. Jhānasamāpattīsu yebhuyyena vihāravohāro, jhānasamāpattiyo samādhippadhānāti vuttaṃ “samādhipakkhānaṃ dhammānaṃ gahitattā vihāro gahito”ti.

“All those” means all those past, ceased Perfectly Self-Enlightened Ones. This shows the following: As for those Perfectly Self-Enlightened Ones who existed in the past, on this side of your aspiration, you might know their worldly mental conduct, like Māra and others, while defining the Dhamma within the scope of a disciple’s knowledge. But as for those who have passed beyond that, who have severed the cycle, cut off proliferation, exhausted the round, and transcended all suffering—those Perfectly Self-Enlightened Ones—how could you know the Dhamma that is beyond the range of your disciple’s knowledge, which belongs to all of them? “By that” means by the knowledge of the Arahant path, which is the basis for omniscient knowledge and is designated as enlightenment. “Endowed with such virtue” means those endowed with that kind of virtue. “Factors of concentration” means concentration and the factors of concentration, becoming “factors of concentration” by the principle of ellipsis where one part represents the whole. Therein, “factors of concentration” should be understood as energy, mindfulness, and the qualities that accord with them. The term “dwelling” is mostly used for the jhānas and attainments; since the jhānas and attainments are predominantly about concentration, it is said: “Because the factors of concentration are grasped, the dwelling is grasped.”

Yathā pana heṭṭhā gahitāpi samādhipaññā paṭipakkhato vimuttattā vimuccana-saṅkhāta-kiccavisesa-dassanavasena vimuttipariyāyena puna gahitā “evaṃvimuttā”ti, evaṃ dibbavihāro dibbavihāravisesadassanavasena puna gahito “evaṃvihārī”ti, tasmā sabbesaṃ samāpattivihārānaṃ vasenettha attho yujjatīti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Vimuttīti saṅkhaṃ gacchanti vimuccitthāti katvā. Esa nayo sesesupi. Paṭippassaddhanteti paṭippassambhanosāpanena. Sabbakilesehi nissaṭattā asaṃsaṭṭhattā vimuttattā ca nissaraṇavimutti nibbānaṃ.

Just as concentration and wisdom, though already mentioned, are taken up again as “thus liberated” by way of showing the specific function designated as liberation, because they are freed from opposing factors; so too the divine abiding is taken up again as “abiding thus” by way of showing the special quality of the divine abiding. Therefore, it should be understood that the meaning here is fitting in the context of all abidings in meditative attainments. It is designated as “liberation” because one has been liberated. This is the method for the remaining cases as well. “Having been pacified” means by way of pacifying and bringing to an end. Because it is an escape from all defilements, unmingled with them, and a liberation from them, Nibbāna is the liberation of escape.

Anāgatabuddhānaṃ panāti pana-saddo visesatthajotano. Tena atītesu tāva khandhānaṃ bhūtapubbattā tattha siyā ñāṇassa savisaye gati, anāgatesu pana sabbaso asañjātesu kathanti imamatthaṃ joteti. Tenāha “anāgatāpī”tiādi. “Attano cetasā paricchinditvā viditā”ti kasmā vuttaṃ? Nanu atītānāgate sattāhe eva pavattaṃ cittaṃ cetopariyañāṇassa visayo, na tato paranti? Nayidaṃ cetopariyañāṇakiccavasena vuttaṃ, atha kho pubbenivāsaanāgataṃsañāṇānaṃ vasena vuttaṃ, tasmā nāyaṃ doso. Viditaṭṭhāne na karoti sikkhāpadeneva tādisassa paṭikkhepassa paṭikkhittattā setughātato ca. Kathaṃ pana thero dvayasambhave paṭikkhepameva akāsi, na vibhajja byākāsīti āha “thero kirā”tiādi. Pāraṃ pariyantaṃ minotīti pāramī, sā eva ñāṇanti pāramiñāṇaṃ, sāvakānaṃ pāramiñāṇaṃ sāvakapāramiñāṇaṃ. Tasmiṃ sāvakānaṃ ukkaṃsapariyantagate jānane nāyamanuyogo, atha kho sabbaññutaññāṇe sabbaññutāya jānane. Keci pana “sāvakapāramiñāṇeti sāvakapāramiñāṇavisaye”ti atthaṃ vadanti, tathā sesapadesupi. Sīla…pe… samatthanti sīlasamādhipaññāvimuttiñāṇasaṅkhātānaṃ kāraṇānaṃ [M 2.0477] jānanasamatthaṃ. Buddhasīlādayo hi buddhānaṃ buddhakiccassa parehi ete buddhāti jānanassa ca kāraṇaṃ.

“And regarding the future Buddhas”—the word “pana” here serves to illuminate a special meaning. Thus, it first indicates that since the past aggregates have already existed, knowledge may operate within its own domain there. However, concerning the future, since they have not yet arisen at all, how could it? Thus, this meaning is illuminated. Hence, it is said, “even in the future,” and so on. “Why is it stated, ‘known by delimiting with one’s own mind’?” you might ask. Isn’t it the case that in the past and future, the mind operates only for seven days, being the scope of mind-penetrating knowledge, and not beyond that? This was not stated in terms of the function of mind-penetrating knowledge but rather in terms of the knowledge of past lives and knowledge of the future. Therefore, there is no fault here. In the case of what is already known, he does not act, because such a prohibition has already been established by way of the training rules, and it would be like breaking a dam. But how did the Elder make a blanket prohibition regarding the two possibilities without analyzing and explaining? It is said, “the Elder, indeed,” and so on. “Measures the ultimate limit”—this is perfection (pāramī); that very knowledge is the knowledge of perfection (pāramiñāṇa). The knowledge of perfection for disciples is the knowledge of disciples’ perfection (sāvakapāramiñāṇa). In this case, the application is not in the knowledge of disciples reaching the highest limit of excellence, but rather in the knowledge of omniscience, knowing through omniscience. Some, however, explain the meaning as, “the knowledge of disciples’ perfection refers to the domain of disciples’ perfection,” and similarly for the remaining terms. “Virtue… able” means able to know the causes designated as virtue, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and knowledge. For the virtues of the Buddhas and so forth are the causes for the Buddhas’ activities and for others recognizing them as Buddhas.

Anumānañāṇaṃ viya saṃsayaṭṭhitaṃ ahutvā idamidanti yathāsabhāvato ñeyyaṃ dhāreti nicchinotīti dhammo, paccakkhañāṇanti āha “dhammassa paccakkhato ñāṇassā”ti. Anuetīti anvayoti āha “anuyogaṃ anugantvā”ti. Paccakkhasiddhañhi atthaṃ anugantvā anumānañāṇassa pavatti “diṭṭhena adiṭṭhassa anumāna”nti veditabbā. Vidite vedakampi ñāṇaṃ atthato viditameva hotīti “anumānañāṇaṃ nayaggāho vidito”ti vuttaṃ. Viditoti viddho [V 2.0180] paṭiladdho, adhigatoti attho. Appamāṇoti aparimāṇo mahāvisayattā. Tenāha “apariyanto”ti. Tenāti apariyantattā. Tena vā apariyantena ñāṇena. Etena thero yaṃ yaṃ anumeyyamatthaṃ ñātukāmo hoti, tattha tattha tassa asaṅgamappaṭihataṃ anumānañāṇaṃ pavattetīti dasseti. Tenāha “so iminā”tiādi. Tattha imināti iminā kāraṇena.

Just as inferential knowledge, not being established in doubt, holds and determines what is to be known according to its true nature as “this is this”—this is called Dhamma, which is direct knowledge, as it is said: “of the knowledge of the Dhamma through direct experience.” It follows, thus it is called inference, as it is said: “having followed the method of inquiry.” For by following the meaning established through direct experience, the arising of inferential knowledge is to be understood as “inference of the unseen by the seen.” When the object is known, the knowing knowledge too is in meaning itself known—thus it is said: “inferential knowledge, the grasp of the method, is known.” “Known” means penetrated, obtained, attained—this is the meaning. “Immeasurable” means boundless due to the vastness of its scope. Therefore, it is said: “without limit.” “Therefore”—because of being limitless. Or, by that limitless knowledge. By this, the Elder shows that wherever he wishes to know any inferable meaning, there his inferential knowledge proceeds unimpeded and unobstructed. Therefore, it is said: “he by this means,” and so on. Here, “by this” means by this cause.

Pākārassa thirabhāvaṃ uddhamuddhaṃ āpetīti uddhāpaṃ, pākāramūlaṃ. Ādi-saddena pākāradvārabandhaparikhādīnaṃ saṅgaho veditabbo. Paccante bhavaṃ paccantimaṃ. Paṇḍitadovārikaṭṭhāniyaṃ katvā thero attānaṃ dassetīti dassento “ekadvāranti kasmā āhā”ti codanaṃ samuṭṭhāpesi.

That which causes the stability of the wall to extend upwards and upwards is called the foundation (uddhāpa), the base of the wall. By the word “etc.,” the inclusion of wall-gates, door-fastenings, moats, and the like should be understood. What exists at the border is called bordering (paccantima). Having made himself like a wise gatekeeper, the Elder, displaying himself, raised the question, “Why say ‘one gate’?”

Yassā paññāya vasena puriso paṇḍitoti vuccati, taṃ paṇḍiccanti āha – “paṇḍiccena samannāgato”ti. Taṃtaṃitikattabbatāsu chekabhāvo byattabhāvo veyyattiyaṃ. Medhati aññāṇaṃ hiṃsati vidhamatīti medhā, sā etassa atthīti medhāvī. Ṭhāne ṭhāne uppatti etissā atthīti ṭhānuppattikā, ṭhānaso uppajjanapaññā. Anupariyāyanti etenāti anupariyāyo, so eva pathoti anupariyāyapatho, parito pākārassa anuyāyanamaggo. Pākārabhāgā sambandhitabbā etthāti pākārasandhi, pākārassa phullitapadeso. So pana heṭṭhimantena dvinnampi iṭṭhakānaṃ vigamena evaṃ vuccatīti āha – “dvinnaṃ iṭṭhakānaṃ apagataṭṭhāna”nti. Chinnaṭṭhānanti chinnabhinnapadesaṃ, chinnaṭṭhānaṃ vā. Tañhi “vivara”nti vuccati. Kīliṭṭhanti malīnaṃ. Upatāpentīti kilesapariḷāhena santāpenti. Vibādhentīti pīḷenti. Uppannāya paññāya nīvaraṇehi na kiñci kātuṃ sakkāti āha “anuppannāya paññāya uppajjituṃ na dentī”ti. Tasmāti paccayūpaghātena [M 2.0478] uppajjituṃ appadānato. Catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu suṭṭhu ṭhapitacittāti catubbidhāyapi satipaṭṭhānabhāvanāya sammadeva ṭhapitacittā appitacittā. Yathāsabhāvena bhāvetvāti yāthāvato sammadeva yathā paṭipakkhā samucchijjanti, evaṃ bhāvetvā.

A person is called wise due to wisdom; that is called sagacity, thus it is said: “endowed with sagacity.” Skillfulness, cleverness, and expertise in various duties are called expertise. “Medhā” harms ignorance, dispels it, thus it is intelligence. One who possesses it is intelligent (medhāvī). “Ṭhānuppattikā” means having arising in place after place, wisdom that arises according to the situation (ṭhānaso uppajjanapaññā). “Anupariyāyo” is that by which one goes around; that itself is the path, the circumambulation path around a wall. “Pākārasandhi” is where the parts of the wall are to be joined, a cracked place in the wall. It is so called because at its lower end, due to the removal of two bricks, it is said to be “the place where two bricks are missing.” “Chinnaṭṭhāna” means a cut and broken place, or a broken place. That indeed is called a “crack” (vivara). “Kīliṭṭha” means stained, defiled. “Upatāpenti” means they torment with the fever of defilements. “Vibādhenti” means they oppress. The hindrances can do nothing to wisdom that has arisen; thus it is said: “they do not allow unarisen wisdom to arise.” Therefore, due to the obstruction of conditions, because of not giving a chance to arise. “Established well in the four foundations of mindfulness” means the mind is properly fixed and applied in the fourfold development of the foundations of mindfulness. “Having developed according to its nature” means having developed it truly and rightly, so that the opposing states are completely eradicated.

Purimanaye satipaṭṭhānāni bojjhaṅgā ca missakā adhippetāti tato aññathā vattuṃ “apicetthā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Missakāti samathavipassanāmaggavasena missakā. “Catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu suppatiṭṭhitacittāti paṭhamaṃ vuttattā satipaṭṭhānesu vipassanaṃ gahetvā satta bojjhaṅge yathābhūtaṃ bhāvetvāti vuttattā maggapariyāpannānaṃyeva ca nesaṃ nippariyāyabojjhaṅgabhāvato tesu ca adhigatameva hotīti bojjhaṅge maggo ca sabbaññutaññāṇañcāti gahite sundaro pañho bhaveyyā”ti mahāsivatthero āha. Na panevaṃ gahitaṃ porāṇehīti adhippāyo. Itīti vuttappakāraparāmasanaṃ. Theroti sāriputtatthero.

In the previous method, the foundations of mindfulness and the enlightenment factors are intended as mixed; hence, to state it differently, “even if here,” and so on, was said. “Mixed” means mixed by way of serenity, insight, and the path. The Elder Mahāsiva said: “Because it was first stated that the mind is well-established in the four foundations of mindfulness, and because it was stated that insight is taken in the foundations of mindfulness and the seven enlightenment factors are developed as they truly are, and moreover, because they are direct enlightenment factors and what is attained is already in them, to include the path and omniscience in the enlightenment factors would make a beautiful question.” However, the ancients did not take it thus—such is the meaning. “Thus” refers to the stated kind. “The Elder” refers to the Elder Sāriputta.

Tatthāti [V 2.0181] tesu paccantanagarādīsu. Nagaraṃ viya nibbānaṃ tadatthikehi upagantabbato upagatānañca parissayarahitasukhādhigamaṭṭhānato. Pākāro viya sīlaṃ tadupagatānaṃ parito ārakkhabhāvato. Anupariyāyapatho viya hirī sīlapākārassa adhiṭṭhānabhāvato. Vuttañhetaṃ – “pariyāyapathoti kho bhikkhu hiriyā etaṃ adhivacana”nti. Dvāraṃ viya ariyamaggo nibbānanagarappavesane añjasabhāvato. Paṇḍitadovāriko viya dhammasenāpati nibbānanagaraṃ paviṭṭhapavisanakānaṃ sattānaṃ sallakkhaṇato. Dinnoti dāpito, sodhitoti attho. Sesaṃ suviññeyyameva.

Therein: in those frontier cities and so on. Nibbāna is like a city, as it is to be approached by those who seek that goal, and for those who have approached it, it is a place for attaining happiness free from danger. Virtue is like a city wall, as it provides complete protection for those who have approached it. Shame is like the circumambulation path, as it is the foundation of the wall of virtue. For this was said: “The circumambulation path, bhikkhu, this is a designation for shame.” The Noble Path is like a gate, as it is the direct means for entering the city of Nibbāna. The General of the Dhamma is like a wise gatekeeper, as he discerns the beings who have entered and who are entering the city of Nibbāna. “Given” means caused to be given, or purified—this is the meaning. The rest is easily understood.

3. Cundasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Cunda Sutta

379. Pubbe sāvatthito veḷuvagāmassa gatattā vuttaṃ “āgatamaggeneva paṭinivattanto”ti. Sattannanti upaseno, revato, khadiravaniyo, cundo, samaṇuddeso ahanti catunnaṃ, cālā, upacālā, sīsūpacālāti, tissannanti imesaṃ sattannaṃ arahantānaṃ. Natthi nu khoti etthāpi “olokento”ti ānetvā sambandho sīhāvalokanañāyena. Bhavissanti me vattāro harituṃ nāsakkhīti sambandho. Idaṃ dāni pacchimadassananti bhūtakathanamattaṃ, na tattha sālayatādassanaṃ yathā tathāgatassa vesāliyā nikkhamitvā nāgāpalokitaṃ.

379. Previously, because he had gone from Sāvatthī to Veḷuvagāma, it was said, “returning by the same path.” “Of the seven” refers to these seven arahants: the four—Upasena, Revata, Khadiravaniya, and Cunda—and the three—Cālā, Upacālā, and Sīsūpacālā. “Is there not?” Here too, “looking around” should be connected, in the manner of a lion’s glance. The connection is with the thought: “There will be those who say of me: ‘He was not able to carry on.’” This now is the last sight—merely a statement of fact, not a showing of attachment, as when the Tathāgata left Vesālī and looked back like an elephant.

Tassa [M 2.0479] tassa visesassa adhiṭṭhānavaseneva iddhibhedadassanaṃ iddhivikubbanaṃ. Sīhassa vijambhanādivasena kīḷitvā nādasadisī ayaṃ dhammakathāti vuttaṃ “sīhavikīḷito dhammapariyāyo”ti. Gamanakālo mayhantīti ettha iti-saddo parisamāpane. Tena therena yathārambhassa vacanapabandhassa samāpitabhāvaṃ joteti. Esa nayo sesesupi edisesu sabbaṭṭhānesu. Yugandharādayo paribhaṇḍapabbatāti veditabbā. Ekappahārenevāti ekappahārena iva. Svāyaṃ iva-saddo na sakkomīti ettha ānetvā sambandhitabbo.

The manifestation of various psychic powers, the display of psychic transformation, is due to the resolve for specific attainments. This Dhamma talk, being like the roar of a lion at play, stretching and so on, is called “the lion’s play of the Dhamma exposition.” In the phrase “The time for me to go,” the word “iti” serves to conclude the discourse. This shows that the elder’s connected speech, as he began it, has now been completed. This method applies to all similar instances elsewhere. The Yugandhara and other mountains should be understood as surrounding mountains. “With a single stroke” means as if with a single stroke. This word “iva” should be brought and connected to “I am unable.”

Paṭipādessāmīti ṭhitakāyaṃ paṭipādessāmi. Patthanākāle anomadassissa bhagavato vacanasutānusārena ñāṇena diṭṭhamattataṃ sandhāya “taṃ paṭhamadassana”nti vuttaṃ. Dhāretuṃ asakkontī guṇasāraṃ. Esa maggoti eso jātānaṃ sattānaṃ maraṇaniṭṭhito pantho. Punapi evaṃbhāvino nāma saṅkhārāti saṅkhārā nāma evaṃbhāvino, maraṇapariyosānāti attho. Ettakanti ettakaṃ kālaṃ. Saṅkaḍḍhitvā saṃharitvā. Mukhaṃ pidhāyāti mukhaṃ chādetvā. Agghikasatānīti makuḷaṅkuracetiyasatāni.

“I will proceed” means I will proceed while standing firm. At the moment of aspiration, in accordance with the words heard from the Blessed One Anomadassi, “that first sight” is said referring to the mere seeing with knowledge. Unable to retain the essential virtues. “This is the path” means this is the way for beings who are born, which ends in death. “Again, formations are of such a nature” means that formations are indeed so, having death as their end. “So much” means so much time. Having contracted, having gathered. “Covering the mouth” means covering the mouth. “Hundreds of agghikas” means hundreds of shrines shaped like buds and shoots.

Purimadivaseti [V 2.0182] atītadivase. Yasmā dhammasenāpatino arahattappattadivaseyeva satthu sāvakasannipāto ahosi, tasmā “pūritasāvakasannipāto esa bhikkhū”ti vuttaṃ. Pañca jātisatānīti bhummatthe, accantasaṃyoge vā upayogavacanaṃ.

“On the previous day” means on the past day. Because on the very day the General of the Dhamma attained arahantship there was an assembly of the Teacher’s disciples, it was therefore said, “This, monks, is a complete assembly of disciples.” “Five hundred births” is either in the sense of location, or a locative of usage in the sense of continuous connection.

Kaḷopihatthoti vilīvamayabhājanahattho. “Cammamayabhājanahattho”ti ca vadanti. Purantareti nagaramajjhe. Vaneti araññe.

“With a bowl in hand” means with a hand holding a wicker vessel. Some say, “with a hand holding a leather vessel.” “Inside the city” means in the middle of the city. “In the forest” means in the wilderness.

Osakkanākāravirahitoti dhammadesanāya saṅkocahetuvirahito. Visāradoti sāradavirahito. Dhammojanti dhammarasaṃ, ojavantaṃ desanādhammanti attho. Dhammabhoganti dhammaparibhogaṃ, parehi saddhiṃ saṃvibhajanavasena pavattaṃ dhammasambhoganti desanādhammameva vadati. Tena vuttaṃ “ubhayenapi dhammaparibhogova kathito”ti.

“Devoid of hesitation” means free from any cause for shrinking back in teaching the Dhamma. “Confident” means free from timidity. “The savor of Dhamma” means the taste of Dhamma; the meaning is the teaching of the Dhamma endowed with savor. “Enjoyment of Dhamma” means the use of Dhamma, the partaking of Dhamma that occurs through sharing with others; it refers to the teaching of the Dhamma itself. Therefore it is said, “By both, the enjoyment of Dhamma is spoken of.”

Piyāyitabbato piyehi. Manassa vaḍḍhanato manāpehi. Jātiyāti khattiyādijātiyā. Nānābhāvo asahabhāvo visuṃbhāvo. Aññathābhāvo aññathattaṃ. Sarīranti rūpadhammakāyasaṅkhātaṃ sarīraṃ. Rūpakāye [M 2.0480] hi bhijjante bhijjanteva. So bhijjeyyāti so mahantataro khandho bhijjeyya.

By dear ones, because they are to be cherished. Because they increase joy in the mind, they are pleasing. “By birth” means by birth as a noble, etc. “Diversity” means non-coexistence, separateness. “Alteration” means otherness. “The body” means the body designated as the collection of material phenomena. For the material body, when breaking, simply breaks. “May that break” means may that greater aggregate break.

Dakkhiṇadisaṃ gatoti dakkhiṇadisāmukhe pavatto. Mahākhandho viyāti mahanto sāravanto sākhākhandho viya. Sākhakhandhā hi disābhimukhapavattākārā, mūlakhandho pana uddhamuggato. Soḷasannaṃ pañhānanti soḷasannaṃ aparāpariyapavattaniyānaṃ atthānaṃ. Ñātuṃ icchito hi attho pañho.

“Gone to the southern direction” means proceeding toward the southern direction. “Like a great trunk” means like a large, substantial trunk with branches. For trunks with branches proceed facing a direction, but the root trunk grows upward. “Of the sixteen questions” means of the sixteen meanings that proceed in succession. For a meaning desired to be known is a question.

4-5. Ukkacelasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-5. Explanation of the Ukkacela Sutta and others

380-381. Amāvasuposatheti amāvasiuposathe, kālapakkhauposatheti attho. Purimanayenevāti anantarasutte vuttanayeneva.

380-381. “On the new moon Uposatha” means on the Uposatha of the new moon, that is, the Uposatha of the dark fortnight. “By the previous method” means by the method stated in the immediately preceding sutta.

6. Uttiyasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Explanation of the Uttiya Sutta

382. Maccudheyyassāti maccuno pavattiṭṭhānassa.

382. “Of Death’s realm” means of the place where Death operates.

8. Brahmasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Explanation of the Brahma Sutta

384. Tasmiṃ [V 2.0183] kāleti paṭhamābhisambodhiyaṃ. Bhikkhuyeva natthi dhammacakkassa appavattitattā, bhikkhuyeva bhikkhulakkhaṇayogato. Ekako maggo, na dvedhāpathabhūtoti ekamaggo, taṃ ekamaggaṃ. Jātiyā khayo vaṭṭadukkhassa antabhūtoti jātikkhayanto, nibbānaṃ, taṃ diṭṭhattā jātikkhayantadassīti evamettha attho daṭṭhabbo.

384. “At that time” means at the first full enlightenment. There were no monks, because the Wheel of the Dhamma had not been set in motion; a monk is so by virtue of possessing the characteristics of a monk. The path is single, not of the nature of a forked path, thus it is a single path; that single path. “The destruction of birth is the end of the suffering of the round”—thus, it is the end of birth, Nibbāna; because he has seen that, he is a “seer of the end of birth”—this is how the meaning should be understood here.

9. Sedakasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Explanation of the Sedaka Sutta

385. Ayaṃ tassa laddhīti ayaṃ idāni vuccamānā tassa ācariyassa laddhi. Tato anāmentoti yena vaṃso namati, tena kāyaṃ anāmento. Tathā namanto hi patitvā cuṇṇavicuṇṇaṃ hoti. Tanti kāyaṃ, taṃ vaṃsaṃ vā. Ākaḍḍhento viyāti namitaṭṭhānato parabhāgena ākaḍḍhento viya. Ekatobhāgiyaṃ katvāti yathāvuttaṃ satiṃ sūpaṭṭhitaṃ [M 2.0481] katvā onataṃ viya katvā. Tathā karaṇaṃ pana tathā vātūpatthambhagāhāpanenāti āha – “vātūpatthambhaṃ gāhāpetvā”ti. Tañca satiyā tadatthaṃ upaṭṭhānenāti vuttaṃ “satiṃ sūpaṭṭhitaṃ katvā”ti. Niccalova nisīdanto antevāsī ācariyaṃ rakkhati, ettakaṃ ācariyassa laddhivasena vuttaṃ. “Ācariyo vaṃsaṃ suggahitaṃ gaṇhanto”tiādi sabbaṃ heṭṭhā vuttanayameva.

385. “This is his view” means this view of that teacher, which is now being spoken of. Then, “not bending” means not bending the body in the way that the bamboo bends. For one who bends in that way, upon falling, becomes crushed to powder. “That” means the body, or that bamboo. “As if pulling” means as if pulling from the other side of the bent place. “Making it one-sided” means having made the aforementioned mindfulness well-established, making it as if bent down. Such an action, however, is by causing it to take support from the wind, as it is said: “having caused it to take support from the wind.” And that is by mindfulness attending to that purpose, as it is said: “having made mindfulness well-established.” The pupil, sitting motionless, protects the teacher—this much is said according to the teacher’s view. “The teacher, taking the well-grasped bamboo,” and so forth—all this is in the same way as explained below.

“Attānameva rakkhatī”ti idaṃ attano rakkhaṇaṃ padhānaṃ katvā vuttaṃ, na antevāsikanti avadhāraṇaphalaṃ. Attarakkhāya panettha sijjhamānāya antevāsikarakkhāpi siddhā eva hotīti. Dutiyapakkhepi eseva nayo. So tattha ñāyoti yā attano eva rakkhā, sā atthato pararakkhāpi hotīti ayamettha ñāyo yuttappayogo. Anuvaḍḍhiyāti yathāvaḍḍhitassa anuanuvaḍḍhiyā. Etena paṭiladdhasampayuttapamodanākāro dassitoti āha – “sapubbabhāgāya muditāyāti attho”ti. Āsevanāyātiādīni padāni anudayatāpariyosānāni (yasmā satipaṭṭhānaṃ sevantassa siddhaṃ attano ca parassa ca rakkhaṇaṃ pakāsenti, tasmā) – “attānaṃ, bhikkhave, rakkhissāmīti satipaṭṭhānaṃ sevitabba”ntiādi vuttaṃ.

“Protects only himself”—this is said making self-protection primary, not that of the disciple; this is the result of the emphasis. For here, when self-protection is being accomplished, the protection of the disciple is also accomplished. The same method applies in the second case as well. That is the principle there: that the protection of oneself is, in essence, also the protection of others—this is the principle, the proper application here. “By increasing” means by the progressive increase of what has increased. By this, the manner of joy connected with what has been regained is shown; thus it is said: “the meaning is with sympathetic joy that includes its preliminary part.” The words beginning with “By cultivation,” culminating in non-malevolence (since they reveal the accomplished protection of both oneself and others for one who cultivates the foundations of mindfulness, therefore it is said: “Monks, thinking, ‘I will protect myself,’ the foundations of mindfulness should be cultivated,” and so forth).

10. Janapadakalyāṇīsuttavaṇṇanā

10. Explanation of the Janapadakalyāṇī Sutta

386. Janapadasmiṃ kalyāṇīti sakalajanapade bhaddā rūpasampattiyā sikkhāsampattiyā ca sundarā [V 2.0184] seṭṭhā. Rūpasampatti ca nāma sabbaso rūpadosābhāvena rūpaguṇapāripūriyā hotīti tadubhayaṃ dassetuṃ “chasarīradosarahitā pañcakalyāṇasamannāgatā”ti vuttaṃ. Taṃ duvidhampi vivaranto “sā hī”tiādimāha. Pañcakalyāṇasamannāgatāti pañcavidhasarīraguṇasampadāhi samannāgatā. Nātidīghā nātirassāti pamāṇamajjhimā dīghatarappamāṇā na hoti, na atirassā, lakuṇḍakarūpā na hoti. Nātikisāti ativiya kisathaddhamaṃsalohitā dissamānā aṭṭhisarīrā jālasarīrā na hoti. Nātithūlāti bhāriyamaṃsā mahodarā na hoti. Nātikāḷā nāccodātāti ativiya kāḷavaṇṇā jhāmaṅgāro viya[M 2.0482], dadhitakkādīhi pamajjitamattakaṃsalohavaṇṇā na hoti. Manussaloke tādisiyā rūpasampattiyā abhāvato atikkantā manussavaṇṇaṃ. Yathā pamāṇayuttā, evaṃ ārohapariṇāhayogato ca paresaṃ pasādāvahā nātidīghatādayo. Evaṃ manussānaṃ dibbarūpatāsampattīpīti vuttaṃ “appattā dibbavaṇṇa”nti. Ettha ca nātidīghanātirassatāvacanena ārohasampatti vuttā ubbedhena pāsādikabhāvato. Kisathūladosābhāvavacanena pariṇāhasampatti vuttā. Ubhayenapi saṇṭhānasampadā vibhāvitā, nātikāḷatāvacanena vaṇṇasampatti vuttā vivaṇṇatābhāvato. Piyaṅgusāmāti pariṇatapiyaṅgupupphasadisasarīranibhāsā. Mukhapariyosānanti adharoṭṭhamāha. Ayaṃ yathāvuttā sarīravaṇṇasampatti. Assāti janapadakalyāṇiyā. Chavikalyāṇatā chavisampattihetukattā tassā. Esa nayo sesesupi. Nakhā eva pattasadisatāya nakhapattāni.

386. 'Kalyāṇī in the country' means she is excellent, beautiful in the entire land by way of her perfection of beauty and accomplishment in training. And beauty, indeed, is the perfection of physical qualities due to the complete absence of bodily flaws; thus, to show both, it is said: 'free from the six bodily defects and endowed with the five beauties.' Explaining these two aspects, he says, 'For she...' and so forth. 'Endowed with the five beauties' means endowed with the fivefold excellence of bodily qualities. 'Neither too tall nor too short' means she is of medium height—not of excessive height, nor too short, not like a dwarf. 'Neither too thin' means she is not excessively emaciated, with visible bones and tendons, appearing skeletal or like a network of veins. 'Neither too stout' means she is not overly heavy with flesh or potbellied. 'Neither too dark nor too pale' means she is not extremely dark like burnt charcoal, nor is she excessively pale like the color of polished bronze cleaned with curds, buttermilk, and so on. Because such perfection of beauty is absent in the human world, it surpasses human appearance. Just as she is proportionate, so, due to the harmony of height and girth, 'neither too tall' and so forth inspire admiration in others. Thus, it is said: 'unattained by divine complexion,' meaning that even among humans, she possesses divine-like beauty. Here, by the statement 'neither too tall nor too short,' the perfection of height is spoken of, due to her pleasing stature in terms of elevation. By the statement of the absence of thinness or stoutness, the perfection of girth is spoken of. By both, the perfection of form is revealed. By the statement 'neither too dark,' the perfection of complexion is spoken of, due to the absence of discoloration. 'Like a piyaṅgu blossom' means her body's radiance is like that of a fully bloomed piyaṅgu blossom. 'The boundary of the face' refers to the lower lip. This describes the aforementioned perfection of body and complexion. 'Of her' refers to the Janapadakalyāṇī. Her skin's beauty is because of her perfection of skin. The same principle applies to the rest as well. 'Her nails are like petals' because her nails are like petals.

(Pasāvo sarīrāvayavena iriyananti āha – “pavattīti attho”ti, pasāvo yathāparitameva kanatanti, na sabhāvasandhānaṃ. Yathāvibhāvasena uttamameva naccaṃ naccati. Te vā vīsatiyāsūtiraṃ dhānappattiyā pavattiyā pavattimakatamandatā vibhāvasuṭatassa uttamameva gītañca gāyatīti attho.) [Etthantare pāṭho asuddho dussodhanīyo ca. Suddhapāṭho gavesitabbo.] Samatittiko telapattoti mukhavattisamaṃ telānaṃ pūritattā samatittikamukhaṃ telabhājanaṃ. Antarena ca mahāsamajjaṃ antarena ca janapadakalyāṇinti janapadakalyāṇiyā, tassā ca naccagītaṃ pekkhituṃ sannipatitamahājanasamūhassa majjhato pariharitabbo netabbo. Nanti telaṃ. Āhareyyāti āpajjeyya. Tatridaṃ opammasaṃsandanaṃ – telapattaṃ viya kāyagatāsati, tassa pariharaṇapuggalo viya vipassako, janakāyā viya puthuttārammaṇāni, asipuriso viya mano, telassa cajanaṃ viya kilesuppādanaṃ, sīsapātanaṃ viya ariyamaggañāṇasīsānuppatti. “Kāyagatā sati no bhāvitā…pe… sikkhitabba”nti vuttattā “pubbabhāgavipassanāva kathitā”ti vuttaṃ.

The body moves with its limbs, hence it is said—'the meaning is "movement".' The body moves just as it is bent, not by its inherent nature. According to its manifestation, the best dance is danced. Or, those twenty strings, through the attainment of rhythm, through the course of movement, the movement itself is made dull, while the manifestation of ease is excellent, and the best song is sung—this is the meaning. 'A bowl of oil filled to the brim' means an oil vessel filled with oil level with its rim. And 'amidst the great assembly and amidst the Janapadakalyāṇī' means that he must be led through the midst of the great crowd gathered to watch the Janapadakalyāṇī and her dance and song. 'Naṃ' means oil. 'Should spill' means should incur. Here is the connection of the simile: mindfulness of the body is like the bowl of oil; the person carrying it carefully is like the insight meditator; the crowd is like the diverse objects of attention; the man with a sword is like the mind; the spilling of oil is like the arising of defilements; the falling of the head is like the non-arising of the head of noble path knowledge. Because it is said, 'Mindfulness of the body is undeveloped... should be cultivated,' it is stated that 'only the preliminary part of insight is discussed.'

Nālandavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Nālanda chapter is completed.

3. Sīlaṭṭhitivaggo

3. The Chapter on the Establishment in Virtue

1-2. Sīlasuttādivaṇṇanā

1–2. Commentary on the Sīla Sutta and Others

387-388. Sīlānīti [V 2.0185][M 2.0483] bahuvacanaṃ anekavidhattā sīlassa. Tañhi sīlanaṭṭhena ekavidhampi cārittādivasena anekavidhaṃ. Tenāha – “catupārisuddhisīlānī”ti. Pañhamaggoti ñātuṃ icchitassa atthassa vīmaṃsanaṃ. Tenāha – “pañhagavesana”nti.

387–388. “Sīlāni” is in the plural because of the various forms of virtue. Indeed, even though virtue is singular in the sense of composing, it can be of many kinds by way of conduct and so on. Hence it is said: “the virtues of fourfold purity.” “Pañhamagga” means the investigation of a meaning one wishes to know. Hence it is said: “the search for questions.”

3-5. Parihānasuttādivaṇṇanā

3–5. Commentary on the Parihānasutta and Others

389-391. Puggalavasena parihānaṃ hoti na dhammavasena. Yo na bhāveti, tasseva parihāyati. Tenāha “yo hī”tiādi.

389–391. Decline occurs by way of individuals, not by way of the Dhamma. For whomever does not cultivate, for him it declines. Therefore he said, “He who…” and so on.

6. Padesasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Padesa Sutta

392. Padesato bhāvitattāti ekadesato bhāvitattā bhāvanāpāripūriyā ananuppattattā. Tenāha – “cattāro hi magge”tiādi.

392. “Padesato bhāvitattā” means because of being developed in part, because the perfection of development has not been attained. Hence it is said: “for the four paths…” and so on.

7. Samattasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Samatta Sutta

393. Samattā bhāvitattāti pariyattā bhāvitattā.

393. “Samattā bhāvitattā” means because of being completely developed.

8-10. Lokasuttādivaṇṇanā

8–10. Commentary on the Loka Sutta and Others

394-396. Mahāvisayattā mahatiyo abhiññā etassāti mahābhiñño, tassa bhāvoti sabbaṃ vattabbaṃ. “Satatavihāravasena vutta”nti vatvā tamatthaṃ pākaṭaṃ kātuṃ “thero kirā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tassāti therassa. Āvajjanassa gatinti ekāvajjanasseva gamanavīthiṃ. Anubandhati cakkavāḷānaṃ sahassaṃ ekāvajjaneneva sahassalokadhātuyā icchitamatthaṃ jānituṃ samatthoti dasseti.

394–396. Because the great direct knowledges are of great scope for him, he is one with great direct knowledge (mahābhiñño). Everything should be stated concerning his state. Having said, “It was said by way of constant abiding,” “the elder, indeed,” and so on, was stated to clarify that meaning. “His” refers to the elder. “The course of attention” means the path of a single act of attention. He pursues a thousand world-systems with a single act of attention, showing that he is capable of knowing the desired object in a thousand world-systems.

Sīlaṭṭhitivaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Sīlaṭṭhiti Vagga is concluded.

4. Ananussutavaggavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Ananussuta Vagga
