Sā eva ca yathāvuttaiddhi yasmā heṭṭhimā heṭṭhimā uparimāya uparimāya pādabhūtā, tasmā vuttaṃ “iddhibhūtaṃ vā pādanti iddhipāda”nti. Sesesūti vīriyasamādhiādīsu. Tattha hi vīriyaṃ cittaṃ vīmaṃsaṃ adhipatiṃ katvā paṭiladdhasamādhi vīmaṃsāsamādhīti attho veditabbo. Idhāti imasmiṃ iddhipādasaṃyutte. Ekaparicchedova atthaniddeso.

813. Concentration arisen based on desire: concentration attained by making the desire to act (kattukamyatāchanda) the dominant factor is desire-concentration. 'Volitional formations of exertion' is a designation for the energy of right effort, which accomplishes the four tasks. 'By these' means by desire-concentration and the volitional formations of exertion. 'The base of spiritual power' means, in the sense of succeeding by way of accomplishment; or, by this method, faith and other qualities succeed, grow, and become exalted—thus they are called 'spiritual power.' It is the foundation (pāda), in the sense of a basis (adhiṭṭhāna), for the desire-concentration and volitional formations of exertion associated with wholesome states of consciousness such as access concentration, and for the remaining aggregates of consciousness and mental factors. And since that very spiritual power (iddhi), as stated, is such that each lower stage serves as the foundation for each higher one, it is said: 'that which has become spiritual power, or its base—thus, a base of spiritual power.' In the remaining cases, such as energy-concentration, it should be understood that concentration is attained by making energy, consciousness, or investigation the dominant factor, thus it is called energy-concentration, consciousness-concentration, or investigation-concentration, respectively. Here, in this Connected Discourse on the Bases of Spiritual Power, the explanation of the meaning is a single section.

5. Iddhipadesasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Discourse on a Portion of Spiritual Power

817. Iddhipadesanti iddhiyā ekadesaṃ. Ko pana soti āha – “tayo ca magge tīṇi ca phalānī”ti.

817. 'A portion of spiritual power' means a single portion of spiritual power. What is that? It is said: 'the three paths and the three fruits.'

6. Samattasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Discourse on Completion

818. Samattanti sāmaññassa samaṃ attanaṃ ijjhanaṃ samattaṃ paripuṇṇaṃ. Vivaṭṭapādakā eva iddhipādā kathitā “apārā pāraṃ gamanāya saṃvattantī”tiādivacanato.

818. 'Completion' (samatta) means the complete and perfect accomplishment of one's own recluseship (sāmaññassa). The bases of spiritual power are indeed called 'foundations for the rolling back' (vivaṭṭapādakā) because of the saying, 'they lead from the near shore to the far shore,' and so on.

10. Cetiyasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Cetiya Discourse

822. Udenayakkhassa [V 2.0206] cetiyaṭṭhāneti udenassa nāma yakkhassa devāyatanasaṅkhepena iṭṭhakāhi kate mahājanassa cittīkataṭṭhāne. Katavihāroti bhagavantaṃ uddissa tattha katavihāro. Vuccatīti purimavohārena [M 2.0504] “udenacetiya”nti vuccati. Gotamakādīsupīti gotamakacetiyanti evamādīsupi. Eseva nayoti cetiyaṭṭhāne katavihārabhāvaṃ atidisati. Vaḍḍhitāti bhāvanāpāripūrivasena paribrūhitā. Punappunaṃ katāti bhāvanāya bahulīkaraṇavasena aparāparaṃ pavattitā ānītā. Yuttayānaṃ viya katāti yathā yuttamājaññayānaṃ chekena sārathinā adhiṭṭhitaṃ yathāruci pavattimarahati, evaṃ yathāruci pavattanārahataṃ gamitā. Patiṭṭhānaṭṭhenāti adhiṭṭhānaṭṭhena. Vatthu viya katāti sabbaso upakkilesavisodhanena iddhivisesānaṃ pavattiṭṭhānabhāvato suvisodhitaparissayavatthu viya katā. Adhiṭṭhitāti paṭipakkhadūrībhāvato subhāvitabhāvena taṃtaṃadhiṭṭhānayogyatāya ṭhapitā. Samantato citāti sabbabhāgena bhāvanupacayaṃ gamitā. Tenāha “suvaḍḍhitā”ti. Suṭṭhu samāraddhāti iddhibhāvanāya sikhappattiyā sammadeva saṃsevitā.

822. At the site of the shrine of the yakkha Udena: at the place esteemed by the great populace, made with bricks in the form of a divine abode for the yakkha named Udena. A monastery was made: a monastery was made there, dedicated to the Blessed One. It is called: by prior usage, it is called the 'Udena Cetiya.' And in the cases of Gotamaka and so on: as in 'Gotamaka Cetiya,' and so on. This is the same method: it extends to the state of being a monastery built at a shrine site. Developed: augmented by means of the fulfillment of development. Done again and again: brought about and made to occur repeatedly by means of making the development frequent. Made like a yoked vehicle: just as a yoked thoroughbred vehicle, controlled by a skilled charioteer, is fit to proceed as desired, so too this was brought to a state of fitness to proceed as desired. In the sense of a foundation: in the sense of a basis. Made like a base-object: made like a well-purified base-object, a place for the arising of special spiritual powers, through the complete purification from all defilements. Established: placed in a state of suitability for this or that determination, due to being well-developed through the removal of opposing factors. Accumulated on all sides: brought to an accumulation of development in every part. Therefore, he said, 'well-developed.' Well undertaken: rightly cultivated to the peak of attainment in the development of spiritual power.

Aniyamenāti “yassa kassacī”ti aniyatavacanena. Niyametvāti “tathāgatassā”ti sarūpaggahaṇena niyametvā. Āyuppamāṇanti paramāyuppamāṇaṃ vadati. Tasseva gahaṇe kāraṇaṃ brahmajālavaṇṇanāyaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Mahāsīvatthero pana mahābodhisattānaṃ carimabhave paṭisandhidāyino kammassa asaṅkheyyāyukatāsaṃvattanasamatthataṃ hadaye ṭhapetvā buddhānaṃ āyusaṅkhārassa parissayavikkhambhanasamatthatā pāḷiyaṃ āgatā evāti imaṃ bhaddakappameva tiṭṭheyyāti avoca.

Indefinitely: by the indefinite expression 'of anyone whatsoever.' Having specified: having specified by taking its own form, 'of the Tathāgata.' The measure of the lifespan: it speaks of the maximum measure of the lifespan. The reason for taking only that should be understood in the same way as explained in the commentary on the Brahmajāla Sutta. However, the Elder Mahāsīva, keeping in mind that the kamma which gives rebirth to great bodhisattas in their final existence has the capacity to lead to an incalculable lifespan, and that the ability of the Buddhas' life-formation to suppress dangers has indeed come down in the Pāli canon, said, 'He could remain for this auspicious aeon itself.'

Khaṇḍiccādīhi abhisuyyatīti etena yathā iddhibalena jarāya na paṭighāto, evaṃ tena maraṇassapi na paṭighātoti atthato āpannamevāti. “Kva saro khitto, kvacani patito”ti aññathā vuṭṭhitenapi theravādena aṭṭhakathāvacanameva samatthitanti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Tenāha “so pana na ruccati…pe… niyamita”nti.

Because it is mentioned that he suffered from broken teeth and so on, it is thereby established in meaning that just as old age cannot be prevented by spiritual power, so too death cannot be prevented by it. 'Where was the arrow shot, where did it fall?'—it should be understood that even though the Theravāda position has arisen differently, it is the statement of the commentary alone that is supported. Therefore, it is said: 'But that is not accepted... et cetera... determined.'

Pariyuṭṭhitacittoti yathā kiñci atthānatthaṃ sallakkhetuṃ na sakkā, evaṃ abhibhūtacitto. So pana abhibhavo mahatā udakoghena appakassa udakassa ajjhottharaṇaṃ viya ahosīti vuttaṃ “ajjhotthaṭacitto”ti. Aññoti therato, ariyehi vā añño yo koci paro [M 2.0505] puthujjano. Puthujjanaggahaṇañcettha yathā sabbena sabbaṃ appahīnavipallāso mārena pariyuṭṭhitacitto kiñci atthānatthaṃ sallakkhetuṃ na sakkoti, evaṃ thero bhagavatā kataṃ nimittobhāsaṃ sabbaso na [V 2.0207] sallakkhesīti dassanatthaṃ. Tenāha “māro hī”tiādi. Cattāro vipallāsāti asubhe “subha”nti saññāvipallāso, cittavipallāso, dukkhe “sukha”nti saññāvipallāso, cittavipallāsoti ime cattāro vipallāsā. Tenāti yadipi itare aṭṭha vipallāsā pahīnā, yathāvuttānaṃ catunnaṃ vipallāsānaṃ appahīnabhāvena. Assāti therassa.

With mind assailed: a mind so overpowered that it is not possible to discern any benefit or harm. That overpowering was like the submerging of a small amount of water by a great flood of water; hence it is said, 'with mind submerged.' Another: someone other than the elder, or someone other than the noble ones, any other ordinary person. The inclusion of 'ordinary person' here is to show that just as an ordinary person, whose distortions have not been entirely abandoned, cannot discern any benefit or harm when their mind is assailed by Māra, so the elder did not at all discern the sign and hint made by the Blessed One. Therefore, it is said, 'For Māra, indeed...' and so on. The four distortions: the distortion of perception and the distortion of consciousness in perceiving the unattractive as 'attractive'; the distortion of perception and the distortion of consciousness in perceiving suffering as 'pleasure'—these are the four distortions. By that: although the other eight distortions were abandoned, it was due to the non-abandonment of the four distortions as stated. Of him: of the elder.

Maddatīti phusanamattena maddanto viya hoti, aññathā tena maddite sattānaṃ maraṇameva siyā, kiṃ sakkhissati na sakkhissatīti adhippāyo. Kasmā na sakkhissati? Nanu esa aggasāvakassa mahiddhikassa mahānubhāvassa kucchiṃ paviṭṭhoti? Saccaṃ paviṭṭho, tañca kho attano mahānubhāvassa dassanatthaṃ, na vibādhanādhippāyena. Vibādhanādhippāyena pana idha “kiṃ sakkhissatī”ti vuttaṃ hadayamaddanassa adhikatattā. Nimittobhāsanti ettha “tiṭṭhatu bhagavā kappa”nti sakalakappaṃ avaṭṭhānayācanāya “yassa kassaci, ānanda, cattāro iddhipādā bhāvitā”tiādinā aññāpadesena attano caturiddhipādabhāvanānubhāvena kappaṃ avaṭṭhānasamatthatāvasena saññuppādanaṃ nimittaṃ. Tathā pana pariyāyaggahaṇaṃ muñcitvā ujukaṃyeva attano adhippāyavibhāvanaṃ obhāso. Jānantoyevāti mārena pariyuṭṭhitabhāvaṃ jānanto eva. Attano aparādhahetuko sattānaṃ soko tanuko hoti, na balavāti āha – “dosāropanena sokatanukaraṇattha”nti. Kiṃ pana thero mārena pariyuṭṭhitacittakāle pavattiṃ pacchā jānātīti? Na jānāti sabhāvena, buddhānubhāvena pana jānāti.

'He crushes': he is as if crushing by mere touch; otherwise, if he were crushed by him, beings would simply die. The intention is, 'What will he be able to do, what will he not be able to do?' Why will he not be able to do it? Has he not entered the belly of the chief disciple, one of great spiritual power and great might? It is true he has entered, but that was to display his own great might, not with the intention to harm. Here, however, it is said with the intention to harm, 'What will he be able to do?' because of the severity of the crushing of the heart. 'The sign and the hint': here, the sign (nimitta) is the production of a suggestion, by way of his capacity to remain for an aeon through the power of his development of the four bases of spiritual power, by means of the indirect statement beginning, 'Ānanda, whoever has developed the four bases of spiritual power,' for the sake of the request to remain for the entire aeon, 'May the Blessed One remain for the aeon.' The hint (obhāsa), however, is the direct manifestation of his own intention, having abandoned the taking up of a roundabout expression. 'Knowing indeed': knowing the state of being assailed by Māra. He said, 'for the purpose of lessening sorrow by attributing fault,' because the sorrow of beings on account of his own transgression is slight, not strong. But does the elder know afterwards of the event at the time his mind was assailed by Māra? He does not know by his own nature, but he knows through the power of the Buddha.

Anatthe niyojento guṇamāraṇena māreti, virāgavibandhanena vā jātinimittatāya tattha tattha jātaṃ mārento viya hotīti “māretīti māro”ti vuttaṃ. Atipāpattā pāpimā. Kaṇhadhammasamannāgato kaṇho. Virāgādiguṇānaṃ antakaraṇato antako. Sattānaṃ anatthāvahaṃ paṭipattiṃ na muñcatīti namuci. Attano mārapāsena pamatte bandhati, pamattā vā bandhū etassāti pamattabandhu. Sattamasattāhato paraṃ satta [M 2.0506] ahāni sandhāyāha “aṭṭhame sattāhe”ti, na pana pallaṅkasattāhādi viya niyatakiccassa aṭṭhamasattāhassa nāma labbhanato. Sattamasattāhassa hi parato ajapālanigrodhamūle brahmuno sakkassa ca paṭiññātadhammadesanaṃ bhagavantaṃ ñatvā “idāni satte dhammadesanāya mama visayaṃ atikkāmessatī”ti sañjātadomanasso hutvā ṭhito cintesi, “handāhaṃ dāni naṃ upāyena parinibbāpessāmi, evamassa manoratho aññathattaṃ gamissati, mama manoratho ijjhissatī”ti cintetvā bhagavantaṃ upasaṅkamitvā ekamantaṃ ṭhito – “parinibbātu dāni, bhante, bhagavā”tiādinā parinibbānaṃ yāci. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “aṭṭhame sattāhe”tiādi. Tattha ajjāti āyusaṅkhāravossajjanadivasaṃ sandhāyāha. Bhagavā cassa atibandhanādhippāyaṃ [V 2.0208] jānantopi taṃ anāvikatvā parinibbānassa akālabhāvameva pakāsento yācanaṃ paṭikkhipi. Tenāha “na tāvāha”ntiādi.

One who engages beings in what is not beneficial kills by destroying virtue, or by creating an impediment to dispassion, or is like one who kills beings born here and there due to the sign of birth—thus he is called 'Māra' because he kills. Due to his extreme wickedness, he is Pāpimā. Being endowed with dark qualities, he is Kaṇha. Because he brings an end to virtues such as dispassion, he is Antaka. Since he does not relinquish a practice that brings what is not beneficial to beings, he is Namuci. He binds the heedless with his Māra-snare, or the heedless are his kinsmen, thus he is Pamattabandhu. Regarding 'in the eighth week,' this refers to the seven days after the seventh week; it is not that the name 'eighth week' is found for a specific task, like the week of sitting on the throne, etc. For after the seventh week, at the foot of the Ajapāla banyan tree, knowing that the Blessed One had been requested by Brahmā and Sakka to teach the Dhamma, Māra became distressed, thinking, 'Now, by teaching the Dhamma, he will cause beings to transcend my domain.' Standing there, he reflected, 'Come now, I shall cause him to attain parinibbāna by some means; thus his aspiration will be altered, and my aspiration will be fulfilled.' Having thought this, he approached the Blessed One, stood to one side, and requested his parinibbāna, saying, 'May the Blessed One now attain parinibbāna, venerable sir,' etc. It is with reference to this that 'in the eighth week,' etc., was said. Therein, 'today' refers to the day of relinquishing the life-formation. And although the Blessed One knew his malicious intent, without revealing it, he rejected the request, making known only that it was not the time for parinibbāna. Therefore he said, 'Not yet shall I…,' etc.

Maggavasena byattāti saccapaṭivedhaveyyattiyena byattā. Tatheva vinītāti maggavasena kilesānaṃ samucchedavinayena vinītā. Tathā visāradāti ariyamaggādhigameneva satthusāsane vesārajjappattiyā visāradā, sārajjakarānaṃ diṭṭhivicikicchādipāpadhammānaṃ vigamena visāradabhāvaṃ pattāti attho. Yassa sutassa vasena vaṭṭadukkhato nissaraṇaṃ sambhavati, taṃ idha ukkaṭṭhaniddesena “suta”nti adhippetanti āha “tepiṭakavasenā”ti. Tiṇṇaṃ piṭakānaṃ samūho tepiṭakaṃ, tīṇi vā piṭakāni tipiṭakaṃ, tipiṭakameva tepiṭakaṃ, tassa vasena. Tadevāti yaṃ taṃ tepiṭakaṃ sotabbabhāvena sutanti vuttaṃ, tameva. Dhammanti pariyattidhammaṃ. Dhārentīti suvaṇṇabhājane pakkhittasīhavasaṃ viya avinassantaṃ katvā suppaguṇasuppavattibhāvena dhārenti hadaye ṭhapenti. Iti pariyattidhammavasena bahussutadhammadharabhāvaṃ dassetvā idāni paṭivedhavasenapi taṃ dassento “atha vā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Ariyadhammassāti maggaphaladhammassa, navavidhassa vā lokuttaradhammassa. Anudhammabhūtanti adhigamāyānurūpaṃ dhammabhūtaṃ. Anucchavikapaṭipadanti tameva vipassanādhammamāha, chabbidhā visuddhiyo vā. Anudhammanti nibbānadhammassa anudhammo, yathāvuttapaṭipadā, tassānurūpaṃ abhisallekhitaṃ appicchatādidhammaṃ. Caraṇasīlāti samādāya vattanasīlā. Anumaggaphaladhammo etissāti vā anudhammā, vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanā, tassa caraṇasīlā. Attano ācariyavādanti attano ācariyassa [M 2.0507] sammāsambuddhassa vādaṃ. Sadevakassa lokassa ācārasikkhāpanena ācariyo, bhagavā, tassa vādo, catusaccadesanā.

'Skilled by way of the path' means skilled through proficiency in penetrating the truths. Likewise, 'trained' means trained by way of the path through the complete eradication of defilements. Similarly, 'confident' means confident by way of attaining the noble path, having achieved confidence in the Teacher’s Dispensation; the meaning is that through the removal of evil states that cause timidity, such as wrong views and doubt, one has attained a state of confidence. Here, that which is 'learned' is that by means of which escape from the suffering of the round of existence is possible; this is meant by the highest designation, thus it is said, 'by way of the Tipiṭaka.' The Tepiṭaka is the collection of the three Piṭakas, or the three Piṭakas are the Tipiṭaka, or the Tipiṭaka itself is the Tepiṭaka; by way of that. 'That very thing' means that which is called 'learned' (suta) because it is to be heard (sotabba), namely, the Tipiṭaka itself. 'Dhamma' means the scriptural Dhamma. 'They retain' means they place it in their hearts, making it imperishable like lion’s fat placed in a golden vessel, by being well-practiced and well-versed in it. Having thus shown the state of being learned and a bearer of the Dhamma by way of the scriptural Dhamma, now, to show it also by way of penetration, 'or else,' etc., is said. 'Of the noble Dhamma' means of the path and fruit Dhamma, or of the ninefold supramundane Dhamma. 'Being in accordance with the Dhamma' means being a state of Dhamma suitable for attainment. 'The suitable practice' refers to that very insight-Dhamma, or the six kinds of purification. 'In accordance with the Dhamma' means the aforementioned practice is in accordance with the Dhamma of Nibbāna; it refers to the corresponding austere qualities such as fewness of wishes. 'Virtue of conduct' means the virtue of undertaking and observing. Or, 'in accordance with the Dhamma' (anudhammā) is that which has the path and fruit Dhamma in accordance with it, namely, insight leading to emergence; 'its virtue of conduct.' 'The doctrine of one’s teacher' means the doctrine of one’s own teacher, the Perfectly Enlightened One. The Blessed One is the teacher (ācariya) because he instructs the world with its gods in proper conduct (ācāra); his doctrine is the teaching of the Four Truths.

Ācikkhissantīti ādito kathessanti, attanā uggahitaniyāmena pare uggaṇhāpessantīti attho. Desessantīti vācessanti, pāḷiṃ sammā pabodhessantīti attho. Paññapessantīti pajānāpessanti, saṅkāsessantīti attho. Paṭṭhapessantīti pakārehi ṭhapessanti, pakāsessantīti attho. Vivarissantīti vivaṭaṃ karissanti. Vibhajissantīti vibhattaṃ karissanti. Uttānīkarissantīti anuttānaṃ gambhīraṃ uttānaṃ pākaṭaṃ karissanti. Sahadhammenāti ettha dhamma-saddo kāraṇapariyāyo “hetumhi ñāṇaṃ dhammapaṭisambhidā”tiādīsu (vibha. 720) viyāti āha “sahetukena sakāraṇena vacanenā”ti.

'They will declare' means they will speak from the beginning; the meaning is that they will cause others to learn by the method they themselves have learned. 'They will teach' means they will recite; the meaning is that they will properly awaken others to the Pāli text. 'They will make known' means they will cause to understand; the meaning is they will clarify. 'They will set forth' means they will establish in various ways; the meaning is they will make manifest. 'They will reveal' means they will make open. 'They will analyze' means they will make distinct. 'They will make plain' means they will make what is not plain and is profound, plain and evident. Regarding 'with the Dhamma': here the word 'dhamma' is a synonym for 'cause,' as in passages like 'knowledge of the cause is the analytical knowledge of the Dhamma' (Vibhaṅga 720). Thus it is said: 'with speech that has a reason and a cause.'

Sappāṭihāriyanti sanissaraṇaṃ. Yathā paravādaṃ bhañjitvā sakavādo patiṭṭhahati, evaṃ hetudāharaṇehi yathādhigatamatthaṃ sampādetvā dhammaṃ kathessanti. Tenāha “niyyānikaṃ katvā dhammaṃ desessantī”ti[V 2.0209], navavidhaṃ lokuttaraṃ dhammaṃ pabodhessantīti attho. Ettha ca “paññapessantī”tiādīhi chahi padehi cha atthapadāni dassitāni, ādito pana dvīhi padehi cha byañjanapadāni. Ettāvatā tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ saṃvaṇṇanānayena saṅgahetvā dassitaṃ hoti. Vuttañhetaṃ nettiyaṃ (netti. saṅgahavāra) “dvādasa padāni suttaṃ, taṃ sabbaṃ byañjanañca attho cā”ti.

'With the marvel' means with an escape. Just as one's own doctrine is established after refuting the doctrines of others, so too will they teach the Dhamma, having established the meaning as they have understood it with reasons and examples. Therefore it is said, 'they will teach the Dhamma, having made it emancipating,' the meaning being that they will awaken others to the ninefold supramundane Dhamma. And here, by the six terms beginning with 'they will make known,' six terms of meaning are shown, but by the initial two terms, six terms of phrasing are shown. By this much, the Tipiṭaka, the word of the Buddha, is shown summarized by the method of exposition. For this is said in the Netti: 'The twelve terms are the sutta; all of that is both phrasing and meaning.'

Sikkhattayasaṅgahitanti adhisīlasikkhādisikkhattayasaṅgahaṃ. Sakalaṃ sāsanabrahmacariyanti anavasesaṃ satthusāsanabhūtaṃ seṭṭhacariyaṃ. Samiddhanti sammadeva vaḍḍhitaṃ. Jhānassādavasenāti tehi tehi bhikkhūhi samadhigatajhānasukhavasena. Vuḍḍhippattanti uḷārapaṇītabhāvūpagamanena sabbaso parivuḍḍhimupagataṃ. Sabbapāliphullaṃ viya abhiññāsampadāhi sāsanābhivuḍḍhiyā matthakappattito. Patiṭṭhitavasenāti patiṭṭhānavasena, patiṭṭhappattiyāti attho. Paṭivedhavasena bahuno janassa hitaṃ bāhujaññaṃ. Tenāha “mahājanābhisamayavasenā”ti. Puthu puthulaṃ bhūtaṃ jātaṃ, puthuttaṃ bhūtaṃ pattanti vā puthubhūtaṃ. Tenāha “sabbākārena puthulabhāvappatta”nti. Suṭṭhu pakāsitanti sammadeva ādikalyāṇādibhāvena paveditaṃ.

'Comprised in the threefold training' means the inclusion of the threefold training in higher virtue, etc. 'The entire holy life of the Dispensation' means the complete, supreme conduct that is the Teacher's Dispensation, without remainder. 'Thriving' means rightly grown. 'Through the taste of jhāna' means through the happiness of jhāna attained by those various monks. 'Having attained growth' means having reached full growth in every way by attaining an eminent and sublime state. Because it has reached the pinnacle of the Dispensation's growth through the accomplishments of the direct knowledges, it is like a tree laden with fruit and flowers. 'By way of being established' means by way of establishment; the meaning is having attained a firm footing. 'For the welfare of many' (bāhujaññaṃ) is for the welfare of many people by way of penetration. Therefore it is said, 'by way of the great populace's realization.' 'Become widespread' (puthubhūtaṃ) means it has become extensive, or it has attained an extensive state. Therefore it is said, 'having attained an extensive state in all aspects.' 'Well-proclaimed' means rightly declared as being good in the beginning, etc.

Satiṃ [M 2.0508] sūpaṭṭhitaṃ katvāti ayaṃ kāyādivibhāgo attabhāvasaññito dukkhabhāro mayā ettakaṃ kālaṃ vahito, idāni pana na vahitabbo, etassa avahanatthaṃ cirataraṃ kālaṃ ariyamaggasambhāro sambhato, svāyaṃ ariyamaggo paṭividdho, yato ime kāyādayo asubhādito sabhāvādito sammadeva pariññātāti catubbidhampi satiṃ yathātathaṃ visaye suṭṭhu upaṭṭhitaṃ katvā. Ñāṇena paricchinditvāti yasmā imassa attabhāvasaññitassa dukkhabhārassa vahane payojanabhūtaṃ attahitaṃ bodhimūle eva parisamāpitaṃ, parahitaṃ pana buddhaveneyyavinayanaṃ parisamāpitaṃ matthakappattaṃ, taṃ dāni māsattayeneva parisamāpanaṃ pāpuṇissati, tasmā āha “visākhapuṇṇamāyaṃ parinibbāyissāmī”ti, evaṃ buddhañāṇena paricchinditvā saccabhāgena vinicchayaṃ katvā. Āyusaṅkhāraṃ vissajīti āyuno jīvitassa abhisaṅkharaṇaṃ phalasamāpattidhammaṃ na samāpajjissāmīti vissaji, taṃ vissajjaneneva tena abhisaṅkharīyamānaṃ jīvitasaṅkhāraṃ na pavattayissāmīti vissaji. Tenāha “tatthā”tiādi.

Having firmly established mindfulness—this division of the body and so forth, designated as individual existence, this burden of suffering has been borne by me for such a long time. Now, however, it is not to be borne. For the sake of not bearing it, the accumulation for the noble path has been prepared for a very long time. This noble path has been fully realized, whereby this body and other aggregates have been thoroughly comprehended as being foul in nature and as they truly are. Thus, the fourfold mindfulness has been well-established in its proper domain, just as it is. Having delimited with knowledge—because the personal welfare, which was the purpose of bearing this burden of suffering designated as individual existence, has been fully accomplished at the root of enlightenment, and the welfare of others through the Buddha’s instruction of those worthy of training, has reached its culmination. Now, in just three months, that accomplishment will be attained. Therefore, he declared, 'On the full moon day of Visākha, I will attain final Nibbāna.' Thus, having delimited with the Buddha’s knowledge and made a decisive judgment based on truth. He relinquished the life-sustaining volition—he relinquished it by resolving not to enter upon the attainment of fruition, a state which sustains life. By that very relinquishment, he relinquished the life-volition that is sustained by it, resolving not to let it continue. Hence it is said, 'Therein,' and so on.

Ṭhānamahantatāyapi pavattiākāramahantatāyapi mahanto pathavīkampo. Tattha ṭhānamahantatāya bhūmicālassa mahantataṃ dassetuṃ “tadā…pe… akampitthā”ti vuttaṃ, sā pana jātikhettabhūtā dasasahassī lokadhātu eva, na yā kāci. Yā mahābhinīhāramahājātiādīsupi akampittha, tadāpi [V 2.0210] tattakāya eva kampane kiṃ kāraṇaṃ? Jātikhettabhāvena tasseva ādito pariggahassa katattā, pariggahaṇañcassa dhammatāvasena veditabbaṃ. Tathā hi purimabuddhānampi tattakameva jātikhettaṃ ahosi. Tathā hi vuttaṃ “dasasahassī lokadhātu nissaddā hoti nirākulā…pe… mahāsamuddo ābhujati, dasasahassī pakampatī”ti ca ādi. Udakapariyantaṃ katvā chappakārappavedhanena. Avītarāge bhiṃsetīti bhiṃsano, so eva bhiṃsanakoti āha “bhayajanako”ti. Devabheriyoti devadundubhisaddassa pariyāyavacanamattaṃ, na cettha kāci bherī devadundubhīti adhippetā, atha kho uppātabhāvena labbhamāno ākāsato nigghosasaddo. Tenāha “devo”tiādi. Devoti megho. Tassa hi tadā acchabhāvena ākāsassa vassābhāvena sukkhagajjitasaññite [M 2.0509] sadde niccharante devadundubhisamaññā. Tenāha “devo sukkhagajjitaṃ gajjī”ti.

By the greatness of its location and by the greatness of its manifestation, the earthquake is great. Therein, to show the greatness of the earthquake in terms of its location, it is said: 'At that time... it shook.' That, however, refers to the ten-thousandfold world-system, which is the field of birth, not just any other. Even on the occasions of the Great Renunciation, the Great Birth, etc., when it shook, why is it that only that much shook? Because from the beginning, it was taken as the field of birth, and its inclusion should be understood according to its inherent nature. For even for the previous Buddhas, the field of birth was just that much. Thus it is said: 'The ten-thousandfold world-system became silent and undisturbed... the great ocean surged, the ten-thousandfold world-system quaked,' and so on. It reached the limit of water, with six kinds of trembling. 'It terrifies those who are not free from lust'—thus it is terrifying; that very terrifying thing is called 'fear-inspiring.' 'The divine drum' is just a synonym for the sound of the divine drum; here no actual drum called the divine drum is intended, but rather the sound arising from the sky as a portent. Therefore it is said: 'The divine,' etc. 'Divine' means a cloud. For at that time, due to the clearness of the sky and the absence of rain, the sound resembling dry thunder was emitted, which was called the divine drum. Therefore it is said: 'The divine one thundered with dry thunder.'

Pītivegavissaṭṭhanti “evaṃ ciratarakālaṃ vahito ayaṃ attabhāvasaññito dukkhabhāro, dāni na cirasseva nikkhipissāmī”ti sañjātasomanasso bhagavā sabhāveneva pītivegavissaṭṭhaṃ udānaṃ udāneti, evaṃ udānentena ayampi attho sādhito hotīti dassanatthaṃ aṭṭhakathāyaṃ “kasmā”tiādi vuttaṃ.

'Overcome by the surge of joy' means that, thinking, 'For so long this burden of suffering, regarded as individual existence, has been carried. Now, very soon, I will lay it down,' the Blessed One, filled with gladness, naturally uttered this inspired utterance of joy. By uttering this, this meaning is also established. To explain this, the commentary says, 'Why?' and so on.

Tulīyatīti tulanti tula-saddo kammasādhanoti dassetuṃ “tulita”nti vuttaṃ. Appānubhāvatāya paricchinnaṃ. Tathā hi taṃ paṭipakkhena parito khaṇḍitabhāvena parittanti vuccati. Paṭipakkhavikkhambhanato dīghasantānatāya vipulaphalatāya ca na tulaṃ na paricchinnaṃ. Yehi kāraṇehi pubbe avisesato “mahaggataṃ atula”nti vuttaṃ, tāni kāraṇāni rūpāvacarato āruppassa sātisayaṃ vijjantīti arūpāvacaraṃ atulanti vuttaṃ, itarañca tulanti. Appavipākanti tīsupi kammesu yaṃ appavipākaṃ hīnaṃ, taṃ tulaṃ. Bahuvipākanti yaṃ mahāvipākaṃ paṇītaṃ, taṃ atulaṃ. Yaṃ panettha majjhimaṃ, taṃ hīnaṃ ukkaṭṭhanti dvidhā bhinditvā dvīsu bhāgesu pakkhipitabbaṃ. Hīnattikavaṇṇanāyaṃ vuttanayeneva appabahuvipākataṃ niddhāretvā tassa vasena tulātulabhāvo veditabbo. Sambhavati etasmāti sambhavoti āha “sambhava^ hetubhūta”nti. Niyakajjhattaratoti sasantānadhammesu vipassanāvasena gocarāsevanāya ca rato. Savipākaṃ samānaṃ pavattivipākamattadāyikammaṃ savipākaṭṭhena sambhavaṃ, na ca taṃ kāmādi^ bhavābhisaṅkhārakanti tato visesanatthaṃ sambhavanti vatvā “bhavasaṅkhāra”nti vuttaṃ. Ossajīti ariyamaggena avassaji. Kavacaṃ viya attabhāvaṃ pariyonandhitvā ṭhitaṃ attani sambhūtattā attasambhavaṃ [V 2.0211] kilesañca abhindīti kilesabhedasahabhāvikammossajjanaṃ dassento tadubhayassa kāraṇamāha “ajjhattarato samāhito”ti.

“It is called ‘weighed’ (tulita) to show that the word ‘tula’ (balance) is derived in the passive sense. It is limited due to its slight power. Thus, because it is surrounded by its opposite, being fragmented, it is called ‘limited’ (paritta). Due to the suppression of its opposite, its long continuity, and its abundant fruit, it is neither weighed nor limited. The reasons for which it was previously stated without distinction that ‘the exalted is incomparable’ (mahaggataṃ atula)—those reasons are found to an even greater extent in the formless realm than in the form realm. Therefore, the formless realm is called ‘incomparable’ (atula), while the other is called ‘weighed’ (tula). That which has little result, being inferior among the three types of kamma, is ‘weighed.’ That which has great result, being superior, is ‘incomparable.’ But what is intermediate here should be divided into two parts, inferior and superior, and placed into these two categories. Following the method stated in the explanation of the threefold inferiority, the distinction between ‘weighed’ and ‘incomparable’ should be understood by determining whether the result is little or abundant. ‘It arises from this’—thus, it is called ‘origin’ (sambhava), meaning ‘being the cause.’ ‘Delighting inwardly’ means delighting in the practice of insight meditation and in having the phenomena of one’s own continuum as its domain. Kamma that yields merely the result of maintaining continuity, being ‘with result’ (savipāka), is ‘origin’ (sambhava). But it is not the volitional formations of existence (bhavābhisaṅkhāra) like those of the sensual realm and so forth. To distinguish it from that, after stating ‘origin,’ it is called ‘formations of existence’ (bhavasaṅkhāra). ‘Released’ (ossaji) means released by the Noble Path. Just as armor envelops the body, so too the defilements, having arisen in the self, remain enveloping the self; therefore, they are called ‘self-originated’ (attasambhava). And it shatters the defilements—thus, to show the abandoning of kamma that accompanies the destruction of defilements, the cause of both is stated: ‘delighting inwardly, composed.’

Paṭhamavikappe avasajjanameva vuttaṃ. Ettha avasajjanākāroti taṃ dassento “atha vā”tiādimāha. Tattha tīrentoti “uppādo bhayaṃ, anuppādo khema”ntiādinā vīmaṃsanto. Tulento tīrentotiādinā saṅkhepato vuttamatthaṃ vitthārato dassetuṃ “pañcakkhandhā”tiādiṃ vatvā bhavasaṅkhārassa avasajjanākāraṃ sarūpato dasseti. Evantiādinā [M 2.0510] pana udānagāthāvaṇṇanāyaṃ ādito vuttamatthaṃ nigamanavasena dasseti. Abhītabhāvañāpanatthañcāti ayampi attho saṅgahitoti daṭṭhabbaṃ.

In the first alternative, only abandonment is mentioned. Here, to show the manner of abandonment, it begins with ‘or else’ (atha vā), and so on. There, ‘tīrento’ means investigating with ‘arising is fear, non-arising is security,’ and so on. To explain in detail the meaning stated concisely with ‘weighing, investigating,’ and so on, having stated ‘the five aggregates,’ and so forth, it shows the manner of abandoning the formations of existence in its own nature. Moreover, with ‘thus,’ and so on, it shows, by way of conclusion, the meaning stated from the beginning in the commentary on the Udāna verses. And this meaning, which makes known the state of being fearless, should also be understood as included.

Cāpālavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Cāpāla Chapter is concluded.

2. Pāsādakampanavaggo

2. The Chapter on the Shaking of the Mansion

1-2. Pubbasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-2. Commentary on the Previous Discourses, and so on.

823-824. Parato imasmiṃ pāsādakampanavagge dasamasutte āvi bhavissanti. Chaabhiññāpādakāti channaṃ abhiññānaṃ pādakabhūtā padhānabhūtā. Yathā paṭhamasutte, tathā dutiyatatiyasuttesupi ca chaabhiññāpādakā iddhipādā kathitāti attho.

823-824. Further on, in the tenth discourse of this Chapter on the Shaking of the Mansion, they will become manifest. ‘Chaabhiññāpādakā’ means the foundations or principal factors for the six direct knowledges. As in the first discourse, so too in the second and third discourses, the bases of spiritual power (iddhipāda) are said to be the foundations for the six direct knowledges—this is the meaning.

3. Chandasamādhisuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on Concentration Born of Desire

825. Yo samādhissa nissayabhūto chando, so idhādhippetoti āha “chandanti kattukamyatāchanda”nti. Tassa ca adhipateyyaṭṭho nissayaṭṭho, na vinā taṃ chandaṃ nissāyāti āha – “nissāyāti nissayaṃ katvā, adhipatiṃ katvāti attho”ti. Padhānabhūtāti seṭṭhabhūtā, seṭṭhabhāvo ca ekassapi catukiccasādhanavasena pavattiyā, tato eva bahuvacananiddeso, padhānasaṅkhāraṭṭhena padhānasaṅkharaṇato. “Chandaṃ ce nissāya…pe… ayaṃ vuccati chandasamādhī”ti imāya pāḷiyā chandādhipati samādhi chandasamādhīti adhipatisaddalopaṃ katvā samāso vuttoti viññāyati, adhipatisaddatthadassanavasena vā chandahetuko, chandādhiko vā samādhi chandasamādhi. Tena “chandasamādhinā ceva padhānasaṅkhārehi padhānabhūtasaṅkhārehi ca samannāgatā”ti vakkhati, taṃ ānetvā [V 2.0212] sambandho. Padhānabhūtāti vīriyabhūtā. Keci vadanti – “saṅkhatasaṅkhārādinivattanatthaṃ padhānaggahaṇa”nti. Atha vā taṃ taṃ visesaṃ saṅkharotīti saṅkhāro, sabbampi vīriyaṃ. Tattha catukiccasādhakato tadaññassa nivattanatthaṃ padhānaggahaṇanti. Yathā chando chandasamādhinā ceva padhānasaṅkhārehi ca samannāgato, evaṃ chandasamādhi chandena ceva padhānasaṅkhārehi ca samannāgato. Padhānasaṅkhārāpi chandena [M 2.0511] ceva chandasamādhinā ca samannāgatāti tīsupi padesu samannāgatasaddo yojetabbo. Tasmāti yasmā tayopi chandādayo ekacittuppādapariyāpannā, tasmā sabbe te dhammā ekato katvā “ayaṃ vuccati…pe… iddhipādo”ti vuttanti. Evaṃ chandādīnaṃyeva cettha iddhipādabhāvo vutto, vibhaṅge pana tesaṃ iddhibhāvo sampayuttānaṃ iddhipādabhāvo vuttoti dassento “iddhipādavibhaṅge panā”tiādimāha.

825. The desire that serves as the foundation for concentration is what is intended here, as stated: ‘Desire means the desire to act.’ Its role as a governing factor is that of a foundation; it is not possible without relying on that desire—hence it is said: ‘Relying means making it a foundation, making it a governing factor.’ ‘Being the chief’ means being the foremost. And this foremost status arises from its operation in accomplishing the fourfold task even in a single instance. Hence, the plural form is used, signifying the function as a chief formation, as it is a chief conditioner. The phrase, ‘If one relies on desire… this is called concentration based on desire,’ indicates that concentration dominated by desire is termed ‘concentration based on desire’ by eliding the word ‘dominated’ in the compound, so it is understood. Alternatively, considering the meaning of the word ‘dominated,’ it can be understood as concentration caused by desire or concentration predominated by desire. Therefore, it will be said: ‘Endowed with concentration based on desire and with the striving-formations, which are the chief formations.’ This is brought in to establish the connection. ‘Chief’ here means energy. Some say: ‘The term ‘chief’ is used to distinguish it from conditioned formations and so forth.’ Alternatively, a formation is so called because it shapes specific qualities, and all energy is a formation. Here, the term ‘chief’ is used because it accomplishes the fourfold task and excludes what is other than that. Just as desire is endowed with concentration based on desire and the striving-formations, so too is concentration based on desire endowed with desire and the striving-formations. Likewise, the striving-formations are endowed with desire and concentration based on desire. Thus, the term ‘endowed’ should be applied in all three instances. Therefore, because the three—desire and the others—are all included in a single thought-moment, all these phenomena are grouped together and called ‘this is called… the basis of spiritual power.’ Thus, here, only the role of desire and the others as bases of spiritual power is explained. However, in the Vibhaṅga, their role as spiritual power and the role of their associated factors as bases of spiritual power are stated. To show this, it is said: ‘But in the Vibhaṅga of the Bases of Spiritual Power…’ and so on.

Idāni nesaṃ iddhipādatāpi sambhavatīti dassento “apicā”tiādimāha. Tattha chandañhi bhāvayato padhānaṃ katvā bhāventassa tathā pavattapubbābhisaṅkhāravasena ijjhamāno chando iddhi nāma, tassa nissayabhūtā padhānasaṅkhārā iddhipādo nāma. Sesadvayepi eseva nayo. Tathā bhāvayantassa mukhyatāmattaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, ijjhanattho pana sabbesaṃ samānanti dassento “sampayutta…pe… ijjhantiyevā”ti āha.

Now, to show that their status as bases of spiritual power is also possible, he begins with ‘moreover’ (apicā), and so on. Here, for one who develops desire, making it primary and cultivating it, the desire that succeeds due to the influence of previously enacted volitional formations is called ‘spiritual power’ (iddhi). The striving-formations that serve as its foundation are called ‘bases of spiritual power’ (iddhipāda). The same method applies to the other two. It is stated thus with reference to the mere prominence of one who cultivates in this way, but to show that the meaning of success is common to all, he says: ‘associated… indeed, they succeed.’

Iddhipāde asaṅkarato dassetuṃ “apicā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha chandādayoti chandasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārā. Sesiddhipādesūti vīriyiddhipādādīsu. Tattha vīriyasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgatoti dvikkhattuṃ vīriyaṃ āgataṃ, tattha purimaṃ samādhivisesaṃ, vīriyādhipatisamādhi eva vīriyasamādhīti dutiyaṃ samannāgamaṅgadassanaṃ. Dveyeva hi sabbattha samannāgamaṅgāni samādhi padhānasaṅkhāro ca, chandādayo samādhivisesanāni, padhānasaṅkhāro pana padhānavacaneneva visesito, na chandādīhīti na idha vīriyādhipatitā padhānasaṅkhārassa vuttā hoti. Vīriyañca samādhiṃ visesetvā ṭhitameva samannāgamaṅgavasena padhānasaṅkhāravacanena vuttanti nāpi dvīhi vīriyehi samannāgamo vutto hoti. Yasmā pana chandādīhi visiṭṭho samādhi, tathāpi visiṭṭheneva ca tena sampayutto padhānasaṅkhāro sesadhammā ca, tasmā samādhivisesanānaṃ vasena cattāro iddhipādā vuttā, visesanabhāvo ca chandādīnaṃ taṃtaṃavassayadassanavasena hotīti “chandasamādhi…pe… iddhipādo”ti ettha nissayatthepi pāda-sadde upādāyaṭṭhena chandādīnaṃ iddhipādatā vuttā hoti, teneva abhidhamme uttaracūḷabhājaniye “cattāro iddhipādā chandiddhipādo”tiādinā (vibha. 457) chandādīnaṃyeva iddhipādatā vuttā, pañhapucchake ca – “cattāro iddhipādā idha bhikkhu chandasamādhī”tiādināva (vibha. 431) uddesaṃ katvāpi puna [V 2.0213] chandādīnaṃyeva kusalādibhāvo [M 2.0512] vibhatto. Upāyiddhipādadassanatthameva hi nissayiddhipādadassanaṃ kataṃ, aññathā catubbidhatā na hotīti ayamettha pāḷivasena atthavinicchayo. Tattha upāyiddhipādadassanatthamevāti chandādike dhure jeṭṭhake pubbaṅgame katvā nibbattitasamādhi chandādhipatisamādhīti chandādīnaṃ iddhiyā adhigamūpāyadassanaṃ upāyiddhipādadassanaṃ, tadatthameva “tathābhūtassa vedanākkhandho…pe… viññāṇakkhandho”ti tattha tattha pāḷiyaṃ nissayiddhipādadassanaṃ kataṃ chandādivisiṭṭhānaṃyeva vedanākkhandhādīnaṃ adhippetattā. Evañcetaṃ sampaṭicchitabbaṃ, aññathā kevalaṃ iddhisampayuttānaṃyeva khandhānaṃ vasena iddhipādabhāve gayhamāne tesaṃ catubbidhatā na hoti visesakāraṇabhāvatoti adhippāyo.

To explain the bases of spiritual power without confusion, the phrase ‘moreover’ (apicā) and so on is stated. Herein, ‘desire, etc.’ means concentration based on desire and the striving-formations. In the remaining bases of spiritual power, such as the energy base of spiritual power, and so on, ‘endowed with energy-concentration and the striving-formations’ means energy is mentioned twice. The first instance refers to a specific concentration—concentration dominated by energy is indeed energy-concentration—while the second shows the factor of endowment. Indeed, in all cases, the factors of endowment are two: concentration and the striving-formations. Desire and the rest are specifications of concentration. However, the striving-formations are specified by the term ‘striving’ itself, not by desire and the like. Therefore, here, the dominance of energy is not stated as a specification of the striving-formations. And energy, having specified concentration, remains stated as a factor of endowment through the term ‘striving-formations.’ Thus, it is not stated that one is endowed with two instances of energy. Since concentration is distinguished by desire and the like, and since the striving-formations and other associated phenomena are also associated with that distinguished concentration, therefore, the four bases of spiritual power are stated through the distinctions of concentration. And the distinguishing nature of desire and the like arises from showing their respective necessities. Thus, in ‘concentration based on desire… the basis of spiritual power,’ here, even in the sense of dependence, by taking the word ‘pāda’ in the sense of foundation, the status of desire and the like as bases of spiritual power is stated. For this very reason, in the Abhidhamma, in the Uttara Cūḷabhājaniya, it is said: ‘The four bases of spiritual power: the desire base of spiritual power…’ (Vibhaṅga 457), where the status of desire and the like as bases of spiritual power is stated. And in the question-and-answer section, after making the recitation: ‘The four bases of spiritual power: here, a monk [develops] concentration based on desire…’ (Vibhaṅga 431), the wholesome and other qualities of desire and the like are further analyzed. Indeed, the explanation of the dependent basis of spiritual power is made solely for the purpose of showing the instrumental basis of spiritual power. Otherwise, the fourfold nature would not exist. This is the analysis of meaning here according to the text. Herein, ‘for the purpose of showing the instrumental basis of spiritual power’ means that concentration, arising with desire and the like as the leading, foremost, and preceding factors, is called ‘concentration dominated by desire.’ Thus, showing the means of achieving spiritual power through desire and the like is showing the instrumental basis of spiritual power. For this very purpose, in the texts, the dependent basis of spiritual power is explained in phrases like ‘for one thus composed, the aggregate of feeling… the aggregate of consciousness,’ because the aggregates of feeling and the like are understood as those distinguished by desire and the like. This should be accepted thus; otherwise, if the status as bases of spiritual power were understood merely in terms of the aggregates associated with spiritual power, their fourfold nature would not exist due to the absence of a distinguishing cause. This is the intended meaning.

Kecīti uttaravihāravāsino. Anibbattoti hetupaccayehi na nibbatto, na sabhāvadhammo, paññattimattanti adhippāyo. Vādamaddanatthāya hoti, abhidhamme ca āgato uttaracūḷavāroti yojanā. Cattāro iddhipādātiādi uttaracūḷavāradassanaṃ. Ime pana uttaracūḷavāre āgatā iddhipādā.

‘Some’ (keci) means the residents of the Northern Monastery. ‘Unproduced’ (anibbatto) means not produced by causes and conditions, not a real phenomenon (sabhāvadhammo), but merely a designation (paññattimattaṃ)—this is the meaning. It exists for the purpose of refuting arguments, and the Uttaracūḷavāra appears in the Abhidhamma—this is the connection. ‘The four bases of spiritual power’ (cattāro iddhipādā) and so on is a presentation of the Uttaracūḷavāra. But these bases of spiritual power appear in the Uttaracūḷavāra.

Raṭṭhapālatthero chande sati kathaṃ nānujānissantīti sattāhāni bhattāni abhuñjitvā mātāpitaro anujānāpetvā pabbajitvā chandameva nissāya arahattaṃ pāpuṇīti āha – “raṭṭhapālatthero chandaṃ dhuraṃ katvā lokuttaradhammaṃ nibbattesī”ti. Soṇattheroti sukhumālasoṇatthero. So hi āyasmā attano sukhumālabhāvaṃ acintetvā ativelaṃ caṅkamanena pādesu uṭṭhitesupi ussāhaṃ avissajjento vīriyaṃ dhuraṃ katvā lokuttaradhammaṃ nibbattesi. Sambhutatthero “cittavato ce alamariyañāṇadassanaviseso ijjheyya, mayhaṃ ijjheyyāti cittaṃ dhuraṃ katvā lokuttaradhammaṃ nibbattesi. Mogharājā “paññavato ce maggabhāvanā ijjheyya, mayhaṃ ijjheyyā”ti paññāpubbaṅgamaṃ paññādhuraṃ paññājeṭṭhakaṃ katvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇīti āha “mogharājā vīmaṃsaṃ dhuraṃ katvā lokuttaradhammaṃ nibbattesī”ti. Idāni nesaṃ ariyānaṃ upaṭṭhānussāhamantajātisampadaṃ nissāya rañño santike laddhavisese amaccaputte nidassanabhāvena dassetuṃ “tattha yathā”tiādimāha. Ettha [M 2.0513] ca punappunaṃ chanduppādanaṃ tosanaṃ viya hotīti chandassa upaṭṭhānasadisatā vuttā, thāmabhāvato ca vīriyassa sūrattasadisatā, cintanappadhānattā cittassa mantasaṃvidhānasadisatā, yonisomanasikāra-sambhūtesu kusaladhammesu paññā seṭṭhāti vīmaṃsāya jātisampattisadisatā vuttā.

The Elder Raṭṭhapāla thought, ‘When desire is present, how could they not consent?’ For seven days, without eating food, he persuaded his parents and went forth; relying solely on desire, he attained arahantship. Thus it is said: ‘The Elder Raṭṭhapāla made desire his yoke and produced the supramundane Dhamma.’ The Elder Soṇa refers to the delicate Elder Soṇa. Indeed, this venerable one, not considering his own delicacy, and not giving up effort even when his feet blistered from prolonged walking, made energy his yoke and produced the supramundane Dhamma. The Elder Sambhūta thought, ‘If the special noble knowledge and vision of one with a developed mind were to succeed, may mine succeed.’ He made mind his yoke and produced the supramundane Dhamma. Mogharājā thought, ‘If the path-development of one with wisdom were to succeed, may mine succeed.’ He made wisdom foremost, made wisdom his yoke, and made wisdom the chief, attaining arahantship. Thus it is said: ‘Mogharājā made investigation his yoke and produced the supramundane Dhamma.’ Now, to show as examples these ministers' sons who had attained distinction in the king’s presence on account of their service, effort, counsel, and noble birth, it is said: ‘Therein, just as…’ Here, repeatedly arousing desire is like pleasing; thus, for desire, a similarity to service is stated. Due to its strength, for energy, there is a similarity to bravery. Because of the predominance of thinking, for the mind, there is a similarity to the arrangement of counsel. Because wisdom is foremost among wholesome states born of careful attention, for investigation, a similarity to noble birth is stated.

4. Moggallānasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Moggallāna Sutta

826. Uddhaccapakatikāti [V 2.0214] vikkhittasabhāvā, vibbhantacittāti attho. Anavaṭṭhitatāya vipphanditacittatāya vipphandamānacittā. Tucchatāya naḷo viyāti naḷo, māno, uggato naḷo etesanti unnaḷāti āha – “unnaḷāti…pe… vuttaṃ hotī”ti. Capalāti cāpalyatā nāma lolabhāvo. Murāti kharavacanā, pharusavacanāti attho. Vikiṇṇavācā nāma samphappalāpinoti vuttaṃ “asaṃyatavacanā”tiādi. Paṭipattidhamme pamuṭṭhā vinaṭṭhā paṭivinaṭṭhā sati etesanti muṭṭhasatīti āha – “naṭṭhassatino”ti. Ubbhantacittāti samādhino abhāvena uddhacceneva uparūpari bhantacittā. Pākatindriyā abhāvitakāyatāya gāmadārakā viya pakatibhūtaindriyā. Nemo vuccati bhūmiyā baddhabhāvanimittapadeso, gambhīro nemo etassāti gambhīranemo. Suṭṭhu nikhātotiādi tassa pādassa suppatiṭṭhitabhāvadassanaṃ.

826. ‘Uddhaccapakatikā’ means having a restless nature, signifying a bewildered mind. Due to instability and an agitated mind, their minds are wavering. ‘Naḷo’ (reed) due to emptiness, like a reed; or pride. ‘Unnaḷā’ means those for whom a reed has risen—hence, it is said: ‘unnaḷā… as stated… is said.’ ‘Capalā’ denotes fickleness, a restless nature. ‘Murā’ means harsh speech, that is, rough words. ‘Vikiṇṇavācā’ refers to those who engage in frivolous talk, as stated: ‘unrestrained speech,’ etc. Those whose mindfulness regarding the practice of the Dhamma is confused, lost, or utterly lost are called ‘muṭṭhasatī’—thus it is said: ‘those who have lost mindfulness.’ ‘Ubbhantacittā’ means a mind that repeatedly wanders due to restlessness from an absence of concentration. ‘Pākatindriyā’ means having faculties in their natural state, like village children, due to an undeveloped physical and mental composure. ‘Nemo’ is said to be the place on the ground where the meditation sign is bound; ‘gambhīranemo’ means one for whom this is deep. ‘Suṭṭhu nikhāto’ (well-dug in), and so on, demonstrates the firmly established nature of that foot.

5. Uṇṇābhabrāhmaṇasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Uṇṇābhabrāhmaṇa Sutta

827. Pahānatthanti anuppādapahānatthaṃ.

827. ‘For the purpose of abandoning’ means for the purpose of abandoning through non-arising.

9. Iddhādidesanāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Iddhādidesanā Sutta

831. Abhiññāpādakaṃ catutthajjhānaṃ adhippetaṃ “iddhilābhāya pavattatī”ti vacanato.

831. The fourth jhāna, being the basis for higher knowledge, is intended, according to the statement, ‘It leads to the attainment of psychic powers.’

10. Vibhaṅgasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Vibhaṅga Sutta

832. Chandaṃ uppādetvāti bhāvanāchandaṃ uppādetvā. Līnākāroti bhāvanācittassa layāpatti. Kosajjena vokiṇṇāpajjanaṃ vuttaṃ.

832. ‘Having aroused desire’ means having aroused the desire for meditation. ‘A state of sinking’ refers to the meditative mind falling into sluggishness. It refers to falling into a state scattered by laziness.

Evaṃ [M 2.0514] pasādābhāvena cittassa vikkhepāpatti, tattha pasāduppādanena yaṃ sampahaṃsanaṃ icchitabbaṃ, tassa uppādanākāraṃ dassetuṃ vuttaṃ “so buddhadhammasaṅghaguṇe āvajjetvā”tiādi. Vatthukāme ārabbha vikkhitto punappunaṃ vikkhitto hotiyevāti vuttaṃ “anuvikkhitto”ti. Uppathaṃ paṭipannassa cittassa daṇḍanaṭṭhena niggaṇhanaṭṭhena suttāni eva daṇḍoti suttadaṇḍo, tena suttadaṇḍena [V 2.0215] cittaṃ tajjetvā. Pañcakāmaguṇe ārabbha pavatto cittavikkhepo punappunaṃ uppajjatevāti vuttaṃ “anuvikkhitto anuvisaṭo”ti.

Thus, due to a lack of serenity, the mind falls into distraction. In that case, to show the way to generate the delight that should be sought by producing serenity, it is said: ‘He, reflecting on the virtues of the Buddha, Dhamma, and Saṅgha,’ etc. ‘Anuvikkhitto’ means repeatedly distracted; one is indeed repeatedly distracted on account of sensual objects. For a mind that has gone astray, the suttas are a rod, in the sense of chastising and restraining; therefore, `suttadaṇḍo` is the rod of the suttas. Having threatened the mind with that rod of the suttas. ‘Anuvikkhitto anuvisaṭo’ means the mental distraction that occurs on account of the five strands of sensual pleasure repeatedly arises and spreads.

Purepacchābhāvo kammaṭṭhānassa manasikāravasena uggahavasena vāti tadubhayaṃ dassetuṃ “katha”ntiādi vuttaṃ. Atilīnādīsu catūsu ṭhānesūti atilīnātipaggahitasaṃkhittaanuvikkhittasaññitesu catūsu ṭhānesu. Tattha bhāvanaṃ anajjhogāhetvāva saṅkoco atilīnatā, ajjhogāhetvā anto saṅkoco saṃkhittatā, antosaṅkhepo atipaggahitatā, accāraddhavīriyatā anuvikkhittatā. Bahiddhā visamavitakkānubhāvanaṃ āpajjanto dvattiṃsākāravasena aṭṭhikasaññāvasena vā gahetabbanti āha – “sarīravasena veditabba”nti. Tenāha “uddhaṃ pādatalā”tiādi (dī. ni. 2.377; ma. ni. 1.110).

To show both the preceding and succeeding stages of the meditation subject, according to attention and grasping, it is said: ‘How,’ etc. In the four states beginning with ‘overly lax’—that is, the four states termed overly lax (`atilīna`), overly energetic (`atipaggahita`), contracted (`saṃkhitta`), and scattered (`anuvikkhitta`). Herein, contraction without having properly entered into the meditation is overly laxity (`atilīnatā`); internal contraction after having entered is contraction (`saṃkhittatā`); internal briefness is identified with over-exertion (`atipaggahitatā`); and excessive application of energy is identified with scatteredness (`anuvikkhittatā`). When one falls into experiencing external, uneven thoughts, he says it should be grasped by way of the thirty-two parts or the perception of bones, stating: ‘It should be understood by way of the body.’ Therefore, he said, ‘From the soles of the feet upwards,’ etc. (Dī. Ni. 2.377; Ma. Ni. 1.110).

Aññamaññaṃ asaṅkarato ākirīyanti pakārato ṭhapīyantīti ākārā, bhāgāti āha – “yehi ākārehīti yehi koṭṭhāsehī”ti. Liṅgīyati sallakkhīyatīti liṅgaṃ, saṇṭhānaṃ. Nimīyati niddhāretvā paricchindīyatīti nimittaṃ, upaṭṭhānaṃ. Yo bhikkhūtiādi ālokasaññaṃ yo uggaṇhāti, taṃ dassanaṃ. Aṅgaṇeti vivaṭaṅgaṇe. Ālokasaññaṃ manasikaroti rattiyaṃ. Vīriyādīsupīti yathā “idha bhikkhu chandaṃ uppādetvā”ti chande vitthāranayo vutto, vīriyādīsupi eso eva vitthāranayo yojetabbo.

‘Ākārā’ (aspects) means they are strewn about and established in a certain way, not mixed with one another; they are parts. Thus it is said: ‘by which aspects’ means ‘by which divisions.’ ‘Liṅgaṃ’ (characteristic) is what is marked and observed; it is the configuration. ‘Nimittaṃ’ (sign) is what is measured, defined after having been ascertained; it is the appearance. The passage beginning ‘Whatever monk,’ etc., is to show one who grasps the perception of light. ‘Aṅgaṇe’ means in an open courtyard. One attends to the perception of light at night. As for energy, etc.—just as in, ‘Here, a monk generates desire,’ the detailed method is stated for desire, so this same detailed method should be applied to energy and the others.

Pāsādakampanavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Pāsādakampana Vagga is concluded.

3. Ayoguḷavaggo

3. The Chapter on the Iron Ball

2. Ayoguḷasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on the Iron Ball

834. Iminā catumahābhūtamayenāti iminā sabbalokapaccakkhena catūhi mahābhūtehi nibbattena catumahābhūtamayena. Manomayo pana nimmitakāyo [M 2.0515] bhagavato rucivasena paresaṃ paccakkho hoti. Omātīti avamāti. Ava-pubbo hi mā-saddo sattiatthopi hotīti “pahoti sakkotī”ti attho vutto. Asambhinnapadanti asādhāraṇapadaṃ aññattha anāgatattā. Kāyassa [V 2.0216] citte samodahanaṃ āropanaṃ tannissitatākaraṇañca atthato cittagatiyā pavattanamevāti āha “cittagatiyā pesetī”ti.

834. ‘By this, made of the four great elements’: by this, which is manifest to the whole world, produced from the four great elements, made of the four great elements. The mind-made, created body, however, is manifest to others according to the Blessed One’s wish. ‘Omāti’ means `avamāti` (he despises). For the word `mā` preceded by `ava` also has the meaning of ‘ability’; thus, the meaning ‘he is able, he is capable’ is stated. ‘Asambhinnapadaṃ’ (unmixed phrase) means an uncommon term, because of not occurring elsewhere. The placing of the body into the mind, the imposing of it, and the making it dependent on it is, in essence, simply proceeding according to the mind's movement. Hence, it is said, ‘He directs it according to the movement of the mind.’

Tattha cittagatigamanaṃ nāma cittavasena kāyassa pariṇāmanena “ayaṃ kāyo imaṃ cittaṃ viya hotū”ti kāyassa cittena samānagatikatāṭhapanaṃ. Kathaṃ pana kāyo dandhapavattiko lahupavattinā cittena samānagatiko hotīti? Na sabbathā samānagatiko. Yatheva hi kāyavasena cittapariṇāmane cittaṃ sabbathā kāyena samānagatikaṃ na hoti. Na hi kadāci taṃ sabhāvasiddhena attano khaṇena avattitvā dandhavuttikassa rūpadhammassa vasena pavattituṃ sakkoti, “idaṃ cittaṃ ayaṃ kāyo viya hotū”ti pana adhiṭṭhānena dandhagatikassa kāyassa anuvattanato yāva icchitaṭṭhānappatti, tāva kāyagatianulomeneva hutvā santānavasena pavattamānaṃ cittaṃ kāyagatiyā pariṇāmitaṃ nāma hoti. Evaṃ “ayaṃ kāyo idaṃ cittaṃ viya hotū”ti adhiṭṭhānena pageva sukhalahusaññāya sampāditattā abhāvitiddhipādānaṃ viya dandhaṃ avattitvā yathā lahukaṃ katipayacittavāreneva icchitaṭṭhānappatti hoti, evaṃ pavattamāno kāyo cittagatiyā pariṇāmito nāma hoti, na ekacittakkhaṇeneva icchitaṭṭhānappattiyā, evañca katvā bāhāsamiñjanapasāraṇūpamāpi upacārena vinā suṭṭhutaraṃ yuttā hotīti. Aññathā dhammatāvilomatā siyā, nāpi dhammānaṃ lakkhaṇaññathattaṃ iddhibalena kātuṃ sakkā, bhāvaññathattameva pana kātuṃ sakkāti.

Here, ‘movement according to the mind’ means, through transforming the body by means of the mind, the establishing of the body’s movement in accordance with the mind, with the thought, ‘May this body be like this mind.’ But how can the body, which is of slow movement, move in accordance with the mind, which is of swift movement? It does not move entirely in accordance with it. Just as, when the mind is transformed by means of the body, the mind does not move entirely in accordance with the body. For it is never able to proceed according to the slow-moving material phenomenon by failing to occur in its own naturally established moment. However, through the determination, ‘May this mind be like this body,’ the mind, conforming to the body’s slow movement until the desired destination is reached, is said to be transformed by the body’s movement as it proceeds in a continuous series. Similarly, by the determination, ‘May this body be like this mind,’ and due to having previously produced a perception of ease and lightness, the body reaches the desired destination swiftly in just a few turns of mind, not proceeding slowly like those who have not developed the bases of psychic power. Thus, the body is said to be transformed by the movement of the mind, not by reaching the desired destination in a single mind-moment. And so, the simile of bending and stretching the arm is more fitting, even without figurative speech. Otherwise, it would be contrary to the natural order of things; nor can the inherent characteristics of phenomena be altered by psychic power, but only their state can be altered.

3-10. Bhikkhusuttādivaṇṇanā

3–10. Commentary on the Bhikkhu Discourse and others.

835-842. “Dvinnaṃ phalāna”nti āgatasuttaṃ sandhāya “dve phalāni ādiṃ katvā”ti vuttaṃ. Sesaṃ suviññeyyameva.

835–842. ‘Of two fruits’ (dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ) means it is said ‘beginning with two fruits’ (dve phalāni ādiṃ katvā), referring to the cited discourse. The rest is easily understood.

Ayoguḷavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on the Iron Ball is concluded.

Iddhipādasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on the Bases for Spiritual Power is concluded.

8. Anuruddhasaṃyuttaṃ

8. The Connected Discourses on Anuruddha

1. Rahogatavaggo

1. The Chapter on Seclusion

1-2. Paṭhamarahogatasuttādivaṇṇanā

1–2. Commentary on the First Discourse on Seclusion, etc.

899-900. Chattiṃsāya [M 2.0516][V 2.0217] ṭhānesūti ajjhattaṃ kāye samudayadhammānupassī, vayo, samudayavayo, bahiddhā samudayo, vayo, samudayavayo, ajjhattabahiddhā samudayo, vayo, samudayavayadhammānupassīti nava anupassanā, tathā vedanāya citte dhammesūti evaṃ chattiṃsāya ṭhānesu. Dutiye dvādasasu ṭhānesūti kāye ajjhattaṃ bahiddhā ajjhattabahiddhā, vedanāya citte dhammesūti evaṃ dvādasasu ṭhānesu.

899–900. ‘In thirty-six instances’ (chattiṃsāya ṭhānesu): internally, contemplating the body as subject to arising, vanishing, and both arising and vanishing; externally, as subject to arising, vanishing, and both arising and vanishing; internally and externally, as subject to arising, vanishing, and both arising and vanishing—thus nine contemplations. Similarly for feelings, mind, and phenomena, thus there are thirty-six instances. In the second, ‘in twelve instances’ (dvādasasu ṭhānesu): in the body internally, externally, and internally and externally; and similarly for feelings, mind, and phenomena—thus there are twelve instances.

3. Sutanusuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Sutanu Discourse

901. Imāya pāḷiyāti imāya hīnattikapāḷiyā. Ime dhammā hīnā lāmakaṭṭhena. Ime dhammā majjhimā hīnapaṇītānaṃ majjhe bhavāti. Uttamaṭṭhena attappakaṭṭhena padhānabhāvaṃ nītāti paṇītā.

901. ‘By this Pāli text’ (imāya pāḷiyā) means by this Pāli text on the inferior triad. These phenomena are inferior due to their base nature. These phenomena are middling, as they exist between the inferior and the excellent. They are ‘excellent’ (paṇītā) because they are brought to a state of prominence by their own pre-eminence, in the sense of being supreme.

4-7. Paṭhamakaṇḍakīsuttādivaṇṇanā

4–7. Commentary on the First Kaṇḍakī Discourse and others.

902-905. Kaṇḍakā etissā atthīti kaṇḍakī, karamandagaccho. Osadhibhāvāpekkhāya itthiliṅganiddeso, tabbahulatāya taṃ vanaṃ “kaṇḍakīvana”nteva paññāyittha. Sahassalokanti sahassacakkavāḷalokaṃ. Dasacakkavāḷasahassaṃ ekāvajjanassa āpāthaṃ āgacchati tathā ālokavaḍḍhanassa katattā.

902–905. ‘Kaṇḍakī’ means ‘it has thorns,’ a karamanda shrub. The feminine gender is indicated with reference to its nature as a medicinal herb; because of its abundance, that forest was known as ‘Kaṇḍakīvana’. ‘A thousand worlds’ (sahassalokaṃ) means the thousandfold world-system. Ten thousand world-systems come within the range of a single advertence, because an increase of light was so produced.

9. Ambapālivanasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Ambapālīvana Discourse

907. Arahattabhāvadīpakanti [V 2.0218] arahattassa atthibhāvadīpakaṃ. “Arahattabhāvadīpika”nti vā pāṭho.

907. ‘Illuminator of the state of arahantship’ (arahattabhāvadīpakaṃ) means that which illuminates the existence of arahantship. Alternatively, the reading may be ‘Arahattabhāvadīpikā’.

Rahogatavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on Seclusion is concluded.

2. Dutiyavaggavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Second Chapter

909-922. Dasabalañāṇanti [M 2.0517] dasavidhabalañāṇaṃ. Ekadesenāti padesavasena. Sāvakānampi attano abhinīhārānurūpaṃ ñāṇaṃ pavattatīti te kālapadesavasena ceva yathāparicayasattapadesavasena ca ṭhānānīti jānanti, sammāsambuddhānaṃ pana anantañāṇatāya sabbattheva appaṭihatameva ñāṇanti āha – “sabbaññubuddhānaṃ panā”tiādi. Etaṃ dasabalañāṇaṃ anantavisayattā nippadesaṃ anūnatāya sabbākāraparipūraṃ.

909–922. ‘Knowledge of the ten powers’ (dasabalañāṇaṃ) refers to the ten kinds of knowledge of power. ‘In part’ (ekadesena) means by way of a portion. The knowledge of the disciples, too, arises in accordance with their aspiration, so they know those instances partially, both in terms of a portion of time and in terms of beings with whom they are familiar. But for the Perfectly Enlightened Buddhas, because their knowledge is infinite, their knowledge is unimpeded everywhere—thus it is said, ‘For the all-knowing Buddhas…,’ and so on. This knowledge of the ten powers, being infinite in scope, is without limitation; being without deficiency, it is complete in every way.

Dutiyavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Second Chapter is concluded.

Anuruddhasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on Anuruddha is concluded.

9. Jhānasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Jhāna Saṃyutta

923. Jhānasaṃyuttaṃ [V 2.0219] suviññeyyameva, missakanti vadanti.

923. The Jhāna Saṃyutta is indeed easily understood; they say it is mixed.

Jhānasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Jhāna Saṃyutta is concluded.

10. Ānāpānasaṃyuttaṃ

10. The Connected Discourses on Mindfulness of Breathing

1. Ekadhammavaggo

1. The Chapter on a Single Thing

1. Ekadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on a Single Thing

977. Etthāti [M 2.0518][V 2.0220] etasmiṃ paṭhamasutte. Vuttameva, tasmā tattha vuttanayeneva veditabbanti adhippāyo.

977. ‘Here’ means in this first discourse. It has indeed been stated; therefore, the intention is that it should be understood in the way stated there.

6. Ariṭṭhasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Ariṭṭha Sutta

982. No-saddo pucchāyaṃ, tasmā nūti iminā samānatthoti āha “bhāvetha nū”ti. Kāmacchandoti vatthukāmesu icchāti āha “pañcakāmaguṇikarāgo”ti. Dvādasasu āyatanadhammesu saparasantatipariyāpannesu. Iminā kāmacchandappahānakittanena paṭighasaññāpaṭivinayakittanena ca anāgāmimaggaṃ katheti pañcorambhāgiyasaññojanasamucchedassa byākatattā. Vipassanaṃ dassentoti “vipassanaṃ anuyuñjathā”ti dassento.

982. The word ‘no’ is interrogative; therefore, it has the same meaning as ‘nū.’ Thus, he says, ‘Should you develop indeed?’ ‘Sensual desire’ (kāmacchanda) means desire for objects of sensual pleasure; thus, he says, ‘lust for the five strands of sensual pleasure.’ This is among the twelve sense-base phenomena, which are included in one's own and others' continuities. By this declaration of the abandonment of sensual desire and the removal of the perception of aversion, he describes the path of the non-returner, since the cutting off of the five lower fetters has been explained. ‘Showing insight’ means showing it by saying, ‘Apply yourselves to insight.’

8. Padīpopamasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Discourse with the Simile of the Lamp

984. “Neva kāyopi kilamati na cakkhūnī”ti aṭṭhakathāyaṃ paduddhāro kato. “Kāyopi kilamati, cakkhūnipi vihaññantī”ti vatvā yattha yathā hoti, tāni dassetuṃ “dhātukammaṭṭhānasmiṃ hī”tiādi vuttaṃ. Cakkhūni phandanti kilamantītiādi ativelaṃ upanijjhāyane hotīti katvā vuttaṃ. Imasmiṃ pana kammaṭṭhāneti ānāpānakammaṭṭhāne. Evamāhāti “bhikkhu cepi ākaṅkheyya, neva kāyo kilameyyā”ti evamāha.

984. “‘Neither does the body tire, nor the eyes’—thus, an extraction of the phrase was made in the commentary. Having said, ‘The body tires, and the eyes are distressed,’ in order to show where each occurs, ‘Indeed, in the meditation subject of the elements,’ etc., was stated. It was stated that ‘the eyes flicker and tire,’ etc., occurs due to excessive contemplation. ‘But in this meditation subject’ means in the meditation subject of mindfulness of breathing. ‘Thus he said’ means he said, ‘Even if a bhikkhu should wish, the body would not tire.’”

Labbhatīti aṭṭhakathādhippāye ṭhatvā vuttaṃ, parato āgatena theravādena so anicchito. Na [V 2.0221] hi tārakarūpamuttāvaḷikādisadisaṃ nimittūpaṭṭhānākāramattaṃ khaṇamattaṭṭhāyinaṃ kasiṇanimittesu viya ugghāṭanaṃ kātuṃ sakkoti. Tenāha “na labbhatevā”ti. Ānisaṃsadassanatthaṃ gahito, ānāpānassatisamādhismiṃ siddhe ayaṃ guṇo sukheneva ijjhatīti. Yasmā bhikkhūti imasmiṃ vāre nāgatanti yathā purimavāre “bhikkhu cepi ākaṅkheyyā”ti [M 2.0519] āgataṃ, evaṃ idha “bhāvite kho, bhikkhave, ānāpānassatisamādhimhī”ti āgatavāre bhikkhuggahaṇamakataṃ, tasmā “so”ti na vuttaṃ.

“‘It is attainable’ is stated from the standpoint of the commentary's intention, but that is not accepted by the later Theravāda tradition. For it is not possible to bring about an ‘opening up’ (ugghāṭana), as in the case of kasiṇa signs, of what is merely the mode of a sign's appearance—lasting only for a moment, like the form of a star, a string of pearls, and so on. Therefore, it is said, ‘It is simply not attainable.’ It is taken up to show the benefit: this quality is easily accomplished when concentration through mindfulness of breathing is perfected. Because ‘bhikkhu’ does not appear in this passage—just as in the previous passage ‘If a bhikkhu should wish’ appeared, so here, in the passage ‘When, bhikkhus, concentration through mindfulness of breathing is developed,’ the word ‘bhikkhu’ is not included—therefore, ‘he’ is not stated.”

9. Vesālīsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Vesālī Sutta

985. Pākāraparikkhepavaḍḍhanenāti pākāraparikkhepena bhūmiyā vaḍḍhanena. Rājagahasāvatthiyo viya idampi ca nagaraṃ…pe… sabbākāravepullataṃ pattaṃ. Anekapariyāyenāti ettha pariyāyasaddo kāraṇavacanoti āha “anekehi kāraṇehī”ti, ayaṃ kāyo aviññāṇakopi saviññāṇakopi evampi asubho evampi asubhoti nānāvidhehi kāraṇehīti attho. Asubhākārasandassanappavattanti kesādivasena tatthāpi vaṇṇādito asubhākārassa sabbaso dassanavasena pavattaṃ. Kāyavicchandanīyakathanti attano parassa ca karajakāye vicchandanuppādanakathaṃ. Muttaṃ vātiādinā byatirekamukhena kāyassa amanuññataṃ dasseti. Tattha ādito tīhi padehi adassanīyatāya asārakatāya ca, majjhe catūhi duggandhatāya, ante ekena lesamattenapi manuññatābhāvamassa dasseti. Atha khotiādinā anvayato sarūpeneva amanuññatāya dassanaṃ. “Kesalomādī”ti saṅkhepato vuttamatthaṃ vibhāgena dassetuṃ “yepī”tiādi vuttaṃ.

985. “‘By the expansion of the surrounding wall’ means by the expansion of the land by means of the surrounding wall. Like Rājagaha and Sāvatthī, this city too...reached full development in every way. ‘In many ways’ (anekapariyāyena)—here, the word ‘pariyāya’ means ‘reason.’ Thus, he says, ‘by many reasons,’ meaning that this body, whether without consciousness or with consciousness, is in this way unattractive, in that way unattractive, for various reasons. ‘Proceeding by showing the aspect of foulness’ (asubhākārasandassanappavattaṃ) means proceeding by way of fully showing the aspect of foulness through hair and so on, and even there, by way of color and so on. ‘A talk that causes revulsion for the body’ (kāyavicchandanīyakathaṃ) means a talk that generates revulsion towards one's own and others' bodies born of kamma. By ‘urine,’ etc., he shows the unpleasing nature of the body by way of exclusion. There, by the first three terms, he shows its unpleasantness to behold and its essencelessness; by the middle four, its foul smell; and by the last one, its lack of pleasingness even in the slightest degree. Then, by ‘but,’ etc., is the showing of its unpleasing nature by way of inclusion, by its very form. To show in detail the meaning briefly stated by ‘hair, body hair,’ etc., ‘whatever,’ etc., was stated.”

Vaṇṇentoti vitthārento. Asubhāyāti asubhamātikāya. Phātikammanti bahulīkāro. Kilesacorehi anabhibhavanīyattā jhānaṃ “cittamañjūsa”nti vuttaṃ. Nissāyāti pādakaṃ katvā.

“‘Describing’ means expounding. ‘On the foul’ (asubhāya) means on the topic of the foul. ‘Abundance’ (phātikamma) means making much of. Because it cannot be overcome by the thieves of the defilements, jhāna is called ‘the mind-casket.’ ‘Relying on’ (nissāya) means having made it the foundation.”

Apare pana “tasmiṃ kira addhamāse na koci buddhaveneyyo ahosi, tasmā bhagavā evamāha – ‘icchāmahaṃ, bhikkhave’tiādī”ti vadanti. Pare kirāti kira-saddo arucisaṃsūcanattho. Tenāha “idaṃ pana icchāmatta”nti.

“But others say, ‘It seems that for half a month there was no one to be trained by the Buddha; therefore, the Blessed One said, “I desire, bhikkhus,” and so on.’ In the phrase ‘Others, it seems’ (pare kira), the word ‘kira’ indicates distaste. Therefore, it is said, ‘This, however, is a mere wish.’”

Anekakāraṇasammissoti ettha kāraṇaṃ nāma kāyassa asuciduggandhajegucchapaṭikūlatāva. Sabbamakaṃsūti puthujjanā nāma sāvajjepi tattha anavajjasaññino hutvā karaṇakārāpanasamanuññatābhedaṃ [V 2.0222] sabbaṃ pāpaṃ akaṃsu[M 2.0520]. Kāmaṃ dasānussatiggahaṇeneva ānāpānassati gahitā, sā pana tattha sannipatitabhikkhūsu bahūnaṃ sappāyā sātthikā ca, tasmā puna gahitā. Tathā hi bhagavā tameva kammaṭṭhānaṃ imasmiṃ sutte kathesi. Āhāre paṭikūlasaññā asubhakammaṭṭhānasadisā, cattāro pana āruppā ādikammikānaṃ ayogyāti tesaṃ idha aggahaṇaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ.

“‘Mixed with many causes’ (anekakāraṇasammissa)—here, ‘cause’ means the impurity, foul smell, disgustingness, and repulsiveness of the body. ‘They did all’ (sabbam akaṃsu)—ordinary people, perceiving what is blameworthy as blameless, committed all evil, distinguished as doing, causing to be done, and approving. Although mindfulness of breathing is included in the taking up of the ten recollections, it was taken up again because it was suitable and beneficial for many of the bhikkhus assembled there. For indeed, the Blessed One taught that very meditation subject in this sutta. The perception of repulsiveness in food is similar to the meditation subject of foulness, but the four immaterial attainments are unsuitable for beginners; thus, their non-inclusion here should be understood.”

Vesāliṃ upanissāyāti vesālīnagaraṃ gocaragāmaṃ katvā. Muhuttenevāti satthari saddhamme ca gāravena upagatabhikkhūnaṃ vacanasamanantarameva uṭṭhahiṃsūti katvā vuttaṃ. Buddhakāle kira bhikkhū bhagavato sandesaṃ sirasā sampaṭicchituṃ ohitasotā viharanti.

“‘Dwelling near Vesālī’ (Vesāliṃ upanissāya) means having made the city of Vesālī their alms-resort village. ‘In a moment’ (muhutteneva)—this was said because the bhikkhus, who had approached with reverence for the Teacher and the true Dhamma, rose up immediately upon hearing his words. It is said that in the Buddha's time, bhikkhus dwelt with attentive ears, ready to receive the Blessed One’s message with their heads.”

Ānāpānapariggāhikāyāti assāsapassāse pariggaṇhanavasena pavattāya satiyā. Sampayutto samādhīti tāya sampayuttaaññamaññapaccayabhūtāya uppanno samādhi. Ānāpānassatiyaṃ vā samādhīti iminā upanissayapaccayasabhāvampi dasseti, ubhayatthāpi sahajātādīnaṃ sattannampi paccayānaṃ vasena paccayabhāvaṃ dasseti. “Yathāpaṭipannā me sāvakā cattāro satipaṭṭhāne bhāventī”tiādīsu uppādanavaḍḍhanaṭṭhena bhāvanāti vuccatīti tadubhayavasena atthaṃ dassento “bhāvitoti uppādito vaḍḍhito vā”ti āha. Tattha bhāvaṃ vijjamānataṃ ito gatoti bhāvito, uppādito paṭiladdhamattoti attho. Uppanno pana laddhāsevano bhāvito, paguṇabhāvaṃ āpādito vaḍḍhitoti attho. Bahulīkatoti bahulaṃ pavattito. Tena āvajjanādivasībhāvappattimāha. Yo hi vasībhāvamāpādito, so icchiticchitakkhaṇe samāpajjitabbato punappunaṃ pavattissati. Tena vuttaṃ “punappunaṃ kato”ti. Yathā “idheva, bhikkhave, samaṇo (ma. ni. 1.139; a. ni. 4.241), vivicceva kāmehī”ti (dī. ni. 1.226; ma. ni. 1.271; saṃ. ni. 2.152; a. ni. 4.123) ca evamādīsu paṭhamapade vutto eva-saddo dutiyādīsupi vuttoyeva hoti, evamidhāpīti āha “ubhayattha evasaddena niyamo veditabbo”ti. Ubhayattha niyamena laddhaguṇaṃ dassetuṃ “ayaṃ hī”tiādi vuttaṃ.

“‘By that which grasps the in-and-out-breaths’ (ānāpānapariggāhikāya) means by the mindfulness that proceeds by way of grasping the in-breaths and out-breaths. ‘Concentration associated with it’ (sampayutto samādhi) means the concentration that has arisen, being a condition of association and mutuality with that mindfulness. ‘Or concentration in mindfulness of breathing’ (ānāpānassatiyaṃ vā samādhi)—by this, he also shows the nature of the decisive support condition; in both cases, he shows the conditionality by way of the seven conditions, such as co-nascence, and so on. In passages like ‘How my disciples develop the four establishments of mindfulness,’ etc., ‘development’ (bhāvanā) is spoken of in the sense of producing and increasing. Showing the meaning in both these ways, he says, ‘“developed” (bhāvito) means produced or increased.’ Here, ‘developed’ (bhāvito) means ‘having gone from here to a state of existence’; the meaning is ‘produced,’ merely obtained. But what has arisen and has been cultivated is ‘developed’; the meaning is ‘brought to proficiency,’ ‘increased.’ ‘Made much of’ (bahulīkato) means frequently practiced. By this, he indicates the attainment of mastery over adverting, etc. For that which has been brought to mastery will be practiced again and again, since it can be entered upon at any desired moment. Hence, it is said, ‘done again and again.’ Just as in passages like ‘Here, bhikkhus, a monk…’ and ‘secluded indeed from sensual pleasures…,’ the word ‘eva’ stated in the first phrase is also stated in the second and subsequent phrases, so too here. Thus, he says, ‘in both cases, a restriction by the word “eva” should be understood.’ To show the quality obtained through the restriction in both cases, ‘For this…’ etc., was stated.”

Asubhakammaṭṭhānanti asubhārammaṇaṃ jhānamāha. Tañhi asubhesu yogakammabhāvato yogino sukhavisesānaṃ kāraṇabhāvato ca “asubhakammaṭṭhāna”nti vuccati. Kevalanti iminā ārammaṇaṃ nivatteti. Paṭivedhavasenāti [M 2.0521] jhānapaṭivedhavasena. Jhānañhi bhāvanāvisesena ijjhantaṃ attano visayaṃ paṭivijjhantameva pavattati yathāsabhāvato paṭivijjhiyati cāti paṭivedhoti vuccati. Oḷārikārammaṇattāti [V 2.0223] bībhacchārammaṇattā. Paṭikūlārammaṇattāti jigucchitabbārammaṇattā. Pariyāyenāti kāraṇena, lesantarena vā. Ārammaṇasantatāyāti anukkamena vicitabbataṃ pattārammaṇassa paramasukhumataṃ sandhāyāha. Santehi sannisinne ārammaṇe pavattamāno dhammo sayampi sannisinnova hoti. Tenāha “santo vūpasanto nibbuto”ti, nibbutasabbapariḷāhoti attho. Ārammaṇasantatāya tadārammaṇānaṃ dhammānaṃ santatā lokuttaradhammārammaṇāhi paccavekkhaṇāhi veditabbā.

“‘The meditation subject of foulness’ (asubhakammaṭṭhāna) refers to the jhāna with a foul object. For it is called ‘the meditation subject of foulness’ because it is the yogi's practice with regard to foul things and because it is a cause of special happiness for the yogi. ‘Merely’ (kevala)—by this, he excludes the object. ‘By way of penetration’ (paṭivedhavasena) means by way of the penetration of jhāna. For jhāna, being accomplished through a special development, proceeds by penetrating its own domain; and because it is penetrated according to its own nature, it is called ‘penetration’ (paṭivedha). ‘Because of its gross object’ (oḷārikārammaṇattā) means because of its repulsive object. ‘Because of its repulsive object’ (paṭikūlārammaṇattā) means because it is an object to be loathed. ‘By way of’ (pariyāyena) means by reason, or by another turn of phrase. ‘Because of the peacefulness of the object’ (ārammaṇasantatāya)—he says this referring to the extreme subtlety of the object that has attained a state to be investigated sequentially. A state that proceeds when the object is peaceful and settled is itself settled. Therefore, he says, ‘peaceful, calmed, extinguished,’ meaning with all fevers extinguished. The peacefulness of the object and the peacefulness of the states having that object are to be known through the reviews that have supramundane states as their object.”

Nāssa santapaṇītabhāvāvahaṃ kiñci secananti asecanako. Asecanakattā anāsittako, anāsittakattā eva abbokiṇṇo, asammisso parikammādinā. Tato eva pāṭiyekko visuṃyeveko. Āveṇiko asādhāraṇo. Sabbametaṃ sarasato eva santabhāvaṃ dassetuṃ vuttaṃ, parikammaṃ vā santabhāvanimittaṃ. Parikammanti ca kasiṇakaraṇādinimittuppādapariyosānaṃ, tādisaṃ ettha natthīti adhippāyo. Tadā hi kammaṭṭhānaṃ nirassādattā asantaṃ appaṇītaṃ siyā. Upacāre vā natthi ettha santatāti yojanā. Yathā upacārakkhaṇe nīvaraṇādivigamena aṅgapātubhāvena ca paresaṃ santatā hoti, na evamimassa. Ayaṃ pana ādisamannā…pe… paṇīto cāti yojanā. Kecīti uttaravihāravāsino. Anāsittakoti upasecanena anāsittako. Tenāha – “ojavanto”ti ojavantasadisoti attho. Madhuroti iṭṭho.
